<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-clifford-tensor-criterion**: Algebraicity of one exact standard even-Clifford Kuga–Satake tensor on a projective K3 surface implies rational HC and GHC in every degree of every self-power.
- **inv-quadratic-locus**: The tensor is realized by an algebraic cycle on every smooth projective complex K3 surface whose transcendental quadratic space embeds rationally and isometrically in the rank-eight quadratic space of signature (2,6).

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-outside-quadratic-locus**: Apply the unconditional Hodge conclusions to a K3 surface whose transcendental quadratic space has no rational isometric embedding into V_P. -> The cycle construction is only available on the quadratic locus; elsewhere only the conditional criterion holds.
- **anti-abstract-embedding**: Replace the prescribed Clifford tensor by an arbitrary nonzero Hodge embedding into abelian cohomology. -> The recovery argument needs the exact multiplication map, which an unspecified embedding does not provide.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The results assert existence of algebraic cycles in rational Chow groups via period domains, Hodge groups, uniruledness, Baire-category and proper-mapping arguments. No finite procedure with explicit steps computes a cycle, so no runnable reference implementation or oracle exists.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves existence of algebraic cycles in Chow groups of K3 powers; it prescribes no program, API or data format that a software project could implement. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Hodge classes on complex projective varieties have no dynamical or geometric-simulation counterpart in the paper; nothing in it is a model, rule set or physics procedure. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper supplies no tool-level procedure. Its theory sits above existing computer algebra machinery (lattice discriminant forms, Lie algebra splittings) and gives no input/output contract for one. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The constructions (limits over countable sets of parameters, Baire-category choices, existence of cycles via proper analytic parameter spaces) are non-constructive and give no finite procedure or complexity bound. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is introduced; the objects are Chow groups, Hodge structures and lattices, treated as abstract mathematical objects. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No numerical kernel, parallel computation or arithmetic pipeline appears in the paper; its content is purely about cycles on complex varieties. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Abelian varieties appear only as Kuga-Satake carriers of Hodge structures over the complex numbers; no group-based hardness assumption, finite field or protocol is studied. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no learning problem, data set or optimisation of a model; the representation theory used is classical invariant theory. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no objective, constraint system or feasibility problem; parameter spaces of cycles are used only to prove existence by dimension and countability arguments. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No communication, replication or consistency issue arises; the paper studies single complex projective varieties and their self-products. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result belongs to pure algebraic geometry and Hodge theory; its main value is as a theorem-level input to other cycle-theoretic work, and it offers no engineering contract of its own. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-quadratic-embedding** (hypothesis): The unconditional cycle construction needs a rational isometric embedding of the transcendental space, with its cup-product form, into V_P. For other K3 surfaces only the conditional criterion is available. Blocked use: ['Asserting HC and GHC on all self-powers for K3 surfaces whose transcendental space does not embed in V_P']
- **limit-exact-tensor** (hypothesis): The criterion requires the cycle to induce the exact standard even-Clifford tensor for one nonisotropic rational vector, one rational polarization and one full even-Clifford realization. Blocked use: ['Substituting an arbitrary nonzero Hodge embedding into abelian cohomology for the prescribed tensor']
- **limit-rational-coefficients** (formal_scope): All cohomology, cycle groups and conclusions use rational coefficients, with integral lattices taken modulo torsion. Blocked use: ['Deducing integral Hodge statements or torsion cycle classes']
- **limit-complex-projective** (hypothesis): The theorems concern smooth projective complex K3 surfaces; period domains and Hodge index positivity are used throughout. Blocked use: ['Applying the results to non-projective analytic K3 surfaces or to positive characteristic']
- **limit-countable-exceptions** (edge_case): The construction points are chosen outside countable exceptional sets after the pencil is fixed, so they are not given by an explicit list. Blocked use: ['Treating the construction point as an explicitly computable parameter']
- **limit-nonconstructive-images** (nonconstructive): Algebraicity at every period is obtained from countable unions of proper analytic images and a Baire-type argument, not from an explicit cycle. Blocked use: ['Reading the result as an algorithm that outputs the cycle', 'Extracting runtime or complexity bounds']
- **limit-picard-jump-ambient** (edge_case): At a Picard jump the fixed perpendicular of P is larger than the transcendental space, so the ambient tensor must be compressed to the actual transcendental space. Blocked use: ['Using the ambient tensor directly as the tensor of the transcendental space at a jump']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the rational Hodge and generalized Hodge conjectures in every cohomological degree of every self-power of a projective complex K3 surface whose transcendental quadratic space, with its cup-product form, admits a rational isometric embedding in $`\mathbb U_{\mathbb Q}^{\oplus2}\perp\langle-1\rangle^4`$. This includes every ample <i>P</i>-polarized K3 surface, including all Picard jumps, for $`P=\mathbb U\oplus D_8(-1)\oplus D_4(-1)`$. On this locus we construct algebraic correspondences inducing every prescribed standard even-Clifford Kuga–Satake tensor. More generally, for any projective complex K3 surface, algebraicity of one exact standard even-Clifford Kuga–Satake tensor implies both conjectures for every self-power.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

|---|---|---|---|---|

| [stmt-main-theorem](#stmt-main-theorem) | theorem | `main:theorem` | 2 | Let $S$ be a smooth projective complex K3 surface. |
| [stmt-main-lattice](#stmt-main-lattice) | lemma | `main:lattice` | 3 | There is a primitive embedding of $P$ in $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}=\mathbb U^{\oplus3}\oplus E_8(-1)^{\oplus2}$ with orthogonal complement $T_P=\mathbb U\oplus\mathbb U(2)\oplus D_4(-1)$. |
| [stmt-ext-conditional](#stmt-ext-conditional) | proposition | `ext:conditional` | 4 | Let $S$ be a projective K3 surface with transcendental space $(T,q)$ of rational dimension $t$, put $V=T(1)$, and let $W=C^+(T,q)=H^1(A,\mathbb Q)$ be an even-Clifford Kuga–Satake realization. |
| [stmt-ghc-lem-cycle-support](#stmt-ghc-lem-cycle-support) | lemma | `ghc:lem:cycle-support` | 5 | Let $X,Y$ be smooth projective varieties, with $\dim X=N$ and $\dim Y=D$, and let $C\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{N+p}(X\times Y)_\mathbb Q$, where $p\ge0$. |
| [stmt-ghc-prop-exterior-coniveau](#stmt-ghc-prop-exterior-coniveau) | proposition | `ghc:prop:exterior-coniveau` | 6 | Let $S$ satisfy the exact even-Clifford algebraicity hypothesis of Proposition 4, and put $t=\dim_\mathbb QT$. |

<a id="intro:section"></a>
### Introduction

The Hodge conjecture asks which cohomology classes on a smooth projective complex variety come from algebraic subvarieties. In its rational form it asserts surjectivity of $$\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}:\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^r(X)_{\mathbb Q}\longrightarrow
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hdg}}^r(X):=H^{2r}(X,\mathbb Q)\cap H^{r,r}(X)$$ for every $r$. The generalized Hodge conjecture asks the corresponding support question for whole Hodge substructures. Write $$N^pH^j(X,\mathbb Q)=
 \sum_{\substack{B\subset X\text{ closed}\\\operatorname{codim}_X B\ge p}}
 \ker\bigl(H^j(X,\mathbb Q)\longrightarrow H^j(X\setminus B,\mathbb Q)\bigr).$$ The generalized conjecture says that this equals the largest rational sub-Hodge structure whose types $(a,b)$ satisfy $a,b\ge p$. We use rational coefficients for both conjectures throughout, and abbreviate them as HC and GHC.

For a projective K3 surface $S$, let $$T_S=\mathop{\mathrm{NS}}(S)_{\mathbb Q}^{\perp}\subset H^2(S,\mathbb Q)$$ with its cup-product form $q$. Its extreme Hodge spaces are $T_S^{2,0}=H^{2,0}(S)$ and its conjugate, both one-dimensional. The Hodge index theorem gives signature $(2,20-\rho(S))$. This small Hodge level makes K3 surfaces a natural setting for the generalized conjecture, but their powers contain tensor Hodge classes and substructures that need not be generated by divisor classes alone.

Kuga and Satake attached abelian varieties to polarized K3 Hodge structures through Clifford algebras <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[KS](#ref-KS)], and Deligne developed the construction in families <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Deligne](#ref-Deligne)]. The distinguished correspondence is absolutely Hodge and motivated <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[DeligneMilne](#ref-DeligneMilne), Proposition 6.26(d) and Corollary 6.27], <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Andre](#ref-Andre), Section 7.1]. Algebraicity is the additional assertion needed here: we require a cycle inducing the prescribed Clifford map, not merely some nonzero Hodge embedding into abelian cohomology.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### One exact Kuga–Satake tensor

<a id="main:kappa"></a>
We specify the Kuga–Satake tensor used throughout the paper. For a quadratic space $(T,q)$ of K3 type we use the Clifford relation $v^2=q(v,v)$. Its full even algebra $W=C^+(T,q)$ carries the standard left Kuga–Satake Hodge structure. If $e_1,e_2$ are an oriented orthonormal basis of the positive period plane with $T^{2,0}=\mathbb C(e_1+ie_2)$, its complex structure is left multiplication by $J=e_1e_2$. Let $A$ be an abelian variety with a specified rational Hodge identification $H^1(A,\mathbb Q)=W$. Such a realization is unique up to the usual isogeny choices. For a nonisotropic $w\in T$ the standard map is $$\label{main:kappa}
 j_w:T(1)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W),\qquad j_w(v)(a)=vaw.$$ Here $\mathbb Q(1)$ has weight $-2$. The right factor need not be a Hodge vector: conjugation by left spin multiplication makes <a href="#main:kappa" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:kappa">[main:kappa]</a> a Hodge map at every period.

Let $E$ be a rational polarization of $W$ and set $E^\flat:W\to W^\vee(-1)$, $y\mapsto E(y,-)$. In the ordering $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W)=W\otimes W^\vee$, apply $(E^\flat)^{-1}$ to the second factor after twisting by $\mathbb Q(-1)$. This gives the specified tensor form <a id="main:iota"></a>$$\label{main:iota}
 \iota_{w,E}:T\longrightarrow W\otimes W
      \subset H^2(A\times A,\mathbb Q).$$ The adjective “standard” below refers to this actual map, not to an unspecified nonzero embedding into the cohomology of an abelian variety; see <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[VanGeemenKS](#ref-VanGeemenKS), Proposition 6.3] for the full even-Clifford construction.

<a id="stmt-ext-criterion"></a>
<div id="ext:criterion" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $S$ be a projective K3 surface. Suppose that, for one rational nonisotropic $w\in T_S$, one rational polarization, and one full even-Clifford realization, the tensor of $j_w(v)(a)=vaw$ is induced by a cycle in $\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S\times A^2)_{\mathbb Q}$. Then rational HC and rational GHC hold in every degree of every self-power of $S$.*

</div>

Section <a href="#ext:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:section">2</a> proves this criterion independently of the concrete K3 family constructed later.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The quadratic locus

Let $\mathbb U$ be the hyperbolic plane with Gram matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, and put $$P=\mathbb U\oplus D_8(-1)\oplus D_4(-1),\qquad
 V_P=(\mathbb U\oplus\mathbb U(2)\oplus D_4(-1))_{\mathbb Q}
     \simeq\mathbb U_{\mathbb Q}^{\oplus2}\perp\langle-1\rangle^4.$$ The form $V_P$ has dimension eight and signature $(2,6)$. Lemma <a href="#main:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:lattice">3</a> relates it to primitive copies of $P$ in the K3 lattice. An *ample $P$-polarization* on $S$ means a primitive embedding $P\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{NS}}(S)$ whose image contains an ample class. At a Picard jump its fixed perpendicular still has dimension eight, although it is then larger than $T_S$.

<a id="stmt-main-theorem"></a>
<div id="main:theorem" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *Let $S$ be a smooth projective complex K3 surface. Suppose that there is a rational isometric embedding $$(T_S,q)\hookrightarrow V_P.$$ For any full even-Clifford realization $H^1(A,\mathbb Q)=C^+(T_S,q)$, any rational nonisotropic $w\in T_S$, and any rational polarization $E$ of this weight-one Hodge structure, there is a cycle $$\Gamma_S\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S\times A\times A)_{\mathbb Q}$$ whose action on every $x\in T_S$ is exactly $\iota_{w,E}(x)$ in $H^2(A\times A,\mathbb Q)$. The same assertion holds on a transported isogenous model with its specified tensor.*

*Moreover, the rational Hodge conjecture and the rational generalized Hodge conjecture hold in every cohomological degree of every self-power $S^m$, for every integer $m\ge1$.*

</div>

In particular the theorem applies to every ample $P$-polarized K3 surface, including all Picard jumps. Its hypothesis concerns the actual transcendental quadratic space with its cup-product form. Standard motivic decompositions also extend these HC and GHC conclusions to all self-powers of Hilbert schemes of points and to the smooth projective moduli spaces specified in Corollary <a href="#transfer:moduli" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:moduli">40</a>.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Context and proof strategy

Geometric constructions for special K3 families include Paranjape’s curve-product and Prym construction for six-line double planes <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Paranjape](#ref-Paranjape), Sections 2–4], and Bolognesi–Laterveer’s treatment of order-three non-symplectic automorphisms whose fixed locus contains a curve <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BolognesiLaterveer](#ref-BolognesiLaterveer), Theorem 4.4 and Section 6]. Floccari and Fu explicitly distinguish an algebraic embedding from the prescribed Kuga–Satake map <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[FloccariFuOG6](#ref-FloccariFuOG6), Conjectures 2.4 and 2.6]; they prove algebraicity of the latter for the K3 surfaces associated with projective singular OG6-varieties <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[FloccariFuOG6](#ref-FloccariFuOG6), Corollary 4.7].

For the lattice $P$ used here, Poon computes the Clifford algebra and constructs a modular map to quaternionic abelian eightfolds <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Poon](#ref-Poon), Theorem 3.1.4 and Section 4]. Van Geemen and Verra study related quaternionic Prym eightfolds and algebraic quaternionic classes <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[VanGeemenVerra](#ref-VanGeemenVerra), Sections 3–4]. These modular and quaternionic-class constructions do not by themselves give the prescribed K3-to-abelian correspondence. The Clifford and half-spin descriptions are classical <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[VanGeemenKS](#ref-VanGeemenKS), Proposition 6.3 and Example 6.6]; our recovery argument keeps the full even module and both tensor orientations.

For powers of a K3 surface, Zarhin’s description of its Hodge group reduces tensor Hodge classes to classical invariant theory <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Zarhin1983](#ref-Zarhin1983)]. When the endomorphism field of the transcendental Hodge structure is CM, ordinary HC is already known for every self-power <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[VarescoPowers](#ref-VarescoPowers), Corollary 2.3]. The totally real case also requires relative determinant tensors; Varesco identifies these generators and explains why classes from the square alone need not suffice <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[VarescoPowers](#ref-VarescoPowers), Theorem 2.16]. For specified four-dimensional families with generic Picard number $16$, he also derives ordinary HC for all powers from algebraicity of the Kuga–Satake correspondences <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[VarescoPowers](#ref-VarescoPowers), Theorem 0.2].

*From the exact tensor to HC and GHC.* Section <a href="#ext:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:section">2</a> proves the general criterion from its one exact standard tensor. Alternated compositions of $j_w$ inject $(\bigwedge^kT_S)(k)$ into the same endomorphism algebra: Clifford PBW and invertibility of the right factor prove injectivity. Algebraic adjoints and a polynomial inverse give return correspondences from $H^2(A^2)$ to $\bigwedge^kT_S$ with target support of codimension $k-1$. The returns first algebraize relative volume tensors in the totally real case; together with the endomorphism-field actions and classical invariant theory, these give ordinary HC. A separate highest-weight argument bounds the number of exterior factors needed to carry a rational Hodge substructure in terms of its Hodge coniveau. Their returns, followed by an HC-realized map, then supply the required geometric support. This proves GHC for whole substructures, not just for Hodge classes.

*A curve source and its K3 comparison.* Sections <a href="#geom:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section">3</a> and <a href="#domination:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="domination:section">4</a> construct double covers $X$ of abelian surfaces. Their resolved inversion quotients $Y$ carry a rank-eight variation $U$ with quadratic form $V_P$ and an open period image. Resolved pullback identifies it with $U_X\subset H^2(X)$, multiplying the cup form by two. On a nonempty geometric open, a Gauss-pencil construction gives a product of curves dominating $X$; when $U_X$ has no nonzero rational $(1,1)$-class, it supplies an algebraic injection $$U_X\hookrightarrow H^1(C_1,\mathbb Q)\otimes H^1(C_2,\mathbb Q).$$ Section <a href="#pencil:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:section">5</a> realizes $U$, with its $Y$-form, in an auxiliary K3 family $S^0$ over the original pencil line. The larger integral anti-invariant lattice is retained to control vanishing roots and descent. The remaining task is to make the specified comparison $U\to U_X$ algebraic; the curve domination alone does not do so.

Section <a href="#comp:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:section">6</a> accomplishes this by two quadratic substitutions. The first, $t=s+u^2$, gives uniruled threefold models on both sides, whose third cohomology has algebraic splittings through curve cohomology with a Tate twist. The required degree-three Hodge comparison is therefore algebraic. The second, $s=b+v^2$, produces a conic with two components over $t=b$. Their difference defines a cylinder that returns this comparison to the surface fibers. Its class lies in the Leray term controlled by degree-three cohomology. Fixing the first models and correspondence before $b$ varies makes the discarded boundary’s Hodge structures fixed, so nonconstant simple periods eliminate the boundary error. Composition with the curve injection now gives an algebraic curve source for $T_{S_b^0}=U_b$.

*The prescribed tensor and all periods.* Section <a href="#final:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:section">7</a> upgrades a curve injection at a full special-orthogonal period to every rational Hodge map into the full even-Clifford tensor square. Among the nontrivial constituents, weight-one effectivity leaves the two half-spin representations; a multiplicity-space calculation and rational Hodge-Hom descent recover the prescribed tensor. The auxiliary K3 need not be $P$-polarized. In Section <a href="#specialization:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="specialization:section">8</a>, a rational change of period coordinates puts the construction into any local ample-$P$ target ball without altering its algebraic pencils. Successive choices give a target seed with $\mathop{\mathrm{NS}}=P$; Buskin’s theorem transports the auxiliary curve source there, and the half-spin argument realizes the fixed target tensor. Proper cycle parameters then extend that same action on the whole rank-eight perpendicular of $P$, in the full degree-two target, throughout the ball. Only afterward does Section <a href="#transfer:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:section">9</a> compress to the actual $T_S$ at a Picard jump. A separate rational period and Buskin transfer reaches the theorem’s full rational quadratic-embedding locus.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Conventions and the lattice

Cohomology means rational Betti cohomology, except when an integral lattice is specified; integral cohomology is taken modulo torsion. For smooth projective varieties $M,N$, a cycle $Z\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^r(M\times N)_{\mathbb Q}$ acts by $$Z_*x=(p_N)_*(\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Z)\smile p_M^*x),\qquad
 Z_*:H^k(M)\longrightarrow H^{k+2r-2\dim M}(N).$$ Degree-changing maps carry the corresponding Tate twist as Hodge maps. We use only cohomological projectors represented by cycles, not idempotence modulo rational equivalence. The geometric $U$ below is distinct from the hyperbolic plane $\mathbb U$; the auxiliary K3 is $S^0$, the double-cover surface is $X$, and the final K3 is $S$.

<a id="stmt-main-lattice"></a>
<div id="main:lattice" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *There is a primitive embedding of $P$ in $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}=\mathbb U^{\oplus3}\oplus E_8(-1)^{\oplus2}$ with orthogonal complement $T_P=\mathbb U\oplus\mathbb U(2)\oplus D_4(-1)$. For every primitive embedding of $P$ in the K3 lattice, the rational orthogonal complement is isometric to $V_P$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-main-lattice"><summary>Proof of stmt-main-lattice</summary>

*Proof.* For an even lattice $L$, write $A_L=L^*/L$ and $q_L:A_L\to\mathbb Q/2\mathbb Z$ for its discriminant form. The forms of $D_8(-1)$ and $D_4(-1)$ are respectively the split and anisotropic forms $u,v$ on $(\mathbb Z/2)^2$. Their values are integral modulo $2\mathbb Z$, so each is isomorphic to its negative. Also $q_{\mathbb U(2)}=u$. Hence $$(A_P,q_P)\simeq u\oplus v\simeq(A_{T_P},-q_{T_P}).$$ An anti-isometry glues $P\oplus T_P$ to an even unimodular lattice of signature $(3,19)$, hence to $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$, with both summands primitive. This gives the asserted embedding. For another embedding, Witt’s extension theorem <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[PetersSterk](#ref-PetersSterk), Corollary 7.2.8] applied to the two copies of the nondegenerate rational space $P_{\mathbb Q}$ gives an isometry of $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3},\mathbb Q}$ carrying one copy to the other. It carries their rational perpendiculars to each other. Finally rescaling one isotropic basis vector identifies $\mathbb U(2)_{\mathbb Q}$ with $\mathbb U_{\mathbb Q}$, and the coordinate description of $D_4$ gives $D_4(-1)_{\mathbb Q}\simeq\langle-1\rangle^4$. ◻

</details>

The lattice $P$ occurs as an exact Néron–Severi lattice. Choose a period in the domain of $T_P$ outside the countably many hyperplanes defined by integral vectors not in $P$. Period surjectivity gives a K3 surface with Néron–Severi group exactly $P$; it is projective because $P$ contains a positive class. Its ample cone contains a primitive integral class of $P$. These are standard consequences of the K3 period and chamber theorems <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Huybrechts](#ref-Huybrechts)].

<a id="ext:section"></a>
### Hodge classes and geometric supports from one exact tensor

We prove Theorem <a href="#ext:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:criterion">1</a>. The argument is independent of the concrete K3 family constructed later; its only Clifford premise is one algebraic realization of the exact standard tensor in that theorem.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Algebraic summands and coefficient extensions

<a id="ext:k3-projectors"></a>
Let $S$ be a projective K3 surface, and write $$N=\mathop{\mathrm{NS}}(S)_{\mathbb Q},\qquad T=N^\perp\subset H^2(S,\mathbb Q),
 \qquad V=T(1).$$ We denote the intersection form on $T$, and its weight-zero version on $V$, by $q$. Choose a point $o\in S$, a divisor basis $D_1,\ldots,D_\rho$ of $N$, and let $(b^{ij})$ be the inverse of $(D_i\cdot D_j)$. The following cycles in $\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S\times S)_{\mathbb Q}$ act as the indicated cohomological projectors: $$\label{ext:k3-projectors}
 \pi_0=[o]\times S,\quad \pi_4=S\times[o],\quad
 \pi_N=\sum_{i,j}b^{ij}D_i\times D_j,\quad
 \pi_T=\Delta_S-\pi_0-\pi_4-\pi_N.$$ The form on $N$ is nondegenerate by the Hodge index theorem. In particular, these projectors and the pairing and inverse pairing on $T$ are algebraic. The latter are given by intersection and the transcendental component of the diagonal. Throughout this section, “projector” means a projector on cohomology represented by a cycle; no assertion of idempotence modulo rational equivalence is needed.

<a id="ext:alternator"></a>
The product of $k$ copies of $\pi_T$, followed by the alternating projector $$\label{ext:alternator}
 \operatorname{Alt}_k=\frac{1}{k!}
       \sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak S_k}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\sigma,$$ is an algebraic projector $\pi_{\wedge,k}$ on $S^k$ with image $\bigwedge^k T\subset H^{2k}(S^k,\mathbb Q)$. Here permutations act by their graphs, with the convention giving the ordinary permutation of the tensor factors. All these factors have even degree. The projector is self-adjoint for the middle intersection pairing: $\pi_T$ is self-transpose, and transposition replaces a permutation by its inverse. The induced pairing on $\bigwedge^kT$ is a nonzero rational multiple of the exterior pairing associated with $q$, hence is nondegenerate.

We will also use algebraic degree-one projectors on abelian varieties. For example, if $A$ has dimension $g$ and $a\geq2$ is an integer, the polynomial $$\pi_1^A=\prod_{\substack{0\leq j\leq2g\\j\ne1}}
       \frac{[a]^*-a^j\mathop{\mathrm{id}}}{a-a^j}$$ in the transpose graph of multiplication by $a$ acts as the projector onto $H^1(A,\mathbb Q)$.

<a id="ext:descent"></a>
An algebraic class with coefficients in a field $K\supset\mathbb Q$ will mean an element of the scalar extension of the rational image of the cycle-class map. This convention makes rational descent transparent. If $A_{\mathbb Q}\subset H_{\mathbb Q}$ is that rational image in a fixed cohomology group, then $$\label{ext:descent}
 H_{\mathbb Q}\cap(A_{\mathbb Q}\otimes_{\mathbb Q}K)=A_{\mathbb Q}
 \quad\text{inside }H_{\mathbb Q}\otimes_{\mathbb Q}K.$$ Indeed tensoring $H_{\mathbb Q}/A_{\mathbb Q}$ with a field is faithful. Products, permutations and contractions by algebraic pairings preserve this notion of algebraicity. All varieties remain over $\mathbb C$; only the coefficients are extended.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Algebraic returns from exterior powers

<a id="stmt-ext-conditional"></a>
<div id="ext:conditional" class="proposition">

<a id="ext:exact-kappa"></a>
**Proposition 4**. *Let $S$ be a projective K3 surface with transcendental space $(T,q)$ of rational dimension $t$, put $V=T(1)$, and let $W=C^+(T,q)=H^1(A,\mathbb Q)$ be an even-Clifford Kuga–Satake realization. Suppose that for one rational nonisotropic $w\in T$ the tensor form of $$\label{ext:exact-kappa}
 j_w:V\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W),\qquad
 j_w(v)(a)=vaw,$$ is induced by a cycle in $\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S\times A^2)_{\mathbb Q}$, using a rational polarization of $W$. Then, for every $1\leq k\leq t$, there is an injective algebraic Hodge map $$F_k:\bigwedge^kV\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W)$$ with an algebraic left inverse on cohomology. More precisely, put $Z=A^2$ and $n=\dim Z$. There are correspondences $$\Gamma_k\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{k+1}(S^k\times Z)_\mathbb Q,\qquad
 R_k\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{n+k-1}(Z\times S^k)_\mathbb Q.$$ Viewing $\bigwedge^kV$ in $H^{2k}(S^k,\mathbb Q)(k)$ and identifying $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W)\simeq(H^1(A,\mathbb Q)\otimes H^1(A,\mathbb Q))(1)\subset H^2(Z,\mathbb Q)(1)$ by a rational polarization, their weight-zero actions satisfy $(\Gamma_k)_*|_{\wedge^kV}=F_k$ and $(R_k)_*F_k=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$. Every rational Hodge class in $\bigwedge^kV$ is the class of a rational algebraic cycle of codimension $k$ on $S^k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ext-conditional"><summary>Proof of stmt-ext-conditional</summary>

*Proof.* Choose an ample divisor class $\ell$ on $A$, and use the polarization $$e(x,y)=\int_Ax\cup y\cup\ell^{g-1},\qquad g=\dim A.$$ Changing the rational polarization in the hypothesis preserves algebraicity: the change on the identified dual factor is a rational Hodge automorphism of $H^1(A,\mathbb Q)$, and hence comes from an abelian homomorphism with rational coefficients. The elementary argument for this fact is given in the proof of Lemma <a href="#transfer:compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:compression">36</a>. Here $n=2g$; put $$B_W=H^1(A,\mathbb Q)\otimes H^1(A,\mathbb Q)\subset H^2(Z,\mathbb Q).$$ The polarization identifies $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W)$ with $B_W(1)$.

<a id="ext:clifford-evaluation"></a>
*The Clifford calculation.* Define $F_k$ by alternating the composition of $k$ copies of $j_w$. Associativity gives $$j_w(v_1)\cdots j_w(v_k)(a)
       =v_1\cdots v_k\,a\,w^k.$$ Thus, if $c_k:\bigwedge^kT\to C(T,q)$ is the antisymmetrized Clifford multiplication, then $$\label{ext:clifford-evaluation}
 F_k(\alpha)(1)=c_k(\alpha)w^k.$$ The Clifford PBW decomposition identifies $c_k$ with the degree-$k$ exterior summand, so it is injective. Moreover $w^{-1}=w/q(w,w)$, and right multiplication by $w^k$ is invertible. Equation <a href="#ext:clifford-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:clifford-evaluation">[ext:clifford-evaluation]</a> proves injectivity in every degree under consideration. For odd $k$, the left and right Clifford multiplications are both odd and their composite still preserves $C^+(T,q)$.

<a id="ext:forward-twist"></a>
The map $j_w$ is a Hodge morphism for the left spin action; tensor products, composition and alternation preserve this property. Consequently $$\label{ext:forward-twist}
 F_k:\bigwedge^kT\longrightarrow B_W(1-k)$$ is the corresponding untwisted Hodge morphism. In particular, the construction uses the exact multiplication in <a href="#ext:exact-kappa" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:exact-kappa">[ext:exact-kappa]</a>; an unspecified algebraic copy of $T$ in $H^2(A^2,\mathbb Q)$ would not imply <a href="#ext:clifford-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:clifford-evaluation">[ext:clifford-evaluation]</a>.

*Algebraic realization and degree.* Represent the tensor form of $j_w$ by the cycle in the hypothesis, projected by $\pi_T$ and $\pi_1^A\boxtimes\pi_1^A$. Take its $k$-fold exterior product and project the input by $\pi_{\wedge,k}$. Composition in $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W)$ contracts $k-1$ pairs of $W$-slots by $e$, in the appropriate tensor order. Each such contraction is algebraic: the cycle $$(\Delta_A)_*(\ell^{g-1})\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{2g-1}(A^2)_{\mathbb Q},$$ regarded as a correspondence to a point, realizes $W\otimes W\to\mathbb Q(-1)$. It lowers cohomological degree by two. The two uncontracted slots give $Z$; any signs arising from permuting degree-one cohomology factors are included in the cycle coefficients. Hence the resulting correspondence, denoted by $\Gamma_k$, has <a id="ext:forward-cycle"></a>$$\label{ext:forward-cycle}
 \Gamma_k\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{k+1}(S^k\times Z)_{\mathbb Q},\qquad
 (\Gamma_k)_*|_{\wedge^kT}=F_k.$$ Indeed its degree shift is $2-2k$, which agrees with $2((k+1)-\dim S^k)$. Its output twist is $1-k$, as in <a href="#ext:forward-twist" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:forward-twist">[ext:forward-twist]</a>.

*An algebraic left inverse.* Let $L=\operatorname{pr}_1^*\ell+\operatorname{pr}_2^*\ell$ on $Z$. The summand $B_W$ is primitive in degree two for $L$. To see this, expand $L^{n-1}$: every term acting on $B_W$ has odd degree in each abelian factor, whereas the only possible top bidegree on $A^2$ is $(2g,2g)$. Thus $L^{n-1}B_W=0$. The Hodge–Riemann bilinear relations imply that $$Q_Z(x,y)=\int_Zx\cup y\cup L^{n-2}$$ is a polarization on $B_W$, up to a fixed sign. Retwisting <a href="#ext:forward-twist" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:forward-twist">[ext:forward-twist]</a> gives a morphism of weight-two Hodge structures $(\bigwedge^kT)(k-1)\to B_W$. Thus its image is a Hodge substructure of $B_W$. The restriction of $Q_Z$ to this image is nondegenerate: the associated definite Hermitian form restricts to a definite form there.

Multiplication by $L^{n-2}$, followed by the transpose of $\Gamma_k$ and by $\pi_{\wedge,k}$, defines an algebraic map <a id="ext:adjoint"></a>$$\label{ext:adjoint}
 G_k:H^2(Z,\mathbb Q)\longrightarrow\bigwedge^kT,\qquad
 G_k\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{n+k-1}(Z\times S^k)_{\mathbb Q}.$$ Here the cohomological degrees are $$H^2(Z)\xrightarrow{\,L^{n-2}\,}H^{2n-2}(Z)
       \xrightarrow{\,{}^t\Gamma_k\,}H^{2k}(S^k).$$ If $Q_k$ denotes middle intersection on $\bigwedge^kT$, self-adjointness of $\pi_{\wedge,k}$ gives <a id="ext:adjoint-identity"></a>$$\label{ext:adjoint-identity}
 Q_k(G_kF_kx,y)=Q_Z(F_kx,F_ky).$$ The pairing on the right is nondegenerate, and $Q_k$ is nondegenerate by the discussion of <a href="#ext:alternator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:alternator">[ext:alternator]</a>. Therefore $D_k=G_kF_k$ is invertible on $\bigwedge^kT$.

<a id="ext:left-inverse"></a>
Its characteristic polynomial has nonzero constant term, so Cayley–Hamilton supplies a polynomial $P_k\in\mathbb Q[X]$ such that $P_k(D_k)D_k=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ on that summand. Composition of cycles realizes $P_k(D_k)$ in codimension $2k$ on $S^k\times S^k$, using $\pi_{\wedge,k}$ as the identity of the summand. The correspondence $$\label{ext:left-inverse}
 R_k=P_k(D_k)G_k\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{n+k-1}(Z\times S^k)_{\mathbb Q}$$ satisfies $R_kF_k=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$; in weight-zero notation it maps $H^2(Z,\mathbb Q)(1)$ to $(\bigwedge^kT)(k)$. This construction uses multiplication by an ample class, transposition and a polynomial inverse on a finite-dimensional summand. It requires no algebraic inverse to a Lefschetz operator. The composite $\Gamma_k\circ R_k$, of codimension $n$ on $Z\times Z$, is the corresponding cohomological projector onto $F_k(\bigwedge^kT)\subset H^2(Z,\mathbb Q)$.

Finally, let $\alpha\in(\bigwedge^kV)^{0,0}_{\mathbb Q}$. Its image is a rational $(1,1)$ class in $B_W$, so Lefschetz’s $(1,1)$ theorem gives a divisor class on $Z$ representing it. Applying $R_k$ yields $\alpha$. The resulting cycle has codimension $1+(n+k-1)-n=k$ on $S^k$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="ghc:subsec:exterior-coniveau"></a>
#### Geometric supports for exterior powers

The same return that algebraizes exterior Hodge classes also places each whole exterior power on a fixed geometric support. We use geometric coniveau $N^p$ as defined in the introduction and set $N^pH^j(X,\mathbb Q)=H^j(X,\mathbb Q)$ for $p\le0$. Recall that a sub-Hodge structure has Hodge coniveau at least $p$ when all its types $(a,b)$ satisfy $a,b\ge p$.

<a id="stmt-ghc-lem-cycle-support"></a>
<div id="ghc:lem:cycle-support" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Support of a correspondence). *Let $X,Y$ be smooth projective varieties, with $\dim X=N$ and $\dim Y=D$, and let $C\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{N+p}(X\times Y)_\mathbb Q$, where $p\ge0$. There is a fixed closed subset $B\subset Y$ of codimension at least $p$ supporting the image of $C_*$ on cohomology. In particular, $$C_*H^j(X,\mathbb Q)\subset N^pH^{j+2p}(Y,\mathbb Q).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ghc-lem-cycle-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-ghc-lem-cycle-support</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a finite cycle representative for $C$. Each component has dimension $D-p$, so the union $B$ of its proper images in $Y$ has dimension at most $D-p$. The restriction of the correspondence to $X\times(Y\setminus B)$ is zero. Compatibility of its action with restriction proves the assertion, including for rational linear combinations of components. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ghc-prop-exterior-coniveau"></a>
<div id="ghc:prop:exterior-coniveau" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Exterior-power coniveau). *Let $S$ satisfy the exact even-Clifford algebraicity hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#ext:conditional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:conditional">4</a>, and put $t=\dim_\mathbb QT$. For every $1\le k\le t$, $$\bigwedge^kT\subset N^{k-1}H^{2k}(S^k,\mathbb Q).$$ Moreover every rational sub-Hodge structure of this exterior power satisfies the generalized Hodge conjecture in its ambient $H^{2k}(S^k,\mathbb Q)$. In particular, the top exterior line is supported in codimension $t$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ghc-prop-exterior-coniveau"><summary>Proof of stmt-ghc-prop-exterior-coniveau</summary>

*Proof.* Retain $Z=A^2$ and $n=\dim Z$ from Proposition <a href="#ext:conditional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:conditional">4</a>. Equation <a href="#ext:left-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:left-inverse">[ext:left-inverse]</a> gives $$R_k\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{n+k-1}(Z\times S^k)_\mathbb Q,
 \qquad R_kF_k=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\quad\text{on }\bigwedge^kT.$$ The support of this cycle has dimension $n+2k-(n+k-1)=k+1$. Lemma <a href="#ghc:lem:cycle-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:lem:cycle-support">5</a> therefore puts its entire image from $H^2(Z,\mathbb Q)$ in coniveau $k-1$, and that image contains $\bigwedge^kT$.

The only possible types of $\bigwedge^kT$ are $(k+1,k-1)$, $(k,k)$ and $(k-1,k+1)$, since the extreme Hodge spaces of $T$ are one-dimensional. Thus a sub-Hodge structure requiring more than coniveau $k-1$ is necessarily of type $(k,k)$. Every rational vector in such a substructure is algebraic by Proposition <a href="#ext:conditional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:conditional">4</a>. Choosing a finite rational basis and taking the union of the supports of its codimension-$k$ cycles proves coniveau $k$. This also applies to $\bigwedge^tT$, whose only type is $(t,t)$. These are all possible coniveau requirements. ◻

</details>

The exterior returns now have both properties needed for the criterion. We first use their algebraicity on Hodge classes to prove ordinary HC for arbitrary powers of $S$. Then Section <a href="#ghc:subsec:k3-ghc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:subsec:k3-ghc">2.6</a> combines that result with the support property to treat arbitrary rational Hodge substructures.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The endomorphism field at an individual surface

The Hodge structure $T$ is simple. Indeed, semisimplicity of polarizable Hodge structures splits any sub-Hodge structure, and at most one summand can contain its one-dimensional $(2,0)$ part. Any other summand would be of type $(1,1)$, contrary to the definition of $T$ and Lefschetz’s $(1,1)$ theorem. Thus $E=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{Hdg}}(T)$ is a division algebra; its faithful action on $H^{2,0}(S)$ embeds it into $\mathbb C$, so it is a number field.

<a id="ext:real-hodge-group"></a>
<a id="ext:cm-hodge-group"></a>
We use Zarhin’s Hodge-group theorem <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Zarhin1983](#ref-Zarhin1983)] in the precise forms restated in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[VanGeemenReal](#ref-VanGeemenReal), Section 2.1 and Theorem 2.8] for the field, adjoint and totally real group, and in <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[VarescoPowers](#ref-VarescoPowers), Section 2.1] for the CM group. The field $E$ is either totally real or CM. The involution induced by the $q$-adjoint is respectively the identity or CM conjugation. If $E$ is totally real, there is a nondegenerate symmetric $E$-bilinear form $\phi$ on $T$ whose trace is $q$, and $$\label{ext:real-hodge-group}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(V)=\operatorname{Res}_{E/\mathbb Q}\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(T,\phi).$$ If $E$ is CM with real subfield $E^+$, the analogous form is Hermitian and $$\label{ext:cm-hodge-group}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(V)=\operatorname{Res}_{E^+/\mathbb Q}\operatorname{U}(T,\phi).$$ Here $\mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(V)$ is the connected rational group generated by the Hodge circle. Its invariant tensors are precisely the rational Hodge tensors of the weight-zero structure $V$.

<a id="ext:eigenspaces"></a>
Put $d=\dim_E T$, and choose a finite Galois field $K/\mathbb Q$ containing the images of all embeddings of $E$. Write $$\label{ext:eigenspaces}
 V_K=\bigoplus_{\eta:E\hookrightarrow K}V_\eta.$$ In the real case, the summands are mutually orthogonal and nondegenerate for $q$, and <a href="#ext:real-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:real-hodge-group">[ext:real-hodge-group]</a> becomes the product of their full special orthogonal groups after extending scalars to an algebraic closure. In the CM case, $q$ pairs $V_\eta$ perfectly with $V_{\bar\eta}$ and pairs it trivially with all other summands. Equation <a href="#ext:cm-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:cm-hodge-group">[ext:cm-hodge-group]</a> becomes a product of full general linear groups, one per conjugate pair, acting on $V_\eta$ and $V_{\bar\eta}$ as standard and dual representations.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The ordinary Hodge assertion

We first obtain the invariant tensors needed for ordinary HC. The following constructions are used only inside this proof of Theorem <a href="#ext:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:criterion">1</a>.

*Volume tensors and real multiplication.* Suppose first that $E$ is totally real. Each line $$\bigwedge^dV_\eta\subset(\bigwedge^dV)_K$$ is fixed by the group in <a href="#ext:real-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:real-hodge-group">[ext:real-hodge-group]</a>. Formation of invariants of a rational algebraic group commutes with extension of scalars. Hence these lines belong to the scalar extension of the rational Hodge-class space of $\bigwedge^dV$. Proposition <a href="#ext:conditional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:conditional">4</a> proves their algebraicity. These relative volume tensors are the additional special-orthogonal generators identified in <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[VarescoPowers](#ref-VarescoPowers), Theorem 2.16].

Choose a nonzero volume tensor $\alpha_\eta$ on $V_\eta$. Contracting $\alpha_\eta\otimes\alpha_\eta$ by $q$ in $d-1$ pairs of slots gives <a id="ext:volume-contraction"></a>$$\label{ext:volume-contraction}
 c_\eta q_\eta^{-1}\in V_\eta\otimes V_\eta,
       \qquad c_\eta\in K^*,$$ where $q_\eta$ is the restricted form. For example, in an orthogonal basis $u_1,\ldots,u_d$ with $q(u_i,u_i)=a_i\ne0$, the remaining tensor is a nonzero scalar multiple of $\sum_i a_i^{-1}u_i\otimes u_i$; its scalar contains the nonzero product $\prod_i a_i$. This verifies in particular that the contraction cannot vanish.

These contractions are algebraic. They are obtained by intersecting along the relevant diagonals and pushing forward on powers of $S$. Two codimension-$d$ tensors initially give codimension $2d$ on $S^{2d}$; contracting $d-1$ pairs of K3 slots leaves codimension $2d-2(d-1)=2$ on $S^2$. Pairing $q_\eta^{-1}$ with $q$ in one slot gives the orthogonal projector $p_\eta$ onto $V_\eta$ in $V_K$, so that projector is algebraic as well. For $a\in E$, its action is <a id="ext:e-action"></a>$$\label{ext:e-action}
 a=\sum_\eta\eta(a)p_\eta.$$ This action is rational; <a href="#ext:descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:descent">[ext:descent]</a>, applied to its cohomological correspondence class on $S^2$, proves rational algebraicity.

*CM endomorphisms and pairings.* Now suppose $E$ is CM. Its norm-one elements span $E$ over $\mathbb Q$. Here is an elementary verification. Their rational span $F$ is a unital subalgebra of the finite field extension $E$, closed under multiplication, and is therefore a field. If $\bar s=-s$, then $$u=\frac{1+s}{1-s},\qquad u\bar u=1,\qquad
 s=\frac{u-1}{u+1}\in F.$$ The denominators are nonzero, and $u+1=2/(1-s)$. Fixing a nonzero such $s$, every $a\in E^+$ satisfies $as\in F$, hence $a=(as)/s\in F$. Thus $F=E$.

Each norm-one element is a Hodge isometry of $T$. Extending it by the identity on $N$ gives a rational Hodge isometry of $H^2(S,\mathbb Q)$, which is algebraic by <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Buskin](#ref-Buskin), Theorem 1.1]. Rational linear combinations and projection by $\pi_T$ give every endomorphism in $E$. Choose a primitive element $b$ of $E/\mathbb Q$. The formulas $$p_\eta=\prod_{\tau\ne\eta}
       \frac{b-\tau(b)\mathop{\mathrm{id}}}{\eta(b)-\tau(b)}$$ then realize the individual eigenprojectors with coefficients in $K$. Applying $p_\eta\otimes p_{\bar\eta}$ to the inverse metric gives the separate standard–dual pairing tensors. This argument requires that the *endomorphism field* be CM; it does not require that $T$ be one-dimensional over that field.

*Invariant generation and Künneth assembly.* First work over an algebraic closure of $K$. The first fundamental theorem for a special orthogonal group on a standard space with nondegenerate symmetric form says that its invariant tensors are generated by the metric and a volume tensor. The first fundamental theorem for a general linear group on copies of its standard space and dual says that invariant tensors are generated by the standard–dual pairings. In the multilinear formulation the number of standard and dual slots must agree, and complete pair contractions span the invariant space. These statements apply to arbitrary numbers of slots; there is no stable range restriction on the spanning assertion <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[KraftProcesi](#ref-KraftProcesi), Sections 4.2 and 10.2]. A tensor formulation of the orthogonal statement is also given in <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[LehrerZhangSO](#ref-LehrerZhangSO), Theorem 2.1, Remark 2.2 and Corollary 4.2]: the top exterior tensor is harmonic and has nonzero norm, and adjoining it to the ordinary orthogonal contractions gives all special orthogonal invariants.

For each tensor power of $V$, use the algebraic projectors of the preceding construction to decompose every slot according to <a href="#ext:eigenspaces" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eigenspaces">[ext:eigenspaces]</a>. Invariants of the product of the independent groups in <a href="#ext:real-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:real-hodge-group">[ext:real-hodge-group]</a> or <a href="#ext:cm-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:cm-hodge-group">[ext:cm-hodge-group]</a> are then obtained factor by factor. In the real case all metric tensors and each relative volume tensor are algebraic by the preceding construction. In the CM case the full groups are general linear, so the algebraic pairings already suffice. Products, permutations and the algebraic duality on $T$ therefore realize every invariant tensor. Scalar extension of invariant spaces and <a href="#ext:descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:descent">[ext:descent]</a> show that every rational Hodge tensor in $V^{\otimes r}$ is algebraic over $\mathbb Q$, as a codimension-$r$ class on $S^r$.

Finally apply the algebraic projectors <a href="#ext:k3-projectors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:k3-projectors">[ext:k3-projectors]</a> to every factor of $S^m$. The only non-Tate summand of $H^*(S,\mathbb Q)$ is $T$ in degree two. Every Hodge class in a Künneth component is therefore a sum of Hodge tensors in $T^{\otimes r}$ tensored with point, divisor and unit classes. If a component has $r$ transcendental slots, $a$ divisor slots and $b$ top-degree slots, then its degree is $2r+2a+4b$ and the cycles constructed above have codimension $r+a+2b$. They thus give all classes in the required codimension on $S^m$.

This proves the ordinary Hodge assertion of Theorem <a href="#ext:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:criterion">1</a>. The only Clifford input in this deduction was the single map in its hypothesis, through Proposition <a href="#ext:conditional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:conditional">4</a>.

<a id="ghc:subsec:k3-ghc"></a>
#### Generalized Hodge conjecture for K3 powers

<span id="ghc:subsec:orthogonal-ghc" label="ghc:subsec:orthogonal-ghc"></span>

To pass from exterior powers to arbitrary Hodge substructures, we combine their return maps with ordinary HC on products of powers. The latter algebraizes maps between actual rational summands, so no rational splitting of a complex middle-exterior representation is required. The Hodge level of a nonzero pure Hodge structure is $\max\{|a-b|:H^{a,b}\ne0\}$.

Let $H\subset H^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q)$ be an actual simple sub-Hodge structure of Hodge coniveau at least $p$, with $0\le p\le d$. Then $H(d)$ has Hodge level at most $2(d-p)$. We will realize each nontrivial $H(d)$ as a quotient of a tensor product of $c\le d-p$ exterior powers of $V=T(1)$. The number $c$, rather than the sum of their exterior degrees, is what controls support. We will show that the product of the $c$ return correspondences, followed by an ordinary-HC realization of the quotient map, has target support of codimension at least $d-c$. The next two lemmas construct the required quotient with this bound on $c$; ordinary HC handles trivial $H(d)$. The assembled proof below gives the correspondence calculation.

<a id="stmt-ghc-lem-orthogonal-carriers"></a>
<div id="ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Orthogonal exterior carriers). *Let $V=T(1)$ for a projective K3 surface with $\operatorname{Hg}(V)=\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(V,q)$ and $t=\dim V\ge3$. Let $H$ be a nontrivial rational simple representation occurring in a direct sum of tensor powers of $V$, endowed with its induced weight-zero Hodge structure. If its Hodge level is at most $2b$, there are integers $c,s\ge1$ and $k_1,\ldots,k_c\ge1$ such that $$c\le b,\qquad s=\sum_i k_i,\qquad k_i\le t,
 \qquad \bigotimes_{i=1}^c\bigwedge^{k_i}V\longrightarrow H$$ is a surjective rational Hodge map.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ghc-lem-orthogonal-carriers"><summary>Proof of stmt-ghc-lem-orthogonal-carriers</summary>

*Proof.* Over $\mathbb C$, choose orthogonal torus coordinates so that the Hodge cocharacter has vector weights $1,0,\ldots,0,-1$. The integral highest weights of tensor representations of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_t$ have the form $$\begin{cases}
 \lambda_1\ge\cdots\ge\lambda_l\ge0,&t=2l+1,\\
 \lambda_1\ge\cdots\ge\lambda_{l-1}\ge|\lambda_l|,&t=2l.
 \end{cases}$$ Only integral tensor weights occur here; half-integral spin weights are not representations in tensor powers of the standard module. For an irreducible constituent of highest weight $\lambda$, the largest value of the Hodge cocharacter on its weights is $\lambda_1$: the cocharacter is dominant, and all weights lie in the convex hull of the Weyl orbit of $\lambda$. The largest value is attained at $\lambda$. The orthogonal Weyl group also gives the value $-\lambda_1$. Hence this constituent has Hodge level exactly $2\lambda_1$.

Put $c=\lambda_1$. In odd dimension take the columns of the partition $(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_l)$. Their number is $c$. The tensor product of their exterior highest-weight vectors is a highest-weight vector of weight $\lambda$. Complete reducibility therefore places the desired irreducible representation in the tensor product of these $c$ exterior powers. In even dimension use the columns of $(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{l-1},|\lambda_l|)$. For columns of height $l$, use the highest-weight vector of the appropriate sign in $\bigwedge^lV_\mathbb C$; the two available weights are $(1,\ldots,1,1)$ and $(1,\ldots,1,-1)$. Their sign is chosen to agree with $\lambda_l$. Shorter columns are unchanged. The same highest-weight argument applies. We keep the full exterior powers, including both middle-exterior constituents, as the carriers.

Choose a complex irreducible constituent $H_\lambda$ of $H_\mathbb C$. It has $c=\lambda_1\le b$. The preceding construction defines a rational representation, for $\mathbf k=(k_1,\ldots,k_c)$, $C_{\mathbf k}=\bigotimes_i\bigwedge^{k_i}V$ whose complexification shares this constituent with $H_\mathbb C$. Consequently $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(V,q)}(C_{\mathbf k},H)\otimes_\mathbb Q\mathbb C
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(V_\mathbb C,q)}((C_{\mathbf k})_\mathbb C,H_\mathbb C)\ne0.$$ This scalar-extension identity follows by taking invariants in $(C_{\mathbf k})^*\otimes H$. Any nonzero rational map in this space is surjective by rational simplicity of $H$, and it is a Hodge map because the Hodge cocharacter lies in $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(V,q)$. This proves the required rational assertion without descending an individually chosen complex projector.

For $t=3$ the argument is the single-coordinate case, with columns of height one. For $t=4$ the coordinates are $\lambda_1\ge|\lambda_2|$, and columns have heights one and two; the two signs in the latter give the same construction. Thus the small orthogonal ranks cause no exception. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ghc-lem-k3-carriers"></a>
<div id="ghc:lem:k3-carriers" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Exterior carriers with endomorphism fields). *Let $V=T(1)$ for a projective K3 surface and let $G=\operatorname{Hg}(V)$. If a nontrivial rational simple representation $H$ occurring in a direct sum of tensor powers of $V$ has induced Hodge level at most $2b$, then there are $1\le c\le b$ and $1\le k_1,\ldots,k_c\le\dim_\mathbb QV$ with a surjective rational Hodge map $$\bigotimes_{j=1}^c\bigwedge^{k_j}V\longrightarrow H.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ghc-lem-k3-carriers"><summary>Proof of stmt-ghc-lem-k3-carriers</summary>

*Proof.* Put $E=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{Hdg}}(T)$ and $a=\dim_E T$. We use the full groups in <a href="#ext:real-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:real-hodge-group">[ext:real-hodge-group]</a> and <a href="#ext:cm-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:cm-hodge-group">[ext:cm-hodge-group]</a>. Choose an algebraic closure of $\mathbb Q$ inside $\mathbb C$ over which these groups split. Highest-weight constituents can be described over that closure and then extended to $\mathbb C$. The multiset of constituents of $H$ is invariant under the absolute Galois group because $H$ is rational. We shall use this rationality before applying any Hodge-level bound.

*The possible orthogonal ranks.* In the totally real case, $a\ge3$. Indeed $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_G(V)=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{Hdg}}(V)=E$, because commuting with the Hodge circle is equivalent to commuting with its rational Zariski closure. This equality remains true after scalar extension, by scalar extension of invariants. Put $e=[E:\mathbb Q]$. If $a=1$, the split orthogonal group is trivial, so its commutant on $V$ has dimension $e^2>e$; here $e=\dim_\mathbb QV\ge2$ because the two extreme K3 Hodge lines are nonzero. If $a=2$, each split standard orthogonal module is the sum of two distinct characters of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_2$, so the commutant has dimension at least $2e>e$. Both contradict the dimension $e$ of $E$. Thus the orthogonal factors below have the ranks covered by Lemma <a href="#ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers">7</a>.

*Totally real widths and columns.* Suppose first that $E$ is totally real. Over $\mathbb C$ the group is $\prod_{\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb C}\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(V_\sigma,q_\sigma)$. There is one distinguished embedding $\sigma_0$ for which $V_{\sigma_0}$ has the types $(1,-1)$ and $(-1,1)$; the other summands have type $(0,0)$. Accordingly the Hodge cocharacter is trivial on the other factors and has vector weights $1,0,\ldots,0,-1$ on the distinguished factor.

Fix one complex irreducible constituent of $H_\mathbb C$, with tuple of orthogonal highest weights $\lambda_\sigma$, and write $w_\sigma=\lambda_{\sigma,1}$ in the coordinates of Lemma <a href="#ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers">7</a>. Galois acts transitively on the embeddings of $E$ and transports the actual standard eigenspace of each orthogonal factor to the actual standard eigenspace at the conjugate embedding. The conjugacy class of cocharacters with weights $1,-1,0,\ldots,0$ on that standard module is therefore preserved. The maximum of this class on a constituent is its first highest-weight coordinate, so Galois transports $w_\sigma$ to the corresponding width at $\sigma_0$. This argument also covers type $D_4$: a triality automorphism exchanging the vector and a half-spin module does not preserve the actual standard module and cannot arise from this rational eigenspace transport. That conjugate constituent still occurs in $H_\mathbb C$. The Hodge-level calculation in Lemma <a href="#ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers">7</a> consequently gives $w_\sigma\le b$ for every $\sigma$.

Set $c=\max_\sigma w_\sigma$. For each factor form its signed orthogonal columns as in that lemma, with heights $h_{\sigma j}$ for $1\le j\le c$, padding with zeros. In the even case the sign of a middle column is retained. Set $k_j=\sum_\sigma h_{\sigma j}$. The exterior decomposition of a direct sum includes $$\bigotimes_\sigma\bigwedge^{h_{\sigma j}}V_\sigma
       \ \subset\ \bigwedge^{k_j}V_\mathbb C.$$ Taking the Cartan components over all columns shows that $\bigotimes_j\bigwedge^{k_j}V_\mathbb C$ contains the chosen tuple of irreducibles. Columns from different embeddings thus occupy the same exterior carrier; their widths are maximized, not added. The ranks three and four are included by Lemma <a href="#ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:lem:orthogonal-carriers">7</a>.

*CM widths and signed columns.* Now suppose that $E$ is CM. Choose one embedding $\eta$ from each conjugate pair and write $$G_\mathbb C=\prod_\eta\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}(W_\eta),\qquad
 V_\mathbb C=\bigoplus_\eta(W_\eta\oplus W_\eta^*),
 \qquad \dim W_\eta=a.$$ Choose the representatives so that the distinguished embedding containing $V^{1,-1}$ is represented by a standard module. On this factor the Hodge cocharacter is conjugate to $\operatorname{diag}(z,1,\ldots,1)$; on the other factors it is trivial. Its action on the distinguished dual is the inverse. A complex irreducible constituent of $H_\mathbb C$ has, on each factor, an integral highest weight $$\lambda_{\eta,1}\ge\cdots\ge\lambda_{\eta,a}.$$ These are highest weights for the full general linear group: negative entries and central determinant characters are retained. The extrema of the distinguished cocharacter on a factor of highest weight $\lambda_\eta$ are $\lambda_{\eta,1}$ and $\lambda_{\eta,a}$, by the weight-polytope description and the permutation Weyl group.

Galois acts transitively on the conjugate embedding pairs. Since the decomposition of $V$ comes from its rational $E$-action, this action transports standard modules to standard modules or their duals. On highest weights the latter possibility replaces $(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_a)$ by $(-\lambda_a,\ldots,-\lambda_1)$; it introduces no additional central twist. Transporting each factor to the distinguished one, rationality of $H$ and its level bound therefore imply $$w_\eta:=\max\{\lambda_{\eta,1},
                  -\lambda_{\eta,a},0\}\le b.$$ In particular this bound controls signed central weights as well as differences of adjacent weights.

Set $c=\max_\eta w_\eta$. For $1\le j\le c$ put $$h^+_{\eta j}=\#\{i:\lambda_{\eta,i}\ge j\},\qquad
 h^-_{\eta j}=\#\{i:\lambda_{\eta,i}\le-j\},\qquad
 k_j=\sum_\eta(h^+_{\eta j}+h^-_{\eta j}).$$ The positive and negative columns can be placed in the same exterior power, because its direct-sum decomposition contains $$\bigotimes_\eta
 \left(\bigwedge^{h^+_{\eta j}}W_\eta
       \otimes\bigwedge^{h^-_{\eta j}}W_\eta^*\right)
       \ \subset\ \bigwedge^{k_j}V_\mathbb C.$$ The highest weight of $\bigwedge^h W$ has $h$ initial entries equal to $1$, and that of $\bigwedge^h W^*$ has $h$ final entries equal to $-1$. Summing these weights over $j$ gives exactly $\lambda_\eta$. Their tensor highest-weight vector therefore produces the desired Cartan component, including its central character. Thus the product of these $c$ full exterior carriers contains the chosen constituent. The cost is the maximum of the positive and negative widths, not their sum.

*Rational descent of the carrier.* In both cases $c=0$ would make the chosen constituent trivial. A rational simple representation with a trivial complex constituent is trivial, by scalar extension of invariants, so our nontriviality assumption gives $c\ge1$. A factor attaining the maximum width has a nonempty column for each $1\le j\le c$; therefore $k_j\ge1$. The displayed direct-sum exterior decompositions also give $k_j\le\dim_\mathbb QV$.

Finally, for $\mathbf k=(k_1,\ldots,k_c)$, keep the rational carrier $C_{\mathbf k}=\bigotimes_j\bigwedge^{k_j}V$, rather than selecting any of its complex summands. Sharing a complex constituent yields $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(C_{\mathbf k},H)\otimes_\mathbb Q\mathbb C
       =\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{G_\mathbb C}((C_{\mathbf k})_\mathbb C,H_\mathbb C)\ne0.$$ The equality follows by scalar extension of invariants in $(C_{\mathbf k})^*\otimes H$. A nonzero rational equivariant map is surjective because $H$ is rationally simple, and it is a Hodge map because $G$ contains the Hodge cocharacter. This handles nonsplit forms, Galois-conjugate constituents and their multiplicities without assuming any complex projector rational. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-ghc-lem-k3-carriers-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ghc-lem-k3-carriers</summary>

*Proof of the generalized Hodge assertion in Theorem <a href="#ext:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:criterion">1</a>.* Put $V=T(1)$ and $G=\operatorname{Hg}(V)$, with the group descriptions <a href="#ext:real-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:real-hodge-group">[ext:real-hodge-group]</a> and <a href="#ext:cm-hodge-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:cm-hodge-group">[ext:cm-hodge-group]</a>. The ordinary Hodge assertion just proved gives rational HC for every power of $S$ under the same exact-map hypothesis. We use that conditional conclusion in both endomorphism-field cases.

Fix a rational sub-Hodge structure $H\subset H^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q)$ of Hodge coniveau at least $p$, where $0\le p\le d$. By semisimplicity it suffices to treat $H$ simple. The K3 Künneth decomposition shows that $H^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q)(d)$ is a direct sum of tensor powers of $V$: its other factors, coming from $H^0$, the Néron–Severi part of $H^2$, and $H^4$, are Tate and become trivial after the total normalization. Thus $H(d)$ is a rational simple representation of $G$, of Hodge level at most $2(d-p)$.

If $H(d)$ is trivial, $H$ has type $(d,d)$. Ordinary HC and a finite basis of cycle classes put $H$ in $N^dH^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q)\subset N^pH^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q)$. Otherwise Lemma <a href="#ghc:lem:k3-carriers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:lem:k3-carriers">8</a> supplies a tuple $\mathbf k=(k_1,\ldots,k_c)$ and a surjective rational Hodge map $$f:C_{\mathbf k}(s)\longrightarrow H(d),\qquad
 C_{\mathbf k}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^c\bigwedge^{k_i}T
       \subset H^{2s}(S^s,\mathbb Q),\quad
 s=\sum_i k_i,\quad c\le d-p.$$ Extend $f$ by zero on a rational Hodge complement of $C_{\mathbf k}(s)$ in $H^{2s}(S^s,\mathbb Q)(s)$, which exists by polarizability. Poincaré duality identifies this extended map to $H(d)$ with a rational Hodge class of codimension $s+d$ on $S^s\times S^m$. Ordinary HC for $S^{s+m}$ therefore gives $$C_f\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{s+d}(S^s\times S^m)_\mathbb Q$$ realizing it.

Retain $Z=A^2$ and $n=\dim Z$ from Proposition <a href="#ext:conditional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:conditional">4</a>. For each column take the return $R_{k_i}$ of <a href="#ext:left-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:left-inverse">[ext:left-inverse]</a>. Their exterior product, with factors reordered, gives $$R_{\mathbf k}\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{nc+s-c}(Z^c\times S^s)_\mathbb Q,
 \qquad (R_{\mathbf k})_*:H^{2c}(Z^c,\mathbb Q)\longrightarrow H^{2s}(S^s,\mathbb Q),$$ whose image contains $C_{\mathbf k}$. Indeed the tensor product of the $F_{k_i}$ is a right inverse on $C_{\mathbf k}$. Composition gives $$C_f\circ R_{\mathbf k}\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{nc+d-c}(Z^c\times S^m)_\mathbb Q,$$ since $(nc+s-c)+(s+d)-2s=nc+d-c$. Its image on $H^{2c}(Z^c,\mathbb Q)$ contains $H$, and its support has dimension $$nc+2m-(nc+d-c)=2m-d+c.$$ Lemma <a href="#ghc:lem:cycle-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ghc:lem:cycle-support">5</a> yields $$H\subset N^{d-c}H^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q)
       \subset N^pH^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q).$$ For a nonsimple substructure, take the finite union of the supports obtained for its simple summands. This treats every actual occurrence, including multiplicities and substructures embedded diagonally across isomorphic Künneth summands.

For the converse, put $X=S^m$ and let $B\subset X$ be any closed support of codimension at least $p$, including a singular or non-equidimensional support. For each irreducible component $B_i$ choose a smooth projective resolution and let $q_i:\widetilde B_i\to X$ be its proper map; put $c_i=\operatorname{codim}_X B_i\ge p$. Deligne’s exactness theorem for a proper smooth variety and a closed support, using the disjoint union of these resolutions as a proper smooth surjective source <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[DeligneHodgeIII](#ref-DeligneHodgeIII), Proposition 8.2.7 and Corollary 8.2.8], gives $$\ker\bigl(H^j(X,\mathbb Q)\longrightarrow H^j(X\setminus B,\mathbb Q)\bigr)
   =\sum_i(q_i)_*H^{j-2c_i}(\widetilde B_i,\mathbb Q).$$ Each proper Gysin map is a Hodge morphism $H^{j-2c_i}(\widetilde B_i,\mathbb Q)(-c_i)\to H^j(X,\mathbb Q)$. The source before twisting has nonnegative Hodge indices, since $\widetilde B_i$ is smooth projective; after twisting both indices are at least $c_i\ge p$. Taking sums over $B$ therefore proves that geometric coniveau $p$ has Hodge coniveau at least $p$.

Thus the two filtrations agree. If $p>d$, the same reverse inclusion forces $N^pH^{2d}(S^m,\mathbb Q)=0$, since a pure structure of weight $2d$ cannot have both indices at least $p$. Cases $p\le0$ are immediate. Odd cohomology of $S^m$ vanishes. The result therefore holds in every degree, not only in middle cohomology or the pure transcendental tensor summand. ◻

</details>

<a id="geom:section"></a>
### Abelian double covers and their periods

We construct a rank-eight variation of K3 type with quadratic space $V_P$ and show that its period map has rank six. The variation occurs in the cohomology of a double cover of an abelian surface. A resolved inversion quotient supplies the quadratic form that will be used in the K3 realization.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The double cover

Work on a smooth finite-level chart in the moduli space of principally polarized abelian surfaces. Let $B$ be an indecomposable principally polarized abelian surface, let $M=\mathcal O_B(H)$ be a symmetric line bundle defining the principal polarization, and choose $\chi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(B)[4]$ of exact order four. The symmetric representative $M$ and the torsion point $\chi$ add only finite choices. Products of line bundles will be written without tensor symbols when convenient.

We recall the theta facts needed below. Ample vanishing and Riemann–Roch on $B$ give $$h^0(B,M\chi)=h^0(B,M\chi^{-1})=1,\qquad h^0(B,M^2)=4,$$ with no higher cohomology for these bundles. The linear system $|M^2|$ is basepoint-free: the sums of opposite translates of $H$ belong to it, and for any point of $B$ one can choose both translates to avoid that point. The normalized symmetric linearization of $M^2$ fixes every section. For example, in period coordinates its four second-order theta sections are $\theta[\epsilon;0](2z,2\tau)$, with $\epsilon\in(\tfrac12\mathbb Z/\mathbb Z)^2$; reindexing the defining series by $n\mapsto-n-2\epsilon$ proves that they are even. Their distinct Fourier congruence classes give a basis. Changing the symmetric theta representative tensors $M$ by two-torsion and leaves its square, with this normalized linearization, unchanged. These descriptions agree with the standard theta constructions in <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[MumfordTheta](#ref-MumfordTheta), [BirkenhakeLange](#ref-BirkenhakeLange)].

Choose $[d]\in\mathbf PH^0(B,M^2)$, and write $D=(d=0)$. We restrict to the nonempty open where $D$ is smooth and avoids $B[2]$; nonemptiness follows from Bertini and basepoint-freeness, by avoiding the finitely many evaluation hyperplanes at $B[2]$. Every such $D$ is invariant under inversion. The parameter space is thus an open in a projective $3$-space bundle over the three-dimensional abelian-data chart, and has dimension six.

All families below are formed on algebraic affine charts of this projective bundle.<span id="geom:section-charts" label="geom:section-charts"></span> After trivializing its rank-four section bundle, choose a nonzero relative functional $\ell$. On the chart $\ell(d)\ne0$ use the actual normalized universal section $\ell(d)=1$. On a simply connected product polydisc, two such normalizations differ by a nonvanishing scalar with a holomorphic square root. Rescaling the cover coordinate by that square root identifies the resulting covers, their involutions, and their rational eigenspaces.

<a id="geom:double-cover"></a>
Let $$\pi:B'\longrightarrow B,\qquad
 \pi_*\mathcal O_{B'}=\mathcal O_B\oplus\chi^2$$ be the degree-two isogeny attached to $\chi^2$. Choose a trivialization of $\pi^*\chi^2$ and form $$\label{geom:double-cover}
 L=\pi^*(M\chi),\qquad
 f:X=\operatorname{Spec}_{B'}(\mathcal O_{B'}\oplus L^{-1})
       \longrightarrow B',\qquad z^2=\pi^*d.$$ The chosen trivialization identifies $L^2$ with $\pi^*M^2$, so $\pi^*d$ defines the multiplication in this algebra. The surface $X$ is smooth. Write $\delta$ for its sheet involution.

The line bundle $\pi^*\chi$ has order two, so $L$ is symmetric. Normalize its symmetric linearization at the origin. Its square and the pullback of the normalized linearization of $M^2$ agree at the origin and hence everywhere, since an automorphism of a line bundle on an abelian variety is scalar. Inversion therefore lifts to an involution $j$ of $X$ commuting with $\delta$. Put $$U_X=H^2(X,\mathbb Q)^{j=1,\,\delta=-1}.$$

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The eigenspace and its quadratic form

<a id="stmt-geom-structure"></a>
<div id="geom:structure" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9**. *There is a smooth double cover $Y\to K=\mathop{\mathrm{Km}}(B')$, with $K$ the resolved Kummer surface, satisfying the following assertions.*

1.  *The anti-invariant lattice $$V=H^2(Y,\mathbb Z)^{\delta=-1}$$ (with torsion removed) is even, has signature $(2,14)$, and has $2$-elementary discriminant group. For fixed $(B,M,\chi)$, monodromy acts trivially on this discriminant group.*

2.  *There are eight mutually orthogonal algebraic anti-invariant classes $a_1,\ldots,a_8$, each of square $-4$. Their perpendicular $$U=\langle a_1,\ldots,a_8\rangle^\perp\subset V_\mathbb Q$$ is a Hodge structure of signature $(2,6)$ and, with the intersection form inherited from $Y$, is rationally isometric to $$V_P=(\mathbb U\oplus\mathbb U(2)\oplus D_4(-1))_\mathbb Q.$$*

<a id="geom:projector"></a>
3.  *Resolved pullback gives a flat Hodge isomorphism $\beta:U\xrightarrow{\sim}U_X$ with $$(\beta x,\beta y)_X=2(x,y)_Y.$$ The algebraic projector $$\label{geom:projector}
     e=\frac14(\Delta_X+\Gamma_j-\Gamma_\delta-\Gamma_{j\delta})
           \in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(X\times X)_\mathbb Q$$ has image $U_X$ in degree two and is zero in every other degree.*

*The constructions are compatible with variation on the indicated smooth parameter opens.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-structure"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-structure</summary>

*Proof.* We first determine the fibre signs of the normalized involution on $L$. Write $B=\mathbb C^2/\Lambda$ and represent $\chi$ by a character $c:\Lambda\to\mu_4$ of exact order four. Then $B'=\mathbb C^2/\Lambda'$, where $\Lambda'=\ker(c^2)$. Choose $\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda'$ and put $k=\lambda\bmod\Lambda'\in\ker\pi$. Translation by $k$ pairs the sixteen points of $B'[2]$. The fibre sign of $\pi^*M$ is the same at $x$ and $x+k$, since these points have the same image in $B$. For the flat factor, representing $x$ by $v$ with $2v\in\Lambda'$ shows that its sign changes by $$\frac{c(2v+2\lambda)}{c(2v)}=c(2\lambda)=-1.$$ Thus the signs of $L$ in each pair are opposite: eight are positive and eight negative.

The branch curve is absent near these sixteen points. At a positive-sign point, $j$ preserves each local sheet of $X$ and acts by $(u,v)\mapsto(-u,-v)$. The quotient has two ordinary double points, whose resolutions give two disjoint $(-2)$-curves over the corresponding exceptional curve of $K$. At a negative-sign point, $j$ exchanges the local sheets. The quotient is smooth, and its map to $B'/\{\pm1\}$ is locally $\mathbb C^2\to\mathbb C^2/\{\pm1\}$. Blowing up this smooth source point resolves the map to $K$ and gives a double cover branched along the exceptional curve of $K$. These local models construct the smooth double cover $Y\to K$.

Set $D'=\pi^{-1}D$. It is smooth and connected, since it is ample, and $(D')^2=16$. Adjunction gives $g(D')=9$. Inversion acts freely on $D'$, so its quotient has genus five. The branch divisor of $Y\to K$ is the disjoint union of that quotient and the eight exceptional curves belonging to the negative signs. Therefore $$e(Y)=2e(K)-\bigl((2-2\cdot5)+8\cdot2\bigr)=48-8=40.$$ The cover algebra, Serre duality, and ample vanishing give $H^1(B',L^{-1})=0$ and hence $H^1(X,\mathbb Q)=f^*H^1(B',\mathbb Q)$. Inversion is $-1$ on this space. Taking quotient invariants, and using invariance of $H^1$ under the intervening blowups, gives $q(Y)=0$. For holomorphic two-forms, $$H^{2,0}(X)=H^{2,0}(B')\oplus H^0(B',L),\qquad
 H^0(B',L)=H^0(B,M\chi)\oplus H^0(B,M\chi^{-1}).$$ The first summand is invariant under both involutions. Inversion replaces $\chi$ by $\chi^{-1}$; the trivialization of $\pi^*\chi^2$ used to identify the pullbacks has the nontrivial deck character, so it exchanges the two one-dimensional summands in $H^0(B',L)$. They are sheet-anti-invariant. Thus $p_g(Y)=2$. Consequently $b_2(Y)=38$, and Hodge index gives signature $(5,33)$ on $H^2(Y)$.

The sheet-invariant rational cohomology is the pullback of $H^2(K,\mathbb Q)$, with pairing multiplied by two, and has signature $(3,19)$. Its perpendicular $V_\mathbb Q$ has signature $(2,14)$. The canonical class $K_Y$ is invariant. For $v\in V$ we have $v\cdot K_Y=0$, and Wu’s characteristic parity formula <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[MilnorStasheff](#ref-MilnorStasheff), Theorem 11.14 and Problem 14-B]$$v^2\equiv v\cdot K_Y\pmod2$$ shows that $V$ is even.

For the integral assertions, let $\mathcal H=H^2(Y,\mathbb Z)/\mathrm{torsion}$ and let $\mathcal H^+$ be its invariant lattice. Integral Poincaré duality makes $\mathcal H$ unimodular. Both eigensublattices are primitive, and $$2x=(x+\delta x)+(x-\delta x)
 \quad\text{for every }x\in\mathcal H.$$ The quotient $\mathcal H/(\mathcal H^+\oplus V)$ is therefore killed by two. Unimodular gluing identifies this quotient with each of $A_{\mathcal H^+}$ and $A_V$, proving that their discriminant groups are $2$-elementary. For fixed $B'$, pullback from the fixed Kummer surface makes the invariant rational cohomology pointwise constant. Its integral sublattice and its discriminant group are consequently fixed as well. The gluing identification is equivariant, so monodromy acts trivially on $A_V$.

At a positive-sign point, denote the two exceptional curves on $Y$ by $E_i^+$ and $E_i^-$. They are exchanged by $\delta$, and $$a_i=[E_i^+]-[E_i^-],\qquad a_i^2=-4.$$ The eight differences are mutually orthogonal. Parallel transport can permute their indices or exchange the two curves, but preserves their span, so its perpendicular $U$ is flat. Near a degeneration at which the branch passes through a positive-sign point, the two labels are governed by a scalar square root; the local action on $a_i$ changes its sign.

The same local models identify $U$ with $U_X$. Resolving the two quotient nodes at a positive-sign point adds precisely one sheet-anti-invariant exceptional direction, namely $a_i$; the blowup at a negative-sign point adds only a sheet-invariant direction. On a common resolution of $X\dashrightarrow Y$, pullback of a class in $U$ is orthogonal to all exceptional classes over $X$, and hence comes from $H^2(X,\mathbb Q)$. Its image is exactly the $(j,\delta)=(1,-1)$ eigenspace. The degree-two projection formula gives $$(\beta x,\beta y)_X=2(x,y)_Y.$$ In particular, $U$ has signature $(2,6)$. We retain its $Y$-pairing throughout the rational quadratic-form and K3 arguments.

The correspondence <a href="#geom:projector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:projector">[geom:projector]</a> is the product of the commuting projectors $(1+j)/2$ and $(1-\delta)/2$. The sheet involution fixes $H^0$ and $H^4$, and the calculation of $H^1(X,\mathbb Q)$ shows that it fixes $H^1$; by duality it fixes $H^3$ as well. Thus $e$ vanishes outside degree two and has image exactly $U_X$ there.

We now determine the rational form on $U$ with its $Y$-pairing. The invariant lattice $\mathcal H^+$ contains the pullback lattice $H^2(K,\mathbb Z)(2)$ with finite index, necessarily a power of two. The latter has absolute determinant $2^{22}$, so $|\det\mathcal H^+|$ is a square in $\mathbb Q^*/\mathbb Q^{*2}$. Unimodular gluing gives the same absolute determinant for $V$. Since the negative index of $V$ is fourteen, its determinant is positive. Splitting off the eight orthogonal lines of square $-4$ therefore gives $$\det(U)=1\quad\text{in }\mathbb Q^*/\mathbb Q^{*2}.$$ For an odd prime $p$, the projectors $(1\pm\delta)/2$ split $\mathcal H\otimes\mathbb Z_p$ orthogonally, so $V\otimes\mathbb Z_p$ is unimodular. The $a_i$ span a unimodular $\mathbb Z_p$-lattice, since their Gram determinant is $(-4)^8$. Orthogonal projection using its inverse Gram matrix is integral over $\mathbb Z_p$. Its perpendicular thus gives a unimodular $\mathbb Z_p$-lattice in $U\otimes\mathbb Q_p$.
