The lattice $T_P$ has rank eight, signature $(2,6)$, and determinant $(-1)(-4)(4)=16$; it too is unimodular at every odd prime. A unimodular quadratic form over $\mathbb Z_p$, for odd $p$, diagonalizes with unit entries, and the Hilbert symbol of two units is $1$. Hence both rational forms have Hasse invariant $1$ at every odd prime. Their real Hasse invariants are $(-1)^{\binom62}=-1$. Hilbert reciprocity then gives the same invariant, namely $-1$, at $2$. The local classification and the rational local-to-global isometry criterion now give $U\simeq V_P$. For a symmetric pairing $q$ we use the quadratic polynomial $Q(x)=q(x,x)$, whose diagonal coefficients are those of $q$ itself in the conventions of <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[PetersSterk](#ref-PetersSterk), Equations (3.1)–(3.2)]. The precise Hilbert-symbol, reciprocity, and classification results are <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[PetersSterk](#ref-PetersSterk), Theorems A.4.4, A.4.6, 3.3.1, and 3.3.5].

The covers, blowups, quotients, and graph correspondences are all relative after the finite choices and restriction to the stated smooth opens. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The character eigenspace and the period differential

<a id="stmt-geom-twisted-hodge"></a>
<div id="geom:twisted-hodge" class="lemma">

<a id="geom:hodge-eigensheaves"></a>
<a id="geom:derivative"></a>
**Lemma 10**. *Put $E=T_B(-\log D)$. There is a flat complex identification of $U_X$ with a character eigenspace having Hodge components $$\label{geom:hodge-eigensheaves}
 U_X^{p,q}\simeq
 H^q\bigl(B,\Omega_B^p(\log D)\otimes M^{-1}\chi\bigr),
 \qquad p+q=2.$$ In particular, $U_X^{2,0}=H^0(B,M\chi)$ is one-dimensional. Fix a nonzero translation-invariant volume form $\Omega$ on $B$, and let $\theta\in H^0(B,M\chi)$ represent this line. Under <a href="#geom:hodge-eigensheaves" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:hodge-eigensheaves">[geom:hodge-eigensheaves]</a>, the period differential is the map on $H^1$ induced by $$\label{geom:derivative}
 E\xrightarrow{\ \theta\ }E\otimes M\chi
       \simeq\Omega_B^1(\log D)\otimes M^{-1}\chi.$$ The Kodaira–Spencer map identifies the tangent space of the full six-dimensional family $(B,M,\chi,[d])$ with $H^1(B,E)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-twisted-hodge"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-twisted-hodge</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\alpha:\widetilde B\to B$ be the cyclic degree-four isogeny trivializing $\chi$, with generator $g$, and let $Z\to B$ be the untwisted double cover $w^2=d$ defined using $M$. On $\widetilde X=Z\times_B\widetilde B$, let $\delta$ change the sign of $w$, and let $g$ act through the isogeny. A trivialization of $\alpha^*\chi$ identifies $$X=\widetilde X/\langle\delta g^2\rangle.$$ On sheet-anti-invariant cohomology, invariance under $\delta g^2$ selects exactly the $g$-eigenvalues $i$ and $-i$. Inversion conjugates $g$ to $g^{-1}$ and exchanges these eigenspaces. Projection to either one therefore identifies their $j$-invariant part with that eigenspace over $\mathbb C$. Choose the generator so that the selected character sheaf is $\chi$; the other convention replaces $\chi$ by $\chi^{-1}$. These finite-group projectors commute with parallel transport.

The eigensheaves can be read directly from the local double-cover equation. If $D=(x=0)$ and $w^2=x$, the sheet-odd holomorphic forms are generated over the base by $$w;\qquad w\,dy,\quad w\,\frac{dx}{x};
 \qquad w\,\frac{dx}{x}\wedge dy$$ in degrees zero, one, and two. The odd generator has the transition rule of $M^{-1}$. Thus the sheet-odd direct image of holomorphic $p$-forms is $\Omega_B^p(\log D)M^{-1}$; selecting the character tensors this by $\chi$. Its logarithmic flat differential has local term $\tfrac12d\log x$, together with the flat connection on $\chi$. The Hodge decomposition of the smooth projective finite cover, with the character projector applied, gives <a href="#geom:hodge-eigensheaves" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:hodge-eigensheaves">[geom:hodge-eigensheaves]</a>.

Infinitesimal changes of local coordinates for the pair $(B,D)$ give a Čech cocycle of logarithmic vector fields in $E$. Locally, a logarithmic field $v$ satisfying $v(f)=af$ lifts on $w^2=f$ to $v+(a/2)w\partial_w$. This lift is unique: an infinitesimal vertical change preserving $w^2=f$ has coefficient $b$ with $2b=0$. Contraction with the sheet-odd two-form computes the Kodaira–Spencer action on the Hodge line. The local generators just displayed identify this contraction with multiplication by $\theta$ in <a href="#geom:derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:derivative">[geom:derivative]</a>. The bundle isomorphism there is the rank-two determinant identity $$E\otimes\mathcal O_B(D)\simeq\Omega_B^1(\log D),
 \qquad \mathcal O_B(D)=M^2.$$ The chosen finite-order line bundles have unique infinitesimal extensions with their torsion data, so they add no tangent directions.

It remains to identify all tangent directions of our family. Adjunction and the logarithmic normal sequence give $$0\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow T_B
       \longrightarrow\mathcal O_D(D)=K_D\longrightarrow0.$$ An infinitesimal translation preserving $D$ is zero: its flow would lie in the stabilizer of $\mathcal O_B(D)$, the finite kernel of the polarization isogeny. Thus $H^0(E)=0$, and the cohomology sequence yields $$0\longrightarrow
 \frac{H^0(K_D)}{H^0(T_B)}
 \longrightarrow H^1(E)\longrightarrow
 \ker\bigl(H^1(T_B)\longrightarrow H^1(K_D)\bigr)
 \longrightarrow0.$$ Here $h^0(K_D)=5$, $h^0(T_B)=2$, and $h^1(T_B)=4$. The sequence $$0\longrightarrow\mathcal O_B\xrightarrow{\ d\ }M^2
       \longrightarrow K_D\longrightarrow0$$ identifies $H^1(K_D)$ with $H^2(\mathcal O_B)$. Under this identification both normal-sequence boundary maps are contraction with $c_1(D)$. Indeed, choose local frames $e_j=g_{ij}e_i$ of $M^2$ and write $d=f_i e_i$, so $f_i=g_{ij}f_j$. Pulling the displayed extension back along $T_B\to K_D$, the local lift of a vector field $v$ is $v(f_i)e_i$. Differences of these lifts, divided by $d$, are $v(\log g_{ij})$. The pulled-back extension therefore has class $c_1(D)\in H^1(\Omega_B^1)$, and its connecting maps in degrees zero and one are the asserted contractions. In degree one the target is the one-dimensional $H^2(\mathcal O_B)$. This contraction is nonzero and its three-dimensional kernel is exactly the tangent space to polarized deformations of $B$. Hence $h^1(E)=3+3=6$.

Extending $M$ imposes the same condition, since $c_1(M)=\tfrac12c_1(M^2)$. Among its infinitesimal extensions the symmetric representative is unique: differences form an $H^1(\mathcal O_B)$-torsor on which inversion acts by $-1$. The order-four point $\chi$ and the associated isogenies extend uniquely because torsion of the relative Picard variety is finite étale. Thus the root and torsion data create no additional infinitesimal condition.

For fixed $B$, the section directions are $H^0(M^2)/\langle d\rangle$. Their images in $H^0(K_D)$ have zero boundary in $H^1(\mathcal O_B)$. On a translation vector $v$, that same boundary is $v\mathbin{\lrcorner}c_1(D)$, and the map $$H^0(T_B)\longrightarrow H^1(\mathcal O_B),\qquad
 v\longmapsto v\mathbin{\lrcorner}c_1(D)$$ is an isomorphism because $D$ is ample. Section directions therefore identify with the entire quotient $H^0(K_D)/H^0(T_B)$. Every polarized first-order deformation lifts to our projective bundle of sections, by ample vanishing and base change. The induced map on the right-hand term is the polarized Kodaira–Spencer isomorphism. The two identifications in the exact sequence prove that the full Kodaira–Spencer map is an isomorphism. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Full rank of the period map

<a id="stmt-geom-period"></a>
<div id="geom:period" class="proposition">

**Proposition 11**. *The period map of $U$, equivalently of $U_X$, has rank six on a nonempty Zariski-open subset of the six-dimensional parameter space. For a very general parameter, its rational Hodge structure is simple, has no nonzero rational $(1,1)$-class, and has Hodge group $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-period"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-period</summary>

*Proof.* Let $F=(\theta=0)$. It is a translate of the theta curve of $B$, hence is a smooth genus-two curve with $\mathop{\mathrm{Jac}}(F)\simeq B$. For clarity, these facts use indecomposability as follows. An integral singular theta curve would have normalization of genus at most one and could not generate the abelian surface. If the theta divisor is not integral, ampleness and square two force two reduced elliptic components meeting once, and hence the product polarization: each component has positive integral intersection with $H$ and nonnegative even self-intersection. These possibilities are excluded. Adjunction gives genus two, and the restriction pairing on $H^1(B,\mathbb Z)$, obtained by cup product with the principal theta class, is unimodular. The induced map on Jacobians, of equal dimension, is therefore an isomorphism.

<a id="geom:quotient-sequence"></a>
Set $K=K_F$ and $\eta=\chi^{-2}|_F$. Restriction $\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(B)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(F)$ is an isomorphism, so $\eta$ is nontrivial two-torsion. Adjunction gives $$(M\chi)|_F=K,\qquad M^2|_F=K^{\otimes2}\otimes\eta.$$ For a transverse $D$, multiplication by $\theta$ yields $$\label{geom:quotient-sequence}
 0\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow E\otimes M\chi
       \longrightarrow(E\otimes M\chi)|_F\longrightarrow0.$$ We shall prove that the last sheaf has no global sections for general $d$. By the cohomology sequence and Lemma <a href="#geom:twisted-hodge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:twisted-hodge">10</a>, this implies injectivity of the period differential on all six tangent directions.

Put $Z=D\cap F$, a reduced divisor of degree four. Restricting the logarithmic normal sequence to $F$ is exact because the intersection is transverse. After tensoring by $K$, its map on global sections is <a id="geom:tangency-matrix"></a>$$\label{geom:tangency-matrix}
 A_d:H^0(F,K)^{\oplus2}\longrightarrow
 H^0\bigl(Z,(K^{\otimes3}\otimes\eta)|_Z\bigr),\qquad
 v\longmapsto (v\mathbin{\lrcorner}dd)|_Z.$$ Its kernel is $H^0(F,(E\otimes M\chi)|_F)$, and source and target both have dimension four. Differentiating a section at its zero scheme is intrinsic, so $A_d$ is independent of local bundle frames.

The restriction sequence for $F$, together with ample vanishing, gives a surjection $$H^0(B,M^2)\longrightarrow H^0(F,K^{\otimes2}\otimes\eta)$$ whose kernel is generated by $\theta\theta'$, where $0\ne\theta'\in H^0(B,M\chi^{-1})$. Its restriction $t=\theta'|_F$ is a nonzero section of $K\otimes\eta$: a section vanishing along $F$ would give a section of the nontrivial flat bundle $\chi^{-2}$. The bundle $K^{\otimes2}\otimes\eta$ is basepoint-free by Riemann–Roch on the genus-two curve. We may therefore fix a general restriction $s=d|_F$ whose four zeros are simple and avoid the zeros of $t$.

Replacing $d$ by $d+\lambda\theta\theta'$ fixes $s$ and $Z$ and replaces $A_d$ by $A_d+\lambda A'$. Choose a basis $v_1,v_2$ of translation-invariant vector fields on $B$. The normal derivatives $$\theta_i=(v_i\mathbin{\lrcorner}d\theta)|_F,\qquad i=1,2,$$ form a basis of $H^0(F,K)$: the map to this space is injective because the theta divisor has finite translation stabilizer, and both spaces have dimension two. In these coordinates, $$A'(a,b)=t(a\theta_1+b\theta_2)|_Z.$$ The canonical pencil of a genus-two curve gives an isomorphism $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2H^0(K)\simeq H^0(K^{\otimes2})$. Moreover, $K^{\otimes2}(-Z)\simeq\eta$ has no section, so restriction to $Z$ is injective. Since $t$ does not vanish on $Z$, we obtain $$\mathop{\mathrm{im}}A'=t\,H^0(K^{\otimes2})|_Z,\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{rank}}A'=3.$$ The kernel is spanned by the skew gradient $k=(\theta_2,-\theta_1)$. The canonical pencil has no base point, so this is a nowhere-zero tangent section of $T_F\otimes K\simeq\mathcal O_F$.

Under $T_F\otimes K\simeq\mathcal O_F$, the vector $k$ is a constant $c\ne0$. With the adjunction convention $\operatorname{Res}(\Omega/h)$, where $\theta=h e$, this constant is $c=-\Omega(v_1,v_2)$: writing $\Omega=J\,dh\wedge du$ identifies $k$ with $-\Omega(v_1,v_2)\partial_u\otimes du$. Consequently $A_d(k)=c\,d_Fs|_Z$. Here $d_Fs|_Z$ is an intrinsic section of $(K^{\otimes3}\otimes\eta)|_Z$. To show that this vector is outside $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}A'$, consider the sequence $$0\longrightarrow\eta\xrightarrow{\ \cdot s\ }K^{\otimes2}
       \longrightarrow K^{\otimes2}|_Z\longrightarrow0,$$ where $\eta^{\otimes2}\simeq\mathcal O_F$ is understood. The connecting class of $(d_Fs)/t|_Z$ pairs by Serre duality with $t\in H^0(K\otimes\eta)$. This pairing is computed by local principal parts. Near $z\in Z$, choose a coordinate $u$ and an $\eta$-frame $\epsilon$ with $\epsilon^2=1$, and write $s=f(u)(du)^2\epsilon$ and $t=g(u)du\,\epsilon$. Here $f(z)=0$, $f'(z)\ne0$, and $g(z)\ne0$. A local lift of $(d_Fs)/t|_Z$ is $(f'/g)(du)^2$. Dividing by $s$ gives the $\eta$-valued principal part $[f'/(gf)]\epsilon$; pairing it with $t$ gives $(f'/f)du$. Thus $$\left\langle\partial\bigl((d_Fs)/t|_Z\bigr),t\right\rangle
   =\sum_{z\in Z}\operatorname{Res}_z\frac{df}{f}=4,$$ up to the fixed nonzero normalization of Serre duality. Each zero is simple. Changing a frame adds a regular differential and leaves the residues unchanged. The formula concerns the four local principal parts; it does not require a global meromorphic differential with those four poles. The connecting class is nonzero, and hence $A_d(\ker A')$ is not contained in $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}A'$.

Choose source and target bases in which $A'$ has an invertible $3\times3$ block and is zero in the remaining row and column. The coefficient of $\lambda^3$ in $\det(A_d+\lambda A')$ is the determinant of that block multiplied by the induced map $\ker A'\to\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}A'$. It is nonzero by the preceding pairing. Thus $A_{d+\lambda\theta\theta'}$ is invertible for general $\lambda$. This construction shows that invertibility is a nonempty open condition in the space of sections. Intersecting with the nonempty open of smooth divisors avoiding $B[2]$ gives the required general $d$.

It follows from <a href="#geom:quotient-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:quotient-sequence">[geom:quotient-sequence]</a> that $H^1(E)\to H^1(E\otimes M\chi)$ is injective. By Lemma <a href="#geom:twisted-hodge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:twisted-hodge">10</a>, its source is the entire six-dimensional tangent space. The type-IV period domain of a quadratic space of signature $(2,6)$ has complex dimension six; the period map is therefore a local isomorphism at these points. The rank condition is Zariski open, since it is the rank condition on the algebraic Kodaira–Spencer map between Hodge bundles.

Finally, on a type-IV period domain the Hodge circles rotate its oriented positive two-planes. These rotations generate the identity component of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U_\mathbb R)$, and rotations from any nonempty open have the same Zariski closure. Hence the Hodge circles in our open period chart are not all contained in any proper rational algebraic subgroup of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U)$.

We recall why the exceptional conditions may be taken complex analytic. A proper rational algebraic subgroup stabilizes a rational line in a tensor representation that is not stabilized by $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U)$. Indeed, take a finite-degree representation of regular functions containing generators of the subgroup’s defining ideal, intersect it with that ideal, and take the top exterior power. The resulting line has precisely the required stabilizer. Polynomial representations and semisimplicity place this representation in a sum of tensor powers and duals of $U(1)$. A Hodge circle stabilizing a rational line fixes it pointwise, because it is connected and compact. Thus it forces a specified rational tensor to have type $(0,0)$. Each such condition is a closed complex-analytic Hodge locus, and it is proper unless that tensor is fixed by all the Hodge circles in the domain. There are only countably many rational tensors. Outside their proper loci, $\mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(U)=\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U)$. The standard representation of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U)$ is absolutely irreducible and has no invariants. Simplicity of $U$ and the absence of nonzero rational $(1,1)$-classes follow. ◻

</details>

<a id="domination:section"></a>
### Coherent half sums and domination by a product of curves

We retain the data $(B,M,\chi,D)$ of Section <a href="#geom:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section">3</a>, with $M=\mathcal O_B(H)$ and $D\in|2H|$ smooth and disjoint from $B[2]$. The regular Gauss fibers of $D$ consist of four opposite pairs. Coherently taking half the sum of one point from each pair produces two curves mapping to $B$. We prove that their addition map has total degree eight and pulls $D$ back to an even divisor. Separate covers of the two curves then give a morphism to the surface $X$ of Equation <a href="#geom:double-cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:double-cover">[geom:double-cover]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The generic Gauss pencil

<a id="stmt-geom-gauss-generic"></a>
<div id="geom:gauss-generic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *For $(B,D)$ in a nonempty Zariski-open subset of the parameter space, $D$ has no nontrivial translation stabilizer. Its quotient $C=D/\{\pm1\}$ is a smooth genus-three curve, and the connected étale cover $D\to C$ is defined by a nontrivial line bundle $\epsilon\in\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(C)[2]$. The Gauss map is the composite $$D\longrightarrow C\xrightarrow{|K_C\otimes\epsilon|}\mathbf P^1,$$ of degrees two and four. No fiber of the degree-four map has all its multiplicities even. The good open remains nonempty on every connected component obtained by adjoining the finite theta, torsion, and level choices used to define $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-gauss-generic"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-gauss-generic</summary>

*Proof.* The smooth ample divisor $D$ is connected: the divisor sequence and $H^1(B,\mathcal O_B(-D))=0$ give $H^0(D,\mathcal O_D)=\mathbb C$. Thus it is irreducible. Adjunction gives $2g(D)-2=D^2=8$. Inversion acts freely because $D$ avoids $B[2]$, so Riemann–Hurwitz gives $g(C)=3$. Connectedness of the double cover makes $\epsilon$ nontrivial.

The curve $D$ generates $B$: containment in a translate of a proper abelian subvariety would make $D$ an elliptic curve with square zero. Hence $J(D)\to B$ is surjective and invariant one-forms on $B$ restrict injectively to $D$. They are anti-invariant under inversion and span the two-dimensional space $H^0(C,K_C\otimes\epsilon)$, whose dimension follows from Riemann–Roch. They have no common zero on $D$, since its tangent line is a nonzero subspace of $T_0B$. They therefore define the basepoint-free pencil $|K_C\otimes\epsilon|$, of degree four. Identifying its target with the projective line of tangent directions identifies the composite with the Gauss map.

We justify that these pairs $(C,\epsilon)$ are general. The open projective bundle of smooth curves in $|2H|$ over the three-dimensional level moduli of principally polarized abelian surfaces has dimension six and maps algebraically to the moduli space $\mathcal R_3$ of genus-three curves with nonzero two-torsion. Its fibers are countable. In fact, fixing $(C,\epsilon)$ fixes the double cover $D$; any map of that curve to an abelian surface is a homomorphism from $J(D)$ followed by a translation. Surjective homomorphisms, up to isomorphism of their targets, form a countable set: the connected kernel is determined by a rational subspace of $H_1(J(D),\mathbb Q)$, and the further finite isogeny choices are countable. For a fixed homomorphism, requiring inversion on the image to be the given involution on $D$ imposes an equation $2b=\text{constant}$ on the translation $b$, giving finitely many choices. Polarizations are discrete, and $[D]=2[H]$ fixes their numerical class; the remaining symmetric theta and level choices are finite. A countable fiber of a finite-type morphism over $\mathbb C$ has dimension zero. Since $\mathcal R_3$ is irreducible of dimension six, the map is dominant on each component of its six-dimensional source. Irreducibility of $\mathcal R_3$ follows from the transitive symplectic monodromy on nonzero two-torsion. The Torelli theorem for curves, with their integral principal polarization <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Huybrechts](#ref-Huybrechts), Chapter 3, Theorem 2.2], likewise gives zero-dimensional fibers for the Jacobian map from $\mathcal R_3$ to the moduli space $\mathcal A_3[\epsilon\ne0]$ of principally polarized abelian threefolds with nonzero two-torsion. This target is irreducible of dimension six: it is a quotient of connected Siegel space, and the symplectic group is transitive on nonzero vectors modulo two. Hence the Jacobian map is dominant.

Choose a theta characteristic $\kappa$ on a genus-three curve and set $$\Theta_\kappa=
 \{\alpha\in J(C):h^0(C,\kappa\otimes\alpha)>0\}.$$ The Riemann theta theorem identifies $\Theta_\kappa$ with a symmetric theta divisor; see <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[MumfordTheta](#ref-MumfordTheta), Chapter II, Section 3, Corollary 3.6 and Theorem 3.10]. An all-even member of $|K_C\otimes\epsilon|$ exists precisely when some $\alpha$ with $2\alpha=\epsilon$ lies on $\Theta_\kappa$: an effective divisor $E$ with $2E\in|K_C\otimes\epsilon|$ corresponds to $\alpha=\mathcal O_C(E)\kappa^{-1}$. This condition is independent of $\kappa$: translating a symmetric theta divisor by two-torsion permutes the set of all sixty-four halves. The condition is closed on $\mathcal A_3[\epsilon\ne0]$: after a finite theta-level cover, the theta divisor and the finitely many half sections are defined, and incidence with each section is closed. Their finite union descends to a closed subset.

It is a proper condition. On a product of three elliptic curves, choose $\epsilon$ nonzero on every factor. Every half then has coordinates of order four, whereas a symmetric product theta divisor is a union of coordinate divisors at two-torsion points. No half lies on that divisor. The resulting nonempty open in the moduli of principally polarized threefolds meets the dominant Jacobian locus, and hence pulls back to a nonempty open in our parameter space. This proves the assertion about fibers. The order-four choice $\chi$ on $B$ is independent of $D$, $C$, and $\epsilon$. Every connected component of its finite étale torsion/level cover surjects onto the connected base, so the same open remains nonempty in each component.

Finally, a translation preserving $D$ belongs to the finite kernel $K(M^2)=B[2]$. Each nonzero element of that kernel acts nontrivially on $\mathbf PH^0(B,M^2)$: otherwise its commutator with all theta-group elements would be trivial, contrary to the nondegenerate theta-group pairing <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BirkenhakeLange](#ref-BirkenhakeLange)]. Its fixed locus is proper. Avoiding the union of these finitely many loci gives a nonempty open on each section fiber. Its intersection with the preceding open proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Coherence, parity, and signed relations

Fix data in the open of Lemma <a href="#geom:gauss-generic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:gauss-generic">12</a>. Let $R\subset\mathbf P^1$ be the regular-value open of the Gauss pencil. Locally on $R$ its fibers on $D$ can be written $\{\pm x_1,\ldots,\pm x_4\}$. A *selection* means an unordered subset containing exactly one point of each opposite pair. The selections form a finite étale cover $\mathcal S\to R$ of degree sixteen. If $s$ is a selection containing $p$, write $s[p]$ for the selection obtained by replacing $p$ by $-p$.

This selection cover and its splitting by the parity of sign changes form the combinatorial part of Donagi’s tetragonal construction <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[DonagiPrym](#ref-DonagiPrym), Sections 2.1–2.2 and 2.5]. The argument below constructs compatible half sums in the given abelian surface $B$ and lifts their addition map through separate curve covers to $X$.

<a id="stmt-geom-halves"></a>
<div id="geom:halves" class="lemma">

<a id="geom:coherent"></a>
**Lemma 13**. *There is an algebraic map $h:\mathcal S\to B$ such that $$\label{geom:coherent}
 2h(s)=\sum_{p\in s}p,\qquad h(s[p])=h(s)-p.$$ The selections split globally into two degree-eight covers $\mathcal S_+\sqcup\mathcal S_-$ according to the parity of the number of sign changes. Their smooth projective completions $C_\pm$, which may be disconnected, carry extensions $a_\pm:C_\pm\to B$ of $h$. Wherever its differential is nonzero, either map is tangent to the direction recorded by its parameter in $\mathbf P^1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-halves"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-halves</summary>

*Proof.* For a fixed regular fiber, a coherent assignment is a function $h$ on its sixteen selections satisfying Equation <a href="#geom:coherent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:coherent">[geom:coherent]</a>. Choosing one selection $s_0$ and one half $b$ of its sum determines it uniquely by $$h(s)=b-\sum_{p\in s_0\setminus s}p.$$ Thus coherent assignments form a torsor under the constant group $B[2]$. This torsor is algebraic and finite étale over $R$: start with the cover of pairs $(s,b)$ satisfying $2b=\sum_{p\in s}p$, and identify $(s,b)$ with $(s[p],b-p)$. After étale-locally labeling the four pairs, the flips commute, are involutions, and their equivalence classes have sixteen elements; the resulting finite étale quotient is precisely the assignment torsor. Parallel transport commutes with the constant $B[2]$-action, so its monodromy acts by translations, despite possible permutations of the selections.

Consider a small disc about any omitted value, including infinity if it is omitted. At the central fiber choose one point from each pair of opposite points of $D$, and near each chosen point take the entire cluster specializing to it. Their union is a monodromy-invariant selection on the punctured disc. Its sum extends holomorphically to the disc. Because multiplication by two on $B$ is étale, that sum has a holomorphic half on the simply connected disc. The corresponding coherent assignment is fixed by local monodromy. A translation of a torsor with a fixed point is the identity. Hence the assignment torsor has trivial monodromy about every puncture, extends étale over $\mathbf P^1$, and is trivial. Any of its global sections gives the algebraic map $h$.

<a id="geom:half-derivative"></a>
Two selections have the same parity if they differ by an even number of flips. The two parity classes define a degree-two étale cover of $R$. The invariant selection over each punctured disc fixes its parity class, hence both classes. This cover also extends étale over $\mathbf P^1$ and is trivial. Labeling its two sections gives the global covers $\mathcal S_\pm$. Properness of $B$ extends their maps uniquely to the smooth projective completions. Finally, differentiation of Equation <a href="#geom:coherent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:coherent">[geom:coherent]</a>, using the invariant tangent space of $B$, gives locally $$\label{geom:half-derivative}
 da_\pm=\tfrac12\sum_{i=1}^4\sigma_i\,dx_i,
 \qquad \sigma_i\in\{1,-1\}.$$ All the $dx_i$ lie in the same Gauss direction. Their sum, whenever nonzero, lies in that direction as well. Continuity gives the same conclusion at the omitted values wherever the extended map has nonzero differential. ◻

</details>

<a id="geom:agreed-sum"></a>
The common assignment on both parities gives an exact identity for any two selections over the same regular value: $$\label{geom:agreed-sum}
 h(s)+h(t)=2h(s)-\sum_{p\in s\setminus t}p
          =\sum_{p\in s\cap t}p.$$ Here we used Equation <a href="#geom:coherent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:coherent">[geom:coherent]</a>. In particular, $h(s^c)=-h(s)$ for the opposite selection $s^c$. Changing the common assignment by $b_0\in B[2]$ changes the left side of Equation <a href="#geom:agreed-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:agreed-sum">[geom:agreed-sum]</a> by $2b_0=0$, so the identity is independent of that choice.

<a id="stmt-geom-relations"></a>
<div id="geom:relations" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *On a regular-value disc, no signed sum of a nonempty subset of the four local branches $x_i$ is constant. Every connected component of $C_+$ and $C_-$ maps nonconstantly to $B$. No image component from $C_+$ is an opposite translate of an image component from $C_-$; that is, their image curves cannot satisfy $Z_+=b-Z_-$ for any $b\in B$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-relations"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-relations</summary>

*Proof.* A one-term relation is impossible because a branch of $D$ is nonconstant. A two-term relation identifies open subsets of $D$ and a translate of $D$ or $-D$. Since $D=-D$, triviality of its translation stabilizer forces the translate to be zero; this would identify two of the distinct opposite pairs, also impossible.

Suppose there is a three-term relation. The monodromy on the four unsigned pairs is transitive, since $C$ is connected. Analytic continuation therefore supplies four constant relations, one omitting each index. Their coefficient matrix has zero diagonal and entries $\pm1$ off the diagonal. Let $J$ denote the all-ones matrix. Modulo two the coefficient matrix is $J-I$ in dimension four, whose square is the identity over $\mathbb F_2$. Its determinant is therefore odd, in particular nonzero. The corresponding homomorphism $B^4\to B^4$ is an isogeny. The four moving branches lie in a fixed finite fiber of this isogeny and must all be constant, a contradiction.

Two four-term relations whose sign vectors are neither equal nor opposite differ in between one and three positions. Subtracting them makes twice a nonempty proper signed partial sum constant. That partial sum is itself locally constant, since $[2]$ has finite fibers, contrary to the cases just proved. Thus a four-term relation, if it exists, determines its sign selection uniquely up to complement. Monodromy must preserve that selection or exchange it with its complement. Every local monodromy has a fixed signed point: a partition of four which is not all-even is one of $1+1+1+1$, $2+1+1$, or $3+1$, and there is a singleton in each case. The étale cover $D\to C$ gives two fixed signed points above that singleton. The selection therefore cannot be exchanged with its opposite. The local monodromies generate the full monodromy, so the four chosen signed points form a global invariant subset of the eight sheets of $D\to\mathbf P^1$. This contradicts connectedness of $D$.

A constant component of $a_\pm$ would give a constant four-term relation by Equation <a href="#geom:coherent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:coherent">[geom:coherent]</a>. Suppose image components from opposite parities satisfy $Z_+=b-Z_-$. Choose a general smooth point $z_+\in Z_+$ and its partner $z_-=b-z_+\in Z_-$, and lift them along local inverse branches of $a_+$ and $a_-$ away from ramification and the exceptional Gauss parameters. The tangent directions at these paired image points agree. Lemma <a href="#geom:halves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:halves">13</a> identifies both directions with their Gauss parameters, so the corresponding local selections $s_+,s_-$ lie over the same parameter and satisfy $h(s_+)+h(s_-)=b$. Opposite parities have either one or three agreed signed points. Equation <a href="#geom:agreed-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:agreed-sum">[geom:agreed-sum]</a> then gives one of the excluded partial relations. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The degree-eight addition map

<a id="stmt-geom-domination"></a>
<div id="geom:domination" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15**. *For every parameter in a nonempty Zariski-open subset of the parameter space, there exist smooth projective connected curves $C_1,C_2$ and a dominant generically finite morphism $$g:C_1\times C_2\longrightarrow X.$$ Whenever additionally $U_X$ has no nonzero rational $(1,1)$-class, an algebraic correspondence induces an injection $$U_X\hookrightarrow H^1(C_1,\mathbb Q)\otimes H^1(C_2,\mathbb Q).$$ In particular, this injection exists at a very general parameter.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-domination"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-domination</summary>

*Proof.* Take the open of Lemma <a href="#geom:gauss-generic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:gauss-generic">12</a> and the common coherent assignment of Lemma <a href="#geom:halves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:halves">13</a>.

<a id="geom:image-cycle"></a>
*Image classes and finiteness.* For either parity, its eight sign vectors satisfy $$\sum_\sigma\sigma_i\sigma_j=8\delta_{ij}.$$ If $\omega$ is an invariant real two-form on $B$, Equation <a href="#geom:half-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:half-derivative">[geom:half-derivative]</a> therefore gives, over a regular-value disc, $$\sum_{s\in\mathcal S_\pm}h(s)^*\omega
       =2\sum_{i=1}^4x_i^*\omega.$$ The right side is exactly the sum over the eight branches $\pm x_i$ of $D$, since inversion fixes invariant two-forms. Integrating over the regular-value open, whose omitted points have measure zero, proves $$\label{geom:image-cycle}
 (a_+)_*[C_+]=(a_-)_*[C_-]=[D]
 \quad\text{in }H_2(B,\mathbb R).$$ Invariant forms represent all of $H^2(B,\mathbb R)$, so this equality of cycle classes includes the degrees of all image maps and every connected component of both sources.

<a id="geom:addition-degree"></a>
Consider addition $$F:C_+\times C_-\longrightarrow B,\qquad
 (p,q)\longmapsto a_+(p)+a_-(q).$$ It is finite on every product of connected components. Otherwise a positive-dimensional fiber would contain a curve. Neither projection of that curve could be constant, since each factor map is nonconstant and hence finite onto its image. Both projections would therefore be surjective, forcing the two image curves to be opposite translates, contrary to Lemma <a href="#geom:relations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:relations">14</a>. Properness now gives finiteness. Each component product is two-dimensional and its finite image is all of $B$. Its total degree, summed over the component products, is, by Equation <a href="#geom:image-cycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:image-cycle">[geom:image-cycle]</a>, $$\label{geom:addition-degree}
 \deg F=[D]^2=(2H)^2=8.$$ One can obtain this formula directly by intersecting one image cycle with a general translate of the negative of the other; the intersection multiplicities count the fibers of addition with the image-map degrees.

*The pullback divisor.* Fix a signed point $x$ of a regular Gauss fiber. For each selection $s\in\mathcal S_+$ containing $x$, let $\tau=s^c[-x]$. It differs from $s$ in three signs, so $\tau\in\mathcal S_-$, and Equation <a href="#geom:agreed-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:agreed-sum">[geom:agreed-sum]</a> gives <a id="geom:four-pairs"></a>$$\label{geom:four-pairs}
 h(s)+h(\tau)=x.$$ There are exactly four such selections $s$. The resulting four points of $C_+\times C_-$ are distinct, since their first selections differ.

As $x$ varies locally on $D$, each construction defines a smooth local curve in the source, parametrized by the common regular Gauss parameter and mapped isomorphically to an open subset of $D$. At its points both factor differentials have image in the tangent line of $D$ at $x$, so $dF$ has rank at most one. Its restriction to the constructed local curve is nonzero. Choose target coordinates $(r,s)$ with $D=(s=0)$ and source coordinates $u=r\circ F,v$, with the local curve given by $v=0$. Then $$F(u,v)=(u,G(u,v)),\qquad G(u,0)=0,\qquad G_v(u,0)=0.$$ Thus the multiplicity of $F^*D$ along each constructed local curve is at least two.

<a id="geom:even-divisor"></a>
Write $F^*D=\sum_\alpha e_\alpha E_\alpha$, with $E_\alpha$ running through the prime divisors on all component products. Every $E_\alpha$ dominates $D$, since $F$ is finite. Put $f_\alpha=[\mathbb C(E_\alpha):\mathbb C(D)]$. The valuation degree formula and Equation <a href="#geom:addition-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:addition-degree">[geom:addition-degree]</a> give $$\sum_\alpha e_\alpha f_\alpha=8.$$ Above a general complex point of $D$, the normalization of $E_\alpha$ has $f_\alpha$ distinct local branches. The four branches constructed above already contribute at least $4\cdot2$ to this sum. They exhaust it, and each has multiplicity exactly two. Several of these local branches may belong to the same prime divisor. This proves, on every component product, the Cartier divisor identity $$\label{geom:even-divisor}
 F^*D=2E_0$$ for an effective integral divisor $E_0$; the smooth source makes $E_0$ Cartier.

*Lifting to the double cover.* Choose connected components $C_1^0\subset C_+$ and $C_2^0\subset C_-$. Pull back $\pi:B'\to B$ separately along their maps and choose a connected component of each pullback. This gives finite étale surjective maps $C_i'\to C_i^0$ and lifts $a_i':C_i'\to B'$. Their sum $$F'=a_1'+a_2':C_1'\times C_2'\longrightarrow B'$$ is finite and surjective: its composite with $\pi$ is the finite surjective map obtained from $F$ by the factor covers. Pullback of Equation <a href="#geom:even-divisor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:even-divisor">[geom:even-divisor]</a> gives $F'^*(\pi^*D)=2E$ on the smooth product $S'=C_1'\times C_2'$.

Let $s_E$ be the canonical section of $\mathcal O_{S'}(E)$ and put $$N=F'^*L\otimes\mathcal O_{S'}(-E),\qquad
 u=F'^*(\pi^*d)/s_E^2\in H^0(S',N^2).$$ The section $u$ is everywhere nonzero. It defines an étale double cover $$\widetilde S'=\operatorname{Spec}_{S'}
        (\mathcal O_{S'}\oplus N^{-1})\longrightarrow S'.$$ This is the normalization of $S'\times_{B'}X$ and has an everywhere-defined morphism to that fiber product. Explicitly, where $E$ has local equation $f=0$, the pulled-back equation of $X$ is $z^2=uf^2$. Its normalization is $y^2=u$, and the morphism back is $z=fy$. This formula also applies at singular points and intersections of $E$ and glues by the definition of $N$.

The étale double cover $\widetilde S'\to S'$ corresponds to a character $$\rho:\pi_1(C_1')\times\pi_1(C_2')\longrightarrow\mathbb Z/2.$$ It is the sum of its two factor restrictions $\rho_1,\rho_2$. Let $C_i\to C_i'$ be the connected finite étale cover corresponding to $\ker\rho_i$, using the identity cover if $\rho_i=0$. These covers are algebraic and projective. The pullback of $\widetilde S'$ to $C_1\times C_2$ is trivial, so choosing either section and composing the preceding morphisms gives $g:C_1\times C_2\to X$. Its composite to $B'$ is finite surjective, so its image has dimension two. The surface $X$ is irreducible: its nonempty reduced branch divisor makes its function-field square class nontrivial. Hence $g$ is dominant and generically finite.

*The cohomological correspondence.* The projection formula gives $g_*g^*=(\deg g)\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ on rational cohomology, so $g^*$ is injective. Suppose $U_X$ has no nonzero rational $(1,1)$-class. Then it has no nonzero Hodge map to $\mathbb Q(-1)$: dualizing such a map with a polarization would give a rational $(1,1)$-class in $U_X$. Thus the two outer projections of $g^*U_X$ in the decomposition $$\begin{split}
 H^2(C_1\times C_2,\mathbb Q)={}&
 \bigl(H^2(C_1)\otimes H^0(C_2)\bigr)
 \oplus\bigl(H^1(C_1)\otimes H^1(C_2)\bigr)\\
 &\oplus\bigl(H^0(C_1)\otimes H^2(C_2)\bigr)
 \end{split}$$ vanish. Choose $p_i\in C_i$ and the algebraic projectors $$\pi_1^{C_i}=\Delta_{C_i}-[p_i\times C_i]-[C_i\times p_i].$$ With $e$ from Equation <a href="#geom:projector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:projector">[geom:projector]</a>, the correspondence $$\bigl(\pi_1^{C_1}\boxtimes\pi_1^{C_2}\bigr)
       \circ{}^t\Gamma_g\circ e
 \in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(X\times C_1\times C_2)_\mathbb Q$$ induces the asserted injection on the whole rational space $U_X$. Proposition <a href="#geom:period" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:period">11</a> supplies the additional Hodge hypothesis at a very general parameter. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Successive generic choices

We call a set residual if its complement is a countable union of closed nowhere-dense subsets. In the connected coordinate domains below, every residual set is dense by Baire’s theorem.

<a id="stmt-geom-genericity"></a>
<div id="geom:genericity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Ordered construction choices). *Every nonempty regular period chart admits a smaller normalized product chart $A\times Q$ of abelian data and affine section coordinates as in Section <a href="#geom:section-charts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section-charts">[geom:section-charts]</a>, mapping into it and lying in the simultaneous geometric open of Propositions <a href="#geom:period" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:period">11</a> and <a href="#geom:domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:domination">15</a>. For any prescribed countable collection of proper closed analytic period loci in the original chart, the following choices are available in this order:*

1.  *abelian data $a$ in a residual subset of $A$;*

2.  *for each such $a$, an actual section pencil in a residual subset of each prescribed nonempty admissible pencil open meeting $Q$;*

3.  *after that pencil is fixed, all but countably many points of its intersection with $Q$ and with a nonempty algebraic good open.*

*The selected points avoid the prescribed period loci. The geometric requirements hold on the algebraic good open, and the exceptional points for proper marked rational Hodge-tensor conditions on its whole connected smooth part form a countable set. The pencils in <span class="upright">(ii)</span> have the form $d_t=d_0+td_1$ with $\ell(d_0)=1$ and $\ell(d_1)=0$, and their leading section $d_1$ is smooth and avoids two-torsion. The final countable subset may include any further countable exceptions arising after the pencil is fixed; no uniform exceptional list across pencils is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-genericity"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-genericity</summary>

*Proof.* Start with any nonempty regular period chart. The simultaneous geometric open of Propositions <a href="#geom:period" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:period">11</a> and <a href="#geom:domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:domination">15</a> is dense, so choose a datum $(a_*,[d_*])$ in its intersection with this chart. In the same section fiber, choose an independent section $d_\infty$ whose divisor is smooth and avoids two-torsion. Trivialize the section bundle, choose $\ell(d_*)=1$ and $\ell(d_\infty)=0$, extend these choices in a local frame, and shrink the abelian base. Smoothness and avoidance of two-torsion are open conditions, so $\mathbf P(\ker\ell)$ contains a good section over every point of this base.

Use the normalization $\ell(d)=1$ of Section <a href="#geom:section-charts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section-charts">[geom:section-charts]</a> and choose a product polydisc $A\times Q$ within the original chart and the simultaneous geometric open. Its period map is a local isomorphism. These choices precede any actual pencil. Affine lines in each section chart are the pencils $d_t=d_0+t d_1$ on the original affine $t$-line, with $\ell(d_0)=1$ and $\ell(d_1)=0$. The good directions form a nonempty dense Zariski-open subset of $\mathbf P(\ker\ell)$. Since the direction map on lines meeting $Q$ is a surjective submersion, pencils with a smooth leading section avoiding two-torsion form a dense open in every nonempty admissible pencil open. Every pencil meeting $Q$ lies in the geometric open at all but finitely many parameters. Its period map is nonconstant, since the period differential is injective on section directions.

Pull back the prescribed analytic conditions to $H_m\subset A\times Q$. For each $m$, the set $$A_m=\{a\in A:\{a\}\times Q\subset H_m\}$$ is closed: it is the intersection, over $q\in Q$, of the closed sets $\{a:(a,q)\in H_m\}$. It has empty interior, since otherwise $H_m$ would contain an open product and hence the connected chart by the identity theorem. The complement of $\bigcup_m A_m$ is therefore residual in $A$. For every $a$ in this complement, each $H_{m,a}$ is a proper closed analytic subset of $Q$.

Fix such $a$. In the open space of affine lines meeting $Q$, put $$\mathcal L_{m,a}=\{\Lambda:\Lambda\cap Q\subset H_{m,a}\},$$ where $\Lambda$ denotes an affine line. This set is closed: every point of a limiting line inside $Q$ is a limit of points on the approximating lines inside $Q$. It has empty interior, since an open set of lines meeting $Q$ sweeps out an open subset of $Q$; equivalently, evaluation from the incidence space of lines and points is a submersion. Excluding these countably many closed nowhere-dense sets leaves a residual set in every nonempty open of admissible pencils. For any remaining line, $\Lambda\cap Q$ is connected, and each condition restricts to a proper analytic subset of this one-dimensional domain, hence to a discrete and countable set. Their union is countable.

For the exceptional Hodge-tensor conditions on the connected smooth open of a whole pencil, enlarge the initial list to include every proper Hodge locus of a rational tensor in the marked local system. Continue markings on the universal cover. A tensor condition holding identically on any open would, by analytic continuation, hold on the selected pencil disc for one of the marked rational tensors already excluded there. Thus every such condition has discrete zeros on marked pencil charts. There are only countably many rational tensors, continuation choices, and charts, so only countably many exceptional pencil parameters.

Intersect the residual pencil choices with the dense open of good directions. The geometric bad parameters are finite, while the local analytic exceptions and the continued Hodge-tensor exceptions are countable. Any further pencil-specific countable set can be added after fixing that pencil. This proves the stated order and conclusions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pencil:section"></a>
### Algebraic K3 pencils with controlled compactifications

We keep the notation of Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">9</a>. The integral lattice $V$ has signature $(2,14)$, whereas its rank-eight rational summand $U$ has signature $(2,6)$. It is useful to embed the larger lattice: the local vanishing cycles below are roots in $V$, although their projections to $U$ need not be roots in an integral K3 lattice. We first realize the periods by polarized K3 surfaces with rational double points. Their canonical contractions retain enough descent data to work over the original parameter curve. A smooth K3 family is obtained on a smaller open of that curve, and its quadratic form on $U$ is the form inherited from $Y$.

<a id="stmt-pencil-realization"></a>
<div id="pencil:realization" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (K3 realization of a section pencil). *Choose the abelian data and the pencil $d_t=d_0+t d_1$ in the generic sets of Lemma <a href="#geom:genericity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:genericity">16</a>. There is a nonempty algebraic open $O\subset\mathbf A^1_t$ and a smooth projective family of K3 surfaces $S^0\to O$ with a flat isometric embedding of rational Hodge variations $$U\ \hookrightarrow\ R^2(S^0/O)_*\mathbb Q.$$ The form on $U$ here is its cup-product form inherited from $Y$. For very general $t$, this summand is the transcendental part of $H^2(S^0_t,\mathbb Q)$. For every $s$ in a nonempty Zariski-open subset of $\mathbf A^1_s$, every smooth projective model of the total family obtained by $t=s+u^2$ is uniruled. The K3 family over $O$ is defined over the original $t$-line; an additional unspecified finite base change is not part of the conclusion.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The lattice extension

In this section $M$ denotes the lattice enlargement, and $\ell(A)$ denotes the minimum number of generators of a finite abelian group $A$.

<a id="stmt-pencil-lattice"></a>
<div id="pencil:lattice" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Enlargement of the lattice). *Let $V$ be an even lattice of signature $(2,14)$ with elementary $2$-primary discriminant group. Suppose a group $G\subset O(V)$ acts trivially on that group. There are an even finite-index overlattice $M$ of $V$, a primitive embedding $M\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$, and a primitive vector $h\in M^\perp$ with $(h,h)>0$, such that the action of $G$ extends to $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$ and is the identity on $M^\perp$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-lattice"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-lattice</summary>

*Proof.* Put $A_V=V^*/V$, and denote its discriminant quadratic and bilinear forms by $q:A_V\to\mathbb Q/2\mathbb Z$ and $b:A_V\times A_V\to\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$. On $A_V\simeq\mathbf F_2^n$, the form $\beta=2b$, valued in $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$, is nondegenerate and symmetric. Let $E=\ker(x\mapsto\beta(x,x))$, so $\dim E\geq n-1$. The restriction of $\beta$ to $E$ is alternating. The inverse image in $E$ of a Lagrangian in its symplectic quotient by the radical is a totally isotropic subspace of dimension $$\frac{\dim E+\dim\operatorname{rad}(\beta|_E)}2
 \ \geq\ \left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor.$$ On this subspace, $b(x,x)=0$ implies that $q(x)$ is integral modulo $2\mathbb Z$; moreover $q(x+y)-q(x)-q(y)=2b(x,y)=0$ modulo $2\mathbb Z$. Thus $q$ restricts to a linear functional with values in $\mathbf F_2$. If $n\geq5$, the subspace has dimension at least two, and this functional has a nonzero isotropic vector.

Adjoin the corresponding isotropic element to the lattice. The new lattice is even and has discriminant form $H^\perp/H$, where $H$ is the order-two subgroup generated by that vector. This reduces the discriminant length by two and leaves the rational quadratic space unchanged. Repetition gives an even overlattice $M$ with $\ell(A_M)\leq4$. Each enlargement is $G$-stable, since $G$ acts trivially on the preceding discriminant group; its action on the new discriminant group is again trivial.

The sufficient primitive-embedding criterion of Nikulin <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Huybrechts](#ref-Huybrechts), Chapter 14, Theorem 1.12] applies, since $$3>2,\qquad 19>14,\qquad
  22-16=6\geq\ell(A_M)+2.$$ Choose such an embedding. Its complement has signature $(1,5)$, and therefore contains a primitive positive integral vector $h$. The complement is primitive in the unimodular K3 lattice, so $h$ is also primitive in $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$. Unimodular gluing identifies the discriminant forms of $M$ and $M^\perp$ with opposite signs. Consequently an isometry of $M$ trivial on $A_M$, together with the identity on $M^\perp$, extends uniquely to $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$. These extensions respect composition.

In particular, a root $r\in V$, with $r^2=-2$, remains an integral root throughout the construction. Its reflection is $$s_r(x)=x+(x,r)r.$$ It is integral on every even overlattice under consideration and on $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$, and is the identity on $M^\perp$. Whenever a monodromy operator on $V$ is a product of these reflections, its extension is the same product of K3 root reflections. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Local monodromy of the section pencil

We analyze the normalized original-line pencil $d_t=d_0+t d_1$ already chosen in Proposition <a href="#pencil:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:realization">17</a> through Lemma <a href="#geom:genericity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:genericity">16</a>. Its abelian data $(B,H,\chi)$ are fixed. The leading member $d_1$ is smooth and avoids two-torsion. The corresponding variations $V$ and $U$ are those of Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">9</a>. The next lemma distinguishes the finite root monodromies from the sign at infinity; the substitution $t=s+u^2$ will kill the latter.

<a id="stmt-pencil-monodromy"></a>
<div id="pencil:monodromy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Local monodromy of the section pencil). *At every finite singular parameter of this general pencil, the monodromy on $V$ is a product of reflections in mutually orthogonal integral $(-2)$-roots in $V$. These roots are perpendicular to the limiting Hodge line. The periods extend to interior periods after killing these finite monodromies. At infinity the monodromy on $V$ is $-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-monodromy"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-monodromy</summary>

*Proof.* *The possible singular members.* The morphism defined by $|2H|$ factors through inversion and realizes the quotient as the classical quartic Kummer surface. A general line in the dual projective space meets its discriminant either at a simple Lefschetz contact in the smooth locus or at a hyperplane through one ordinary Kummer node. Here is the transversality assertion being used. The hyperplanes containing each Kummer node form a plane in the dual space. A general point of such a plane avoids every other node and is transverse to the quadratic tangent cone at that node. For tangencies in the smooth locus, the conormal incidence has generically separable projection to the dual variety. If its image has dimension less than two, a general line avoids it. If it has dimension two, its general smooth point represents a unique contact, because that contact point is recovered as the tangent hyperplane to the dual variety. Separability makes the quadratic contact nondegenerate. The smooth-contact dual component cannot be a node plane, by biduality. The higher contacts, intersections of these discriminant components, and their singular loci have codimension at least two in the dual projective space. A general line avoids them and meets each remaining component transversely. Upstairs on $B$, the first event gives two ordinary nodes exchanged by inversion. At a two-torsion point in the second event, the invariant local equation has a nondegenerate quadratic term and its constant term vanishes to first order in $t$. Thus the branch has an ordinary node with transverse smoothing. The base points of a general pencil are smooth on every member, since the differentials of $d_0$ and $d_1$ there are independent. Pulling back along the isogeny to $B'$ gives disjoint copies of these events. We examine their inversion quotients.

*An exchanged pair.* At an exchanged pair away from the fixed points, the branch curve on $K=\mathop{\mathrm{Km}}(B')$ acquires one ordinary node. The local double surface is $z^2=xy+t$. Its vanishing sphere has square $-2$, and the deck involution $\delta$ negates it. It therefore gives an integral root of $V$ and its Picard–Lefschetz reflection.

*A positive-sign fixed point.* At a fixed point with nonbranch exceptional curve on $K$, choose coordinates in which the upstairs model is $$z^2=x^2+y^2+t,\qquad
 j(x,y,z)=(-x,-y,z).$$ These coordinates can be chosen equivariantly: the branch equation is even in $(x,y)$, and the holomorphic Morse lemma, applied to its nondegenerate even quadratic-coefficient matrix, preserves that parity. The parameter is replaced locally by its constant term, which is a coordinate because the discriminant is met transversely. On the chart $a=x^2,\ v=y/x$ of the resolved base quotient, the double surface has equation $$z^2=a(1+v^2)+t.$$ At $t=0$, its branch is the exceptional curve $a=0$ together with a residual curve meeting it at $v=i,-i$. At each crossing the equation is $z^2=ab+t$. There are consequently two disjoint ordinary node smoothings, and two orthogonal integral anti-invariant roots in $V$. The local monodromy is their product. The difference of the two lifts of the nonbranch exceptional curve, of square $-4$, need not be fixed by this monodromy; it is not being used as one of the individual roots.

*A negative-sign fixed point.* At a fixed point with branch exceptional curve, the involution in the upstairs quadratic model is instead $$j(x,y,z)=(-x,-y,-z).$$ It acts antipodally on the real vanishing sphere $S^2$, and hence by $-1$ on its degree-two class. There is no vanishing class in the $j$-invariant quotient cohomology. To check the resolution contribution, for $t\ne0$ the points $(0,0,\pm\sqrt t)$ are exchanged by $j$. Their quotient is one smooth point. On the chart represented near one chosen square root, the induced sheet involution is $(x,y)\mapsto(-x,-y)$. Blowing up this point therefore gives a $\mathbf P^1$ fixed pointwise by $\delta$; its cohomology class is $\delta$-invariant. More explicitly, on the Kummer-resolution chart $a=x^2,\ v=y/x$, put $\eta=xz$. The finite double-cover model is $$\eta^2=a\bigl(a(1+v^2)+t\bigr),\qquad
 \delta(\eta)=-\eta.$$ For $t\ne0$, the preimage of $a=0$ is that ramification curve; its square is $-1$, because the base exceptional curve has square $-2$. Its class is the only added resolution class at this point. Thus this event contributes no negative eigenspace to $V$.

*The full monodromy operator.* All the displayed roots are integral classes on the nearby smooth $Y$. They are mutually orthogonal because their Milnor neighborhoods are disjoint, and the local monodromy negates each of them. To see that there is no further monodromy, use the orthogonal decomposition of variations in Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">9</a>, $$V_\mathbb Q=U\oplus\langle a_1,\ldots,a_8\rangle_\mathbb Q,
 \qquad U\simeq U_X.$$ On $X$, the complement of the disjoint node neighborhoods is locally topologically trivial, so Picard–Lefschetz gives the product of the corresponding commuting reflections. An exchanged pair has a one-dimensional $j$-invariant vanishing space. At a positive fixed point, $j$ has degree $+1$ on the vanishing sphere and contributes one dimension to $U_X$. At a negative fixed point it has degree $-1$ and contributes none. On the difference lines $\mathbb Qa_i$, the two labels are exchanged by $\sqrt t\mapsto-\sqrt t$ exactly at the affected positive fixed points; all other difference lines are fixed. Consequently the full operator on $V_\mathbb Q$ is an involution whose negative eigenspace has one dimension per exchanged pair and two per positive fixed point. This is precisely the number of mutually orthogonal roots found above. Those roots span that negative eigenspace, proving that the entire operator is their product of reflections. The same argument includes all disjoint lifts of a single event through the isogeny.

*The limiting periods.* After a quadratic cover at each ordinary node, the upstairs surface family has a simultaneous local analytic resolution. In the $A_1$ case used here, write $xy=(z-v)(z+v)$ and blow up the non-Cartier ideal $(x,z-v)$. On the chart $z-v=xa$, the equations are $z=v+xa$ and $y=a(2v+xa)$, so $(x,a,v)$ are smooth coordinates. On the other chart $x=(z-v)b$, they are $z=by-v$ and $x=b(by-2v)$, with smooth coordinates $(b,y,v)$. Both projections to $v$ are smooth and their central fibers resolve the node. The relative two-form $dx\wedge dz/x$ becomes $dx\wedge da$ on the first chart, while the equal expression $-dy\wedge dz/y$ becomes $db\wedge dy$ on the second. Thus the resolution is crepant. These are the local $A_1$ charts of the classical simultaneous-resolution constructions <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Brieskorn](#ref-Brieskorn), [ArtinResolution](#ref-ArtinResolution)]. They glue to the unchanged locus because the exceptional neighborhoods are disjoint.

Choose a nonzero $\theta_+\in H^0(B',L)^{j=+1}$. Using an invariant volume form on $B'$, the Hodge line of $U_X$ has frame $$\frac{\theta_+\,dx\wedge dy}{z}.$$ Crepantness extends it regularly after the local base change and resolution. It remains a nonzero global form on the resolved central surface: test at a point of $B'$ outside the branch divisor and the zero divisor of $\theta_+$. At a minus-sign node its coefficient is odd and vanishes at that node, but this does not make the global Hodge-line frame vanish over the parameter.

The chosen simultaneous resolution need not carry the involutions. Their flat actions on cohomology nevertheless extend after the base change, preserve the limiting Hodge filtration by continuity, and retain the prescribed eigenspaces. The nonzero limiting holomorphic form on the projective resolved central surface has positive norm. Thus $U_X$, and hence $U$, has an interior limiting period. The section just used was single-valued before the local base change, so inertia fixes its limiting vector. In particular the negative eigenspace of local monodromy is perpendicular to that vector. The additional exceptional-difference classes remain algebraic, giving the same conclusion for the roots in $V$.

*Infinity.* Near infinity write $q_\infty=t^{-1}$. The equation is $z^2=q_\infty^{-1}(d_1+q_\infty d_0)$. The leading member $d_1$ is smooth and avoids the fixed points. After adjoining $\sqrt{q_\infty}$ and rescaling $z$, the family extends smoothly. The only twist around $q_\infty=0$ is the change of sign of this square root. This is $\delta$, which acts as $-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ on $V$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The period stack and polarized contraction

The monodromies just calculated describe integral period data; they do not yet give an algebraic K3 family on the original curve. To construct that family, we pass through polarized contractions, for which reflections in contracted roots act trivially. A *polarized ADE K3 surface* is a normal surface with rational double points whose minimal resolution is a K3 surface, together with a primitive ample polarization. Here a polarization in a family is a relative Picard class, locally represented by a line bundle. An ADE K3 surface is the canonical polarized contraction of its resolution with the pulled-back big and nef class; this contraction is given by a sufficiently high power of the class. The relative-Picard convention removes scalar automorphisms of a representing line bundle from the moduli problem.

Fix a primitive positive vector $h\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$. Choose a connected component $\mathscr D_h$ of $$\{[\sigma]\in\mathbf P(h^\perp\otimes\mathbb C):
       (\sigma,\sigma)=0,\ (\sigma,\overline\sigma)>0\},$$ and let $\Gamma_h$ be the isometries of $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$ fixing $h$ and preserving this component. Unimodular gluing identifies $\Gamma_h$ with the stable orthogonal group of $h^\perp$ preserving $\mathscr D_h$: restriction acts trivially on the discriminant group, and every such restricted isometry extends by the identity on $\mathbb Zh$. Thus $[\Gamma_h\backslash\mathscr D_h]$ is the integral period stack for primitive degree $(h,h)$ polarizations. This identification is independent of the choice of the primitive vector of that square; the usual transitivity follows from the Eichler criterion for the K3 lattice, which contains two hyperbolic planes; see <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Huybrechts](#ref-Huybrechts), Chapter 14, Corollary 1.10].

<a id="stmt-pencil-periodstack"></a>
<div id="pencil:periodstack" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (The contraction morphism from the period stack). *Let $\mathscr P_h=[\Gamma_h\backslash\mathscr D_h]$, and let $\mathscr M_h$ denote the moduli stack of primitively polarized ADE K3 surfaces of degree $(h,h)$. There is a morphism $$q:\mathscr P_h\longrightarrow\mathscr M_h$$ whose composition with the period map of quasi-polarized smooth K3 surfaces is their canonical polarized contraction. The pullback of the Hodge line of $\mathscr M_h$ is the tautological period line, including its $\Gamma_h$-linearization.*

*For $[\sigma]\in\mathscr D_h$, let $W_\sigma$ be generated by the reflections in the roots $$R_\sigma=\{r\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}:r^2=-2,\ (r,h)=0,\ (r,\sigma)=0\}.$$ The kernel of the homomorphism on stabilizers induced by $q$ is exactly $W_\sigma$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-periodstack"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-periodstack</summary>

*Proof.* The existence and factorization of $q$ are <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[DiLorenzoFringuelliVistoli](#ref-DiLorenzoFringuelliVistoli), Lemma 1.12]. We use the analytic form of that morphism. Its hypotheses are primitive ample polarization classes on ADE K3 surfaces, primitive big and nef classes on smooth K3 surfaces, and the full integral period stack over $\mathbb C$. The stable group and the representable, surjective étale period map of the quasi-polarized stack are described in <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[DiLorenzoFringuelliVistoli](#ref-DiLorenzoFringuelliVistoli), Sections 1.6 and 1.8; proof of Lemma 2.6 for surjectivity]. Taking one component of the period domain with its stabilizer gives the equivalent stack used here. We now prove the two stated compatibilities.

Let $\mathscr K_h$ be the smooth quasi-polarized K3 stack. Over a local étale period chart its universal smooth family contracts to the pullback of the ADE family. The latter is flat with Gorenstein fibers, so its relative dualizing sheaf is invertible. Off the exceptional curves, contraction identifies the relative two-forms. In the total smooth family over this full period chart the exceptional locus has codimension at least two: the curves occur only over root walls. The identification of the two line bundles therefore extends over the exceptional locus. On each fiber it is the usual crepant identification for resolution of rational double points. This gives the claimed comparison of Hodge lines after pullback to $\mathscr K_h$.

For descent, consider $\mathscr K_h\times_{\mathscr P_h}\mathscr K_h$. It is locally smooth over the period domain, and the ample locus is dense in every component. There each period arrow comes from an actual polarized isomorphism by Torelli, so the two induced Hodge-line identifications agree. Equality extends over this reduced relation space. Hence the comparison descends with its entire $\Gamma_h$-linearization, including on the root mirrors.

Fix a root $r\in h^\perp$ and consider its mirror. Its reflection $s_r$ fixes the period vector, so its image under $q$ acts trivially on the Hodge line. At a very general point of the mirror, the resolution has rational Picard space $\mathbb Qh\oplus\mathbb Qr$. The polarized automorphism induced by $q(s_r)$ lifts uniquely to that resolution. It fixes $h$, and preservation of effectivity forces it to fix the effective choice of $r$. It is symplectic. The transcendental Hodge structure is simple, being polarizable of K3 type with no nonzero rational $(1,1)$-classes. A Hodge endomorphism fixing its $(2,0)$-line pointwise is the identity: the kernel of the difference from the identity is a nonzero Hodge substructure. The lifted automorphism thus acts identically on all second cohomology and is the identity by Torelli.

On the whole mirror, $q(s_r)$ and the identity are sections of a separated isomorphism space. Their equality on the dense locus just considered extends everywhere. Since root reflections generate $W_\sigma$, this group is killed even at points with several or persistent contracted roots. This agrees with the generic root-reflection kernel identified in <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[DiLorenzoFringuelliVistoli](#ref-DiLorenzoFringuelliVistoli), Lemma 3.6].

Conversely, an integral Hodge isometry fixing $h$ takes the ample chamber of a chosen resolution to another chamber whose closure contains $h$. The Weyl group generated by the algebraic roots perpendicular to $h$ acts transitively on these chambers: only walls through $h$ separate chambers incident to $h$. After composition with an element of $W_\sigma$, strong Torelli realizes the isometry by an automorphism of the resolution. By the factorization defining $q$, its image is the induced automorphism of the contraction. If the image is the identity, its lift is the identity on the dense complement of the exceptional curves and hence everywhere. The original isometry then belongs to $W_\sigma$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Descent and the relative canonical class

The use of the integral period stack in Lemma <a href="#pencil:periodstack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:periodstack">20</a> is essential: the local system and its monodromy supply a map to that stack, whereas a map to the coarse period space need not supply a family. The following curve statement also keeps track of ramified descent and of the relative canonical bundle.

<a id="stmt-pencil-descent"></a>
<div id="pencil:descent" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Realization over the original curve). *Let $C$ be a smooth projective complex curve, let $D\subset C$ be finite, and put $C^\circ=C\setminus D$. Suppose the following data are given:*

1.  *a local system $\mathcal L$ of lattices isometric to $\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}$, with a primitive flat section $h$ of positive square;*

2.  *a holomorphic line $\mathcal F\subset\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal O_{C^\circ}$ with $$(\mathcal F,\mathcal F)=0,\qquad
     (\sigma,\overline\sigma)>0\quad(0\ne\sigma\in\mathcal F),\qquad
     (\mathcal F,h)=0;$$*

3.  *finite local monodromy at every point of $D$, and an interior limit of the period line after a local cover killing that monodromy.*

*Then there is a normal projective model $f:\mathcal Z\to C$ whose restriction to $C^\circ$ is a family of polarized ADE K3 surfaces realizing these integral period data modulo contracted roots.*

<a id="pencil:rootcondition"></a>
<a id="pencil:canonical"></a>
*Suppose, in addition, that the local monodromy at each $c\in D$, viewed on the limiting marked lattice, belongs to the Weyl group generated by $$R_c=\{r\in\mathcal L_c:r^2=-2,\ (r,h)=0,\ (r,\mathcal F_c)=0\}.
 \tag{\(*\)}\label{pencil:rootcondition}$$ Then $\mathcal Z\to C$ can be chosen to be a flat family of polarized ADE K3 surfaces on all of $C$. Its extended Hodge line descends to a line bundle $\lambda$ on $C$, and $$\omega_{\mathcal Z/C}\simeq f^*\lambda,
 \qquad K_{\mathcal Z}=f^*(K_C\otimes\lambda).
 \label{pencil:canonical}$$*

*Under hypotheses <span class="upright">(i)</span>–<span class="upright">(iii)</span>, independently of <a href="#pencil:rootcondition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pencil:rootcondition">[pencil:rootcondition]</a>, let $\mathcal U\subset\mathcal L_\mathbb Q$ be a nondegenerate rational sub-local system containing $\mathcal F$, and suppose every flat root which is of type $(1,1)$ on an open set is perpendicular to $\mathcal U$. After shrinking a nonempty algebraic open in $C^\circ$, there is a smooth projective K3 family $\mathcal S^0$ there and an identification of $\mathcal U$ with an isometric Hodge summand of $R^2\mathcal S^0_*\mathbb Q$. Here a flat root means a locally flat section; it need not be a globally invariant vector. No change of the base curve occurs in this conclusion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-descent</summary>

*Proof.* *The family and its algebraization.* The torsor of markings of $(\mathcal L,h)$, with a component chosen by $\mathcal F$, and the equivariant period map define a holomorphic map $C^\circ\to\mathscr P_h$. Its monodromy lies in $\Gamma_h$: it fixes $h$, has trivial restricted discriminant action by unimodular gluing, and preserves the period component by continuity. Pullback along $q$ in Lemma <a href="#pencil:periodstack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:periodstack">20</a> gives an actual analytic family of polarized ADE K3 surfaces on $C^\circ$, with its compatible descent data.

Choose a finite Galois cover $\widetilde C\to C$ whose ramification indices at the points above $D$ are divisible by the local monodromy orders. Such a cover exists: choose a common multiple $N$ of these orders, use Riemann–Roch with sufficiently large poles away from $D$ to prescribe a simple zero at each point of $D$, and normalize the cyclic cover obtained by adjoining an $N$-th root of that function. Additional branch points lie over points where the original family is already defined. If $D$ is empty the identity cover suffices. The pulled-back local system and period extend across the punctures: in a flat trivialization the latter is the holomorphic interior extension in hypothesis <span class="upright">(iii)</span>. They define a map $\widetilde C\to\mathscr P_h$, and hence a proper analytic family $\widetilde{\mathcal Z}\to\widetilde C$ of polarized ADE K3 surfaces. The original monodromy data equip it, functorially through $q$, with an action of the Galois group. All identifications and cocycles are provided by this stack morphism; no choices of smooth simultaneous resolutions have to glue.

This compact analytic family is algebraic and normal. For normality, use flatness and the normal ADE fibers over the smooth base. To check the projectivity needed for GAGA, represent its relative polarizing class by line bundles on small open sets of the base. A common sufficiently divisible power is relatively ample. On an overlap, the ratio of two such representatives is trivial on each fiber. Its direct image is a line bundle, and evaluation is an isomorphism, by cohomology and base change and $H^1(\mathcal O)=0$ on the fibers. Thus the ratio comes from the base. After local trivializations, the remaining gluing obstruction is a scalar cocycle in $H^2(\widetilde C,\mathcal O_{\widetilde C}^*)$. The exponential sequence makes this group zero, since $$H^2(\widetilde C,\mathcal O_{\widetilde C})=0,
 \qquad H^3(\widetilde C,\mathbb Z)=0.$$ The polarizing power consequently glues to a relatively ample line bundle. For a sufficiently high power its direct image is a vector bundle, and the family embeds into the associated projective bundle on $\widetilde C$. That vector bundle and the closed embedded family are algebraic by GAGA <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[SerreGAGA](#ref-SerreGAGA), Theorems 2–3]. More explicitly, first algebraize the vector bundle on the projective curve, then algebraize the coherent ideal of the family in its projective bundle. The finite group action algebraizes by the same fully faithful comparison. Twist the relative ample bundle by a sufficiently positive base bundle to make it ample on the total space. The tensor product of its translates by the Galois group is ample and has its natural linearization; invariant sections of a sufficiently high power give a projective finite quotient. That quotient is a normal projective model $\mathcal Z\to C$. Over $C^\circ$ its construction is descent of the pullback of the initial family, also at any auxiliary branch points. This proves the first assertion, with no cover remaining in its conclusion.

*Descent at a Weyl puncture.* Assume <a href="#pencil:rootcondition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pencil:rootcondition">[pencil:rootcondition]</a>. On a disc killing the monodromy, write the map to the original disc as $t=w^m$. The cyclic inertia group acts on the central polarized surface via the homomorphism on stabilizers induced by $q$. Its image is the identity by Lemma <a href="#pencil:periodstack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:periodstack">20</a>. Thus the group acts pointwise on the whole central surface as the identity, including its singular points.

Here is the corresponding scheme-theoretic descent calculation. Let $A$ be a local completed ring of $\widetilde{\mathcal Z}$ along that fiber. Flatness makes $w$ a nonzerodivisor. If $g$ generates inertia, then $g(w)=\zeta w$, with $\zeta$ primitive of order $m$, and $g$ is the identity on $A/(w)$. Set $B=A^{\langle g\rangle}$. Reynolds averaging makes $B\to A/(w)$ surjective. If $a=wb$ is invariant, then $g(b)=\zeta^{-1}b$. For $m>1$, reduction modulo $w$ forces $b$ to be divisible by $w$. At each subsequent division the character changes by $\zeta^{-1}$; until the $m$-th division it is nontrivial and the reduction must again vanish. This gives, also trivially when $m=1$, $$B\cap wA=w^mB,\qquad B/(t)\simeq A/(w).$$ There is also an exact base-change statement. Write $A_j=\{a:g(a)=\zeta^j a\}$, for $0\leq j<m$. Successive division by $w$ gives $A_j=w^jB$; character projection and the fact that $w$ is a nonzerodivisor then give $$A=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{m-1}w^jB,
 \qquad
 B\otimes_{\mathbb C[[t]]}\mathbb C[[w]]\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }A,
 \quad t\longmapsto w^m.$$ These assertions pass from completed rings to the local rings by faithful flatness of completion. Thus the quotient central fiber is exactly the original reduced ADE surface, and the upstairs family is its flat pullback. The quotient is flat over the parameter disc, since $t$ is a nonzerodivisor in $B$, a torsion-free module over the discrete valuation ring of the disc. This proves the assertion about all fibers and rules out a multiple central fiber.

The underlying ADE family has now descended. To retain its primitive polarization, choose on a smaller invariant analytic $w$-disc a line bundle $L$ representing its relative Picard class; the preceding scalar-cocycle argument applies on the disc as well. The same base-change argument used above shows that $g^*L\otimes L^{-1}$ comes from the disc and that automorphisms of $L$ are base units; the base line is trivial after shrinking. For a $g$-semilinear lift $T$, write $T^m=c\,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$, where commutation with $T$ makes $c=c(t)$ an invariant base unit. Replacing $T$ by $aT$ replaces $c$ by $c\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}g^i(a)$. Every unit in $\mathbb C\{t\}$ has an $m$-th root after shrinking, whose norm as an invariant upstairs unit is its $m$-th power. Taking such a root of $c^{-1}$ therefore arranges $T^m=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$. On the proper connected reduced central fiber, $T$ is one scalar $\chi\in\mu_m$. Replacing $T$ by $\chi^{-1}T$ makes it the identity on $L/(w)L$, without changing the relative Picard class or the separately prescribed Hodge-line linearization.

At any central point, including an ADE point, work with the actual analytic local rings and let $s$ be a local generator of $L$. Then $s_*:=m^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}T^i(s)$ is invariant and congruent to $s$ modulo $w$, hence is still a generator. The invariant local modules are consequently free of rank one over the invariant local rings and pull back to $L$. Their invariant transition units glue them to an actual invertible sheaf on the analytic quotient. Under $B/(t)\simeq A/(w)$ its central restriction is exactly $L_0$, so the primitive ample class, not a power, descends. These local classes agree with the original one off the punctures and glue on $C$; the preceding scalar-cocycle argument glues their primitive representatives, which GAGA algebraizes on the projective quotient.

The Hodge line must descend with its prescribed character, separately from the scalar adjustment of the polarizing bundle. Its limiting inertia action is trivial: root reflections fix a vector perpendicular to their roots. Averaging a local frame which is nonzero at the center gives an invariant frame with that same central value, and hence a nowhere-vanishing frame on a smaller disc. Consequently the extended Hodge line descends to a line bundle $\lambda$ on $C$.
