By Lemma <a href="#pencil:periodstack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:periodstack">20</a>, it is the Hodge line of the descended family, with precisely the linearization prescribed by $\mathcal L$. No character twist has been discarded.

Every ADE K3 fiber is Gorenstein with trivial dualizing sheaf. A flat family with these Gorenstein fibers over a smooth curve is a Gorenstein morphism, so $\omega_{\mathcal Z/C}$ is invertible. Its direct image is a line bundle and formation commutes with base change, since every fiber has a one-dimensional space of canonical forms. Evaluation is an isomorphism on each fiber and therefore everywhere. The identification of this direct image with $\lambda$ proves <a href="#pencil:canonical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pencil:canonical">[pencil:canonical]</a>. For the subsequent intersection argument, its restriction to codimension one would already suffice.

*A smooth family on an open of the original curve.* This part uses the normal contraction family from the first assertion, not the additional Weyl condition. We resolve its generic fiber over $k=\mathbb C(C)$, then recover $\mathcal U$ by comparing local period lifts through the cohomology of that same contraction. The generic polarized ADE surface has a projective minimal resolution over $k$. One way to see the field assertion is to resolve its rational double points over a finite Galois extension. Every Galois translate is a minimal resolution of the same surface; the unique isomorphism over that surface supplies a descent datum and automatically satisfies its cocycle. A norm of a relatively ample bundle makes this descent projective. The descended resolution is defined over $k$ itself. Spreading the morphism and its projective embedding, and then shrinking an algebraic open of $C^\circ$, gives a smooth projective K3 family $\mathcal S^0\to C_0$ resolving the fixed contraction family. All fibers and exceptional configurations can be assumed to be of the generic type on this open.

Here are the details of the cohomological comparison on $C_0$. For the minimal resolution $\pi:S\to Z$ of a complex ADE K3 surface, let $E_Z\subset H^2(S,\mathbb Q)$ be spanned by its exceptional curves. The rational cohomology sequence for the resolution gives $$\pi^*:H^2(Z,\mathbb Q)\hookrightarrow H^2(S,\mathbb Q),
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{im}}(\pi^*)=E_Z^\perp.$$ Indeed, the exceptional fibers are trees of rational curves, with $H^1=0$, and the restriction map in degree two records intersection with their components. It is onto on their span because the ADE intersection matrix is negative definite. This proves both the injectivity and the stated image. In a resolving family these pullbacks are maps of higher direct-image sheaves. Shrinking $C_0$ once more makes the sheaves local systems, by constructibility for the algebraic proper morphisms. They preserve Hodge structures and the cup-product forms on the indicated orthogonal complements.

Locally lift the marked period map through the étale map $\mathscr K_h\to\mathscr P_h$. Its K3 family has an identification of its contraction with the fixed family obtained from $q$. The exceptional root lattice is locally constant on $C_0$; all its roots are therefore persistent in the sense of the hypothesis, and are perpendicular to $\mathcal U$. The marked copy of $\mathcal U$ in this local lift consequently lies in the image of $H^2$ of the contraction. The displayed pullback description gives a flat, isometric map from it to $R^2\mathcal S^0_*\mathbb Q$. For two such local lifts, compare their minimal resolutions over the identity of their common contraction on each fiber. The resulting marking discrepancy has trivial image under $q$, so belongs to the Weyl group in Lemma <a href="#pencil:periodstack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:periodstack">20</a>. This group fixes $\mathcal U$ pointwise. Thus the maps just constructed agree on overlaps, including their flat structures, and glue over $C_0$. Nondegeneracy of $\mathcal U$ makes its image an orthogonal Hodge summand. This proves the final assertion on the original curve.

For clarity about the shrinking in this argument, the mirrors of the $h$-perpendicular roots are locally finite in $\mathscr D_h$. On a compact set of periods, a root orthogonal to $\langle h,\Re\sigma,\Im\sigma\rangle$ has bounded norm in a fixed positive definite metric, since its square is $-2$. Only finitely many lattice vectors can occur. A finite collection of such local period charts covers the compact finite cover used above. In each chart a nonpersistent mirror has only isolated intersections with the period curve. Local finiteness and compactness therefore leave only finitely many such intersection parameters. Removing their images, as well as the exceptional points from spreading, gives the open used here. The argument uses constant root type there; it requires no uniqueness assertion for simultaneous resolutions across jumps in the root configuration. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pencil-uniruledcriterion"></a>
<div id="pencil:uniruledcriterion" class="corollary">

**Corollary 22** (A canonical-degree criterion). *Under the Weyl condition of Lemma <a href="#pencil:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:descent">21</a>, suppose $\deg(K_C\otimes\lambda)<0$. Every smooth projective model of the function field of $\mathcal Z$ is uniruled.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-uniruledcriterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-uniruledcriterion</summary>

*Proof.* Let $p:\widetilde{\mathcal Z}\to\mathcal Z$ be a smooth projective resolution, and let $A$ be sufficiently ample on $\mathcal Z$. Since $\mathcal Z$ is normal and Gorenstein, the difference $K_{\widetilde{\mathcal Z}}-p^*K_{\mathcal Z}$ is supported on the exceptional divisors. Every such divisor has zero intersection with $(p^*A)^2$: its image has codimension at least two, and the projection formula applies. Geometrically, two general members of $|A|$ have a complete-intersection curve avoiding all these images. Equation <a href="#pencil:canonical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pencil:canonical">[pencil:canonical]</a> consequently gives $$K_{\widetilde{\mathcal Z}}\cdot(p^*A)^2
   =\deg(K_C\otimes\lambda)\,(A|_{\mathcal Z_c})^2<0.$$ Here $\mathcal Z_c$ is any general fiber, and its displayed self-intersection is positive by ampleness. The divisor $p^*A$ is nef, so every effective divisor, and by continuity every pseudo-effective divisor, has nonnegative intersection with $(p^*A)^2$. Thus $K_{\widetilde{\mathcal Z}}$ is not pseudo-effective. The theorem of Boucksom–Demailly–Păun–Peternell <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[BDPP](#ref-BDPP), Corollary 0.3] applies to this smooth projective threefold and gives uniruledness. This is a birational property, proving the assertion for every smooth projective model. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Realization and the Hodge line

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-uniruledcriterion-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-uniruledcriterion</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#pencil:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:realization">17</a>.* *Realization over the $t$-line.* By Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">9</a>, the monodromy of $V$ is trivial on its discriminant group. Apply Lemma <a href="#pencil:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:lattice">18</a>, extending that monodromy to the K3 lattice and choosing its fixed positive class $h$. The lattice embedding preserves the form inherited from $Y$. Give this lattice the period line of $V$, which is also that of $U$. Over $\mathbb Q$ its orthogonal decomposition is $$\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3},\mathbb Q}=U\oplus\langle a_1,\ldots,a_8\rangle_\mathbb Q
                       \oplus M_\mathbb Q^\perp.$$ The last two summands, of dimensions eight and six, are of type $(1,1)$, so the complement of $U$ has dimension fourteen. For the choices specified in Lemma <a href="#geom:genericity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:genericity">16</a>, and a very general parameter, $U$ has no rational $(1,1)$-classes by Proposition <a href="#geom:period" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:period">11</a>. If a locally flat rational root remains algebraic on a nonempty open, its projection to $U$ in the displayed orthogonal decomposition is a rational flat class of type $(1,1)$ there. Analytic continuation keeps it of that type wherever its flat continuation is defined. At a very general point this projection is zero, so it is identically zero. Every persistent root is therefore perpendicular to $U$. Lemmas <a href="#pencil:monodromy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:monodromy">19</a> and <a href="#pencil:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:descent">21</a> now give, over a nonempty open of the original line, the asserted smooth projective K3 family with the specified $U$-variation. Its orthogonal complement has dimension fourteen and is entirely of type $(1,1)$. Lefschetz’s $(1,1)$-Theorem and the absence of rational $(1,1)$-classes in $U$ show that its very general transcendental part is exactly $U$. This determines the rational Hodge summand and its pairing; the integral Néron–Severi lattice of the auxiliary K3 surface is not specified by this construction.

*The quadratic substitution.* Let $\Delta$ be the finite union of the critical values and $\mathbf A^1_t\setminus O$. For every $s\notin\Delta$, make the substitution $t=s+u^2$. Its finite branch value is $t=s$, a point of the smooth open $O$. It is unramified over every finite critical value, so those monodromies remain the products in Lemma <a href="#pencil:monodromy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:monodromy">19</a>. At any other removed finite value the original period data extend, and the local monodromy is the identity. At infinity the ramification index is two and kills the square-root twist. Thus the full extended K3 lattice has trivial monodromy there: the square is the identity on $M$, and the action on $M^\perp$ was already the identity. Every remaining finite monodromy is a product of integral root reflections fixing $h$ and the limiting period vector, by Lemma <a href="#pencil:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:lattice">18</a>. All local monodromies therefore satisfy the Weyl condition in Lemma <a href="#pencil:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:descent">21</a>. Apply that lemma on $\mathbf P^1_u$ to obtain a flat projective ADE K3 family $f:\mathcal Z\to\mathbf P^1_u$ and its Hodge line $\lambda$.

*The Hodge line and uniruledness of the ADE model.* We first prove uniruledness for this model using its Hodge line, whose degree is exactly one. We then identify its function field with the square pullback of the fixed $S^0$-family.

Indeed, the compactified upstairs double cover is defined over $B'\times\mathbf P^1_u$ using $L\boxtimes\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1)$. In homogeneous coordinates $[u_0:u_1]$ its branch section is $(d_0+s d_1)u_0^2+d_1u_1^2$, with pullback to $B'$ understood. On the affine chart $u=u_1/u_0$ its equation is $$z^2=d_0+(s+u^2)d_1.$$ The double-cover formula for the relative dualizing sheaf, with $K_{B'}\simeq\mathcal O_{B'}$, gives the anti-sheet part of the direct image of relative canonical forms as $$H^0(B',L)\otimes\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1).$$ Taking $j=+1$ picks out the fixed one-dimensional eigenspace of $H^0(B',L)$ from Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">9</a>, hence gives $\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1)$. Choose a nonzero $\theta_+\in H^0(B',L)^{j=+1}$. For completeness, with $v=1/u$ and $w=z/u$ the equation becomes $$w^2=d_1+(d_0+s d_1)v^2,$$ which is smooth at infinity. Local relative forms transform as $$\frac{\theta_+\,dx\wedge dy}{z}
     =v\,\frac{\theta_+\,dx\wedge dy}{w},$$ the transition for $\mathcal O(1)$. In particular the displayed frame on the finite chart has a simple zero, measured against the nonzero frame on the infinity chart, at $v=0$. At every finite nodal fiber, Lemma <a href="#pencil:monodromy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:monodromy">19</a> shows that the frame extends after the local resolving cover and is a nonzero global form on the central resolution. There is therefore no additional integral zero or pole at a finite parameter. The limiting inertia acts trivially on that form: its vanishing roots are perpendicular to the limiting period vector. The invariant-frame argument in Lemma <a href="#pencil:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:descent">21</a> consequently introduces no fractional local weight either. The Hodge-line identification in Lemma <a href="#pencil:periodstack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:periodstack">20</a> includes these exact characters, so the period realization transfers this extension to $\lambda$. We obtain $\lambda\simeq\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1)$, as line bundles with the stated local extensions.

The canonical bundle of this normal polarized contraction model is $$f^*(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(-2)\otimes\lambda)
      =f^*\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(-1).$$ Thus $\deg(K_{\mathbf P^1}\otimes\lambda)=-2+1=-1$, and Corollary <a href="#pencil:uniruledcriterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:uniruledcriterion">22</a> shows that every smooth projective model of its function field is uniruled.

*The algebraic square-pullback function field.* We use a common finite cover for this construction and the fixed construction over the $t$-line. Put $K=\mathbb C(t)$, $K_s=\mathbb C(u)$ with $t=s+u^2$, and $\widetilde K=\mathbb C(\widetilde C)$, where $\widetilde C\to\mathbf P^1_t$ is the finite Galois cover chosen in Lemma <a href="#pencil:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:descent">21</a>. Choose the fields in a common algebraic closure of $K$, put $\widetilde K_s=\widetilde K K_s$, and let $\widetilde C_s$ be the normalization of $\mathbf P^1_u$ in $\widetilde K_s$. This is the normalization of an irreducible component of $\widetilde C\times_{\mathbf P^1_t}\mathbf P^1_u$. Since $\widetilde K/K$ is Galois, so is $\widetilde K_s/K_s$; its group $G_s$ is identified by restriction with a subgroup of $G=\operatorname{Gal}(\widetilde K/K)$.

This common cover is admissible in the second application of Lemma <a href="#pencil:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:descent">21</a>. Indeed, at a point where the original cover has ramification index $e$, the substitution has index $d$, and the original local monodromy has order $m\mid e$, normalization of the fiber product gives index $e/\gcd(e,d)$. The pulled-back monodromy has order $m/\gcd(m,d)$, which divides that index. This also covers auxiliary branch points, where $m=1$. Thus all pulled-back local monodromies are killed. We take the extended period map on $\widetilde C_s$ to be the pullback of the previous extended map, with the restricted equivariance data.

Write $\widetilde{\mathcal Z}_t\to\widetilde C$ for the algebraized family in the first construction. Functoriality of the stack morphism $q$ identifies the analytic family on $\widetilde C_s$, together with its $G_s$-action, with the analytification of the cartesian algebraic pullback $$\widetilde{\mathcal Z}_t\times_{\widetilde C}\widetilde C_s
       \longrightarrow \widetilde C_s.$$ This pullback is already flat and projective with ADE K3 fibers. If the new analytic family is algebraized separately as in the lemma, the fully faithful GAGA comparison used there, applied to the projective total spaces over $\widetilde C_s$, algebraizes the displayed analytic identification and its compatible group action. We may therefore use this literal algebraic pullback, with the induced $G_s$-action, in forming the second quotient.

Let $Z_K$ be the generic fiber of the original contraction over $K$. Original descent identifies $\widetilde{\mathcal Z}_{t,\widetilde K}$ with $Z_K\times_K\widetilde K$, including its $G$-descent datum. After extending scalars to $\widetilde K_s$ and restricting to $G_s$, this is exactly the descent datum of $Z_K\times_K K_s$. Uniqueness of effective finite Galois descent therefore identifies the generic fiber of the second quotient with $Z_K\times_K K_s$. The fixed smooth generic K3 surface $S^0_K$ is a resolution of $Z_K$; its base change is still birational to $Z_K\times_K K_s$, since geometric integrality ensures that the base change retains the dense open on which the resolution is an isomorphism. Consequently the second model $\mathcal Z$ has the function field of the actual algebraic square pullback of the fixed $S^0$-family. The uniruledness just proved therefore applies to every smooth projective model of that fixed pulled-back family, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="comp:section"></a>
### An algebraic comparison by two square substitutions

We construct a correspondence between two surface fibers by passing through the third cohomology of uniruled threefolds. The first substitution, $t=s+u^2$, makes degree-three Hodge maps algebraic. The second, $s=b+v^2$, produces a reducible conic whose difference of components recovers degree two with the explicit factor $-4$. Two points require care: the degree-three comparison must apply to the particular Leray class furnished by the conic, and the omitted boundary must have fixed Hodge structures before $b$ varies. We formulate the argument for one fixed pencil, including this order of choices.

<a id="stmt-comp-twosquares"></a>
<div id="comp:twosquares" class="theorem">

**Theorem 23** (Two-substitution comparison). *Let $O\subset\mathbf A^1_t$ be a nonempty Zariski open set, and let $$f:\mathcal S\longrightarrow O,
 \qquad g:\mathcal X\longrightarrow O$$ be smooth projective families of connected surfaces, with the fibers of $f$ K3 surfaces. Suppose that the following conditions hold.*

1.  *A polarized rational subvariation $\mathscr U\subset R^2f_*\mathbb Q$ of K3 type, with nondegenerate restricted cup product, is given. Its period map is nonconstant, and $U_t=\mathscr U_t$ is simple for all but countably many $t\in O$.*

2.  *There are commuting involutions $j,\delta$ of $\mathcal X$ over $O$ such that $$e=\tfrac14(1+j)(1-\delta)$$ annihilates $R^qg_*\mathbb Q$ for $q\ne2$, together with an isomorphism of rational variations of Hodge structure $$\varphi:\mathscr U\xrightarrow{\sim}eR^2g_*\mathbb Q.$$*

3.  *For $s$ in a nonempty Zariski open subset of $\mathbf A^1$, the total spaces of the two surface families pulled back by $t=s+u^2$ have smooth projective uniruled threefold models.*

*Then there are a nonempty Zariski open set $G\subset O$ and a countable set $E\subset G$ such that, for every $b\in G\setminus E$, a correspondence $$\Theta_b\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S_b\times X_b)_\mathbb Q$$ induces $\varphi_b$ on $U_b$. The open set $G$ and all auxiliary correspondences used before the choice of $b$ can be fixed for the given pencil.*

</div>

The isomorphism $\varphi$ in the theorem is the specified map of variations; it need not preserve the two cup-product forms. A cycle on a product acts from its first factor to its second, with the convention of the Introduction. In particular, a codimension-three cylinder from a surface to a fourfold gives $H^2(-)(-1)\to H^4(-)$, and its transpose gives $H^4(-)\to H^2(-)(-1)$. We suppress Tate twists on individual vectors.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Third cohomology of uniruled threefolds

<a id="stmt-comp-uniruled"></a>
<div id="comp:uniruled" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24**. *If $Z$ is a smooth projective uniruled threefold over $\mathbb C$, then $H^3(Z)$ is a direct summand of a finite sum of spaces $H^1(C_i)(-1)$, with both maps of the splitting induced by algebraic correspondences. Consequently, every rational Hodge map between the third cohomologies of two such threefolds is algebraic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-uniruled"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-uniruled</summary>

*Proof.* Uniruledness gives a generically finite dominant rational map $T\times\mathbf P^1\dashrightarrow Z$ for a smooth projective surface $T$. Indeed, choose a two-dimensional parameter space of rational curves dominating $Z$ and, if necessary, make a generically finite change of its parameter surface to trivialize the generic rational curve. Principalize the base ideal of the rational map in the smooth product, using projective blowups with smooth centers <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Wlodarczyk](#ref-Wlodarczyk), Theorem 1.0.1]. This gives a morphism $a:\widetilde{T\times\mathbf P^1}\to Z$. The identity $a_*a^*=(\deg a)\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ gives an algebraic splitting of $H^3(Z)$ from the third cohomology of the source.

The product formula and the blowup formula give $$H^3(T\times\mathbf P^1)=H^3(T)\oplus H^1(T)(-1),$$ and each blowup along a curve adds its $H^1(-)(-1)$; a point adds nothing in degree three. The product projections are algebraic, and the full blowup decompositions are given by algebraic projectors <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[LiBlowup](#ref-LiBlowup), Appendix A, equation (9) and Proposition A.1]; see also <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Fulton](#ref-Fulton), Section 6.7] for the Chow blowup formalism. To see the sign in the only new summand, let $\pi:\widetilde Z\to Z$ be a blowup along a smooth curve $C$, with exceptional divisor $j:E\hookrightarrow\widetilde Z$ and $q:E\to C$. Its inclusion is $j_*q^*$ and its retraction is $-q_*j^*$, since $$q_*j^*j_*q^*y
 =q_*\bigl(c_1(\mathcal O_E(-1))\smile q^*y\bigr)=-y.$$ The other summand has inclusion $\pi^*$ and projection $\pi_*$. These maps and the product projections provide the required algebraic splittings at every step of the resolution.

For completeness, the needed splittings for a surface use only divisor classes. Choose a smooth ample curve $i:C\hookrightarrow T$. Hard Lefschetz says that multiplication by $[C]$ is an isomorphism $H^1(T)(-1)\to H^3(T)$. Since this is $i_*i^*$, restriction $i^*:H^1(T)\to H^1(C)$ is injective and $i_*:H^1(C)(-1)\to H^3(T)$ is surjective. Polarizability gives a Hodge retraction $r$ of $i^*$ and a Hodge section $s$ of $i_*$. Poincaré duality identifies their tensors with rational classes of type $(0,0)$ in, respectively, $$H^1(C)\otimes H^1(T)(1)
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 H^1(T)\otimes H^1(C)(1).$$ The underlying untwisted classes have type $(1,1)$ on $C\times T$ and $T\times C$, so the Lefschetz $(1,1)$ theorem represents them by rational divisors. Their actions on the indicated degrees are $r$ and $s$. Together with restriction and Gysin, they split both surface summands through $H^1(C)(-1)$. Taking products with the point and fundamental classes of $\mathbf P^1$ realizes these splittings in the product decomposition above. Composing with $a^*$ and $(\deg a)^{-1}a_*$ proves the first claim.

Finally, a Hodge map between two finite sums of $H^1(C_i)(-1)$ is given by divisor correspondences on products of the curves. Composing with the splittings gives a cycle in $\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^3(Z\times Z')_\mathbb Q$ with exactly the prescribed action on $H^3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Coefficient projections on affine curves

The degree-three comparison uses the canonical $H^1(R^2)$ term on the affine square-pullback curves. We first fix the coefficient projections on the original curve $O$, so that the same projections remain available after both substitutions. We work in the rational derived category of sheaves on a complex curve; Tate twists record the Hodge structures on the corresponding cohomology groups.

<a id="stmt-comp-affine"></a>
<div id="comp:affine" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25**. *Let $B$ be a smooth affine complex curve.*

1.  *A bounded complex on $B$ with finite-rank local-system cohomology only in even degrees has a unique splitting as the direct sum of its cohomology sheaves, shifted to their degrees, inducing the identity on each cohomology sheaf.*

2.  *If $A$ is a bounded complex with local-system cohomology, then there is a natural exact sequence $$0\longrightarrow H^1(B,\mathcal H^{n-1}A)
     \longrightarrow \mathbb H^n(B,A)
     \longrightarrow H^0(B,\mathcal H^nA)\longrightarrow0.$$ For a morphism of such complexes, its action on the left-hand term is determined by its action on $\mathcal H^{n-1}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-affine"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-affine</summary>

*Proof.* An affine complex curve has the homotopy type of a one-dimensional CW complex. For local systems $L,M$, $$\operatorname{Ext}^r_B(L,M)
   =H^r(B,L^\vee\otimes M)=0\qquad(r>1).$$ For two distinct nonzero even degrees $i<j$, the obstruction to splitting the corresponding truncation triangle lies in an $\operatorname{Ext}^{j-i+1}$ group, and the ambiguity lies in an $\operatorname{Ext}^{j-i}$ group. Their degrees are at least three and two, respectively, so both vanish. Induction proves (i), including uniqueness with the specified action on cohomology. In (ii), the hypercohomology spectral sequence has only columns zero and one; its degeneration and functoriality give the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="comp:k3split"></a>
<a id="comp:xsplit"></a>
For the K3 family of Theorem <a href="#comp:twosquares" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:twosquares">23</a>, this gives a canonical splitting of rational complexes $$\label{comp:k3split}
 Rf_*\mathbb Q\simeq
 \mathbb Q\oplus(R^2f_*\mathbb Q)[-2]\oplus\mathbb Q(-2)[-4].$$ We make this splitting on the original affine curve $O$ and pull it back under subsequent base changes. On the other side, the group-algebra idempotent has a single nonzero cohomology sheaf and therefore gives canonically $$\label{comp:xsplit}
 eRg_*\mathbb Q\simeq (eR^2g_*\mathbb Q)[-2]\simeq\mathscr U[-2].$$ Use the restricted cup product to write $p:R^2f_*\mathbb Q\to\mathscr U$ for the orthogonal projection. Pullback of <a href="#comp:k3split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:k3split">[comp:k3split]</a>, followed by $p$, and <a href="#comp:xsplit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:xsplit">[comp:xsplit]</a>, followed by $\varphi^{-1}$, define coefficient projections after any base change from $O$. These are morphisms of rational sheaf complexes. Their use on a surface base requires neither a new derived splitting there nor algebraicity of the projector $p$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### The geometric Hodge comparison in degree three

The next lemma gives the precise coefficient comparison that we need. It uses a Hodge class on a relative product to construct a Hodge map; algebraicity of that map will then follow from Lemma <a href="#comp:uniruled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:uniruled">24</a>.

<a id="stmt-comp-hodgelift"></a>
<div id="comp:hodgelift" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26**. *Let $I$ be a smooth affine curve, let $h_S:E^S\to I$ be a smooth projective K3 family, and let $h_X:E^X\to I$ be a smooth projective surface family with a relative algebraic idempotent $e$ acting on the direct-image complex whose action on $R^q h_{X*}\mathbb Q$ vanishes for $q\ne2$. Suppose that $$a:R^2h_{S*}\mathbb Q\longrightarrow eR^2h_{X*}\mathbb Q$$ is a morphism of rational Hodge variations. Let $Z^S,Z^X$ be smooth projective compactifications which extend the two maps to a smooth projective compactification of $I$ and are unchanged over $I$. Write $\overline I$ for that compactified curve. There is a rational Hodge map $c:H^3(Z^S)\to H^3(Z^X)$ satisfying $$e\,\operatorname{res}_X(cz)
   =H^1(I,a)\bigl(\operatorname{res}_S z\bigr),
 \qquad z\in H^3(Z^S),$$ where $H^3(E^S)$ and $eH^3(E^X)$ are identified canonically with $H^1(I,R^2h_{S*}\mathbb Q)$ and $H^1(I,eR^2h_{X*}\mathbb Q)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-hodgelift"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-hodgelift</summary>

*Proof.* Put $R=E^S\times_I E^X$, and resolve its closure in $Z^S\times_{\overline I}Z^X$, preserving $R$. This gives a smooth projective fivefold $\overline R$ with proper maps $\pi_S:\overline R\to Z^S$ and $\pi_X:\overline R\to Z^X$. On each fiber, Poincaré duality represents $a$ by the unique tensor in the $H^2(E^S_t)\otimes H^2(E^X_t)$ summand of $H^4(R_t)$ with that correspondence action. The tensors form a flat rational section $\alpha$ of $R^4(R/I)_*\mathbb Q$ and have type $(2,2)$.

The global invariant cycle theorem <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[DeligneHodgeII](#ref-DeligneHodgeII), Theorem 4.1.1] identifies the image of $H^4(\overline R)\to H^4(R_t)$ with the monodromy invariants. Restriction is a morphism of polarizable pure Hodge structures. Its image therefore contains $\alpha_t$, and semisimplicity gives a rational lift $\widetilde\alpha\in H^4(\overline R)$ of type $(2,2)$. For example, split restriction onto its image as a Hodge map and apply the splitting to $\alpha_t$. The restriction of this lift to every fiber is $\alpha$, because both define flat sections. Define $$c(z)=(\pi_X)_*
       \bigl(\widetilde\alpha\smile\pi_S^*z\bigr).$$ Cup product raises degree by four and the proper pushforward lowers degree by four, so this is a rational Hodge map from $H^3$ to $H^3$.

Its restriction to $I$ is the relative cohomological correspondence defined by $\widetilde\alpha|_R$. Pullback, cup product, and integration along the smooth proper relative surface projection give a morphism $Rh_{S*}\mathbb Q\to Rh_{X*}\mathbb Q$; on degree-two cohomology sheaves it is $a$. For the source, $R^3h_{S*}\mathbb Q=0$, and for the projected target, $eR^3h_{X*}\mathbb Q=0$. Lemma <a href="#comp:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:affine">25</a>(ii), with $n=3$, therefore identifies the entire source and projected target $H^3$ with their canonical $H^1(R^2)$ terms. Functoriality shows that the projected open action of $c$ is exactly $H^1(I,a)$, as asserted. The proof uses no claim that $\widetilde\alpha$ is algebraic. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Compatible projective models

The following construction will be used for both surface families. It fixes the first compactifications before the relative correspondence and the point $b$ are chosen.

<a id="stmt-comp-models"></a>
<div id="comp:models" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *For a smooth projective surface family $\mathcal Y\to O$, its first square pullbacks admit models $Z$ projective over $\mathbf A^1_s$ and a nonempty open set $G\subset O$ such that $Z_G\to G$ is smooth. These models may be chosen with the following additional property. For every $b\in G$ there is a smooth projective fourfold $M_b$, with morphisms to $\mathbf P^1_t$ and $\mathbf P^1_v$, whose inverse images of $V_0\subset\mathbf P^1_v$ and $O\subset\mathbf P^1_t$ are, respectively,*

1.  *$Z_G\times_GV_0$, where $V_0\to G$ is $v\mapsto b+v^2$ and $V_0=\{v\in\mathbf A^1:b+v^2\in G\}$;*

2.  *$\mathcal Y\times_OQ_b$, where <a id="comp:conic"></a>$$\label{comp:conic}
     Q_b=\{u^2+v^2=(t-b)w^2\}\subset O\times\mathbf P^2.$$*

*The two identifications agree on their intersection. In particular, both specified spaces are preserved as actual open subvarieties. Shrinking $G$ before choosing $b$ preserves these conclusions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-models"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-models</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a smooth projective compactification $\overline{\mathcal Y}\to\mathbf P^1_t$ whose inverse image over $O$ is $\mathcal Y$. It is obtained by taking a projective closure of the family and resolving away from its smooth open. Take the component containing the given open. Its source is smooth and integral, and its map to the nonsingular curve is flat. The generic fiber is geometrically integral: a smooth geometrically connected surface is geometrically integral. Hence the base changes below have the dense unchanged open and the same function field as the indicated pullback. The map $$C=\mathbf P^1_u\times\mathbf A^1_s\longrightarrow\mathbf P^1_t,
 \qquad t=s+u^2,$$ is a morphism: in homogeneous coordinates on $\mathbf P^1_u$, with $u=U_0/U_1$, it is $[U_0:U_1]\mapsto[U_0^2+sU_1^2:U_1^2]$. Set $W=\overline{\mathcal Y}\times_{\mathbf P^1_t}C$. Choose a projective resolution $Z\to W$ as a sequence of blowups, all supported over $t\notin O$. The flat base change is generically smooth and therefore reduced, with no component supported only over the boundary; its open over $O$ is smooth. Resolution is performed on each component if needed. Strong projective resolution in characteristic zero, preserving the regular locus, permits this construction <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[Wlodarczyk](#ref-Wlodarczyk), Theorems 1.0.2–1.0.3]. The map $Z\to\mathbf A^1_s$ is projective, and is smooth after deleting finitely many values of $s$. Fix this entire construction, and take $G$ inside its smooth locus and inside $O$.

Let $B_b$ be the normalization of $\mathbf P^1_t\times\mathbf P^1_v$ in the field $$\mathbb C(t,v)\bigl(\sqrt{t-b-v^2}\bigr).$$ It is a projective surface finite over the product. Its finite-$v$ open is $\mathbf P^1_u\times\mathbf A^1_v$, with $t=b+u^2+v^2$. Its open over $O$ is $Q_b$: the latter is smooth and the map $$Q_b\longrightarrow O\times\mathbf P^1_v,
 \qquad (t,[u:v:w])\longmapsto(t,[v:w])$$ has no base point: $v=w=0$ would force $u=0$. It is proper and has finite fibers, hence is finite of degree two, with the required function field. The same verification on the finite-$v$ open uses $\mathbf P^1_u\times\mathbf A^1_v$ and the morphism $[U_0:U_1]\mapsto[U_0^2+(b+v^2)U_1^2:U_1^2]$. These assertions identify both opens by the universal property of normalization. Smoothness of $Q_b$ follows in the affine chart $w=1$ from the derivative in $t$, and at infinity from the derivatives in $u,v$.

Start with the projective fourfold $W_b=\overline{\mathcal Y}\times_{\mathbf P^1_t}B_b$. On its finite-$v$ open, pull back the chosen blowup sequence $Z\to W$ by $s=b+v^2$. This map of affine lines is finite flat, so blowup commutes with the base change. Each blowup extends to the whole current projective model without altering its open over $O$. Explicitly, if $j:U\hookrightarrow W_b^{(r)}$ is its finite-$v$ open and $\mathcal I\subset\mathcal O_U$ is the next center ideal, extend it by $$\mathcal J=\ker\bigl(\mathcal O_{W_b^{(r)}}
          \longrightarrow j_*(\mathcal O_U/\mathcal I)\bigr).$$ This is a coherent ideal: the schemes are noetherian, the open immersion is quasi-compact, and the kernel is a quasi-coherent submodule of $\mathcal O_{W_b^{(r)}}$. It restricts to $\mathcal I$ and is the unit ideal off the closed inverse image of $\mathbf P^1_t\setminus O$. Thus its blowup has the asserted properties.

After extending the finite sequence, both opens in the statement are present and smooth. A final projective resolution which is an isomorphism on the regular locus preserves their union. It gives $M_b$ and the two morphisms to the parameter lines. The construction uses one fixed set of discarded $s$-values, independent of $b$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Proof of the comparison theorem

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-models-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-models</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#comp:twosquares" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:twosquares">23</a>.* We retain the notation $\mathscr U$, $p$, and $\varphi$ above.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### *The fixed part.*

We first prove the vanishing that will make each cylinder class restrict to zero on the projective threefold fibers: <a id="comp:noinvariants"></a>$$\label{comp:noinvariants}
 H^0(O,\mathscr U)=0.$$ By the fixed-part theorem, the invariant part of the geometric polarized variation is a constant Hodge subvariation; see <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[DeligneHodgeII](#ref-DeligneHodgeII), Theorem 4.1.1 and Corollary 4.1.2]. A nonzero such part would equal $U_t$ at a simple fiber, and hence everywhere as a local system. The fixed-part theorem would then make its period constant, contrary to the hypothesis.

<a id="comp:noinvariantsI"></a>
For $s\in O$, put $I_s=\{u\in\mathbf A^1:s+u^2\in O\}$. The pullback of $\mathscr U$ to $I_s$ has the same monodromy image as $\mathscr U$ on $O$. To see this, remove the branch value $s$ temporarily. A loop about $s$ changes the sheet of the double cover and has trivial monodromy on $\mathscr U$; it can therefore be used to adjust the parity of any loop without changing its monodromy. Consequently, $$\label{comp:noinvariantsI}
 H^0(I_s,\mathscr U)=0\qquad(s\in O).$$

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### *A relative correspondence in degree three.*

Apply Lemma <a href="#comp:models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:models">27</a> to both families, obtaining models $Z^S\to\mathbf A^1_s$ and $Z^X\to\mathbf A^1_s$. Shrink a common nonempty open $G\subset O$ so that both families $\zeta^k:Z_G^k\to G$ are smooth projective and their fibers are uniruled. Write $E_s^S$ and $E_s^X$ for their unchanged opens over $I_s$. The Leray spectral sequence over the affine curve $I_s$ and the degree-two projections give maps $$\begin{aligned}
 r_s^S &:H^3(Z_s^S)\longrightarrow
 H^3(E_s^S)=H^1(I_s,R^2f_*\mathbb Q)
 \xrightarrow{p}H^1(I_s,\mathscr U),\\
 r_s^X &:H^3(Z_s^X)\longrightarrow
 eH^3(E_s^X)=H^1(I_s,eR^2g_*\mathbb Q)
 \xrightarrow{\varphi^{-1}}H^1(I_s,\mathscr U).
\end{aligned}$$ All coefficients in these expressions have been pulled back to $I_s$.

Here is the Hodge-theoretic content of this comparison. Give $H^1(I_s,\mathscr U)$ the mixed Hodge structure transported from $eH^3(E_s^X)$ by the second identification above. The weight-three part $$T_s=W_3H^1(I_s,\mathscr U)$$ is pure and polarizable. Restriction from a smooth projective compactification has image equal to the lowest weight <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[DeligneHodgeII](#ref-DeligneHodgeII), Corollary 3.2.17]; the algebraic projector on the open commutes with the weight filtration. Thus $r_s^X$ is a surjective Hodge map onto $T_s$. This argument uses $e$ only on $E_s^X$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#comp:hodgelift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:hodgelift">26</a> with $a=\varphi p$, after pullback to $I_s$. It gives a rational Hodge map $$c_s:H^3(Z_s^S)\longrightarrow H^3(Z_s^X),
 \qquad r_s^Xc_s=r_s^S.$$ In particular, $r_s^S$ is also a Hodge map into this same pure space $T_s$. This proves the coefficient compatibility at exactly the strength required, using geometric invariant cycles and the canonical Leray term over a curve. Lemma <a href="#comp:uniruled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:uniruled">24</a> now realizes $c_s$ by a cycle in $\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^3(Z_s^S\times Z_s^X)_\mathbb Q$.

We next spread the cycles just obtained. Write $\Delta=\mathbf A^1\setminus O$. The punctures of $I_s$ are the roots of $u^2=c-s$ for $c\in\Delta$, together with infinity in its compactification. Because $s\in G\subset O$, they remain distinct. Locally in $s$ their motion gives a topologically trivial punctured curve with its pulled-back coefficient system. Consequently $H^1(I_s,\mathscr U)$ forms a local system over $G$, and $r^S,r^X$ are morphisms of local systems.

<a id="comp:rcomparison"></a>
The projective family $Z^S_G\times_G Z^X_G$ has relative dimension six. Use the relative Hilbert schemes for polynomials of degree three to parameterize its codimension-three subschemes, and take all finite products together with all rational coefficient lists <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[GrothendieckHilbert](#ref-GrothendieckHilbert), Theorem 3.2]. This is a countable collection of finite-type parameter spaces and includes a representative of every rational codimension-three cycle. Stratify them into connected smooth locally closed varieties. The degree-six fundamental cycle class of a universal flat subscheme is locally constant in the cohomology of the ambient fibers. Indeed, over a smooth stratum the ambient total space is smooth, so the universal structure sheaf has a finite locally free resolution. Flatness over the stratum makes derived restriction to any fiber equal to ordinary restriction. The degree-six part of its Chern character restricts to the fundamental cycle class, with generic multiplicities. This follows from Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch, dévissage, and the compatibility of algebraic and topological Chern classes <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[Fulton](#ref-Fulton), Theorem 15.2, Example 15.2.16(a), and Proposition 19.1.2]. Since these characteristic classes come from a class on the total space, their fiber restrictions are flat. Lemma <a href="#final:hilbert" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:hilbert">34</a> proves the corresponding assertion for the cycle parameter spaces used in specialization. Thus on each stratum the equality $$\label{comp:rcomparison}
 r^X C_*=r^S\quad\hbox{on }R^3\zeta^S_*\mathbb Q$$ holds everywhere or nowhere. Successful strata cover $G$ pointwise, since a cycle has been constructed for every $s\in G$. A nondominating finite-type stratum has finite image in the irreducible curve $G$. If all successful strata were nondominating, their images would be countable, whereas $G(\mathbb C)$ is uncountable.

<a id="comp:relativeC"></a>
Choose a successful dominating stratum. A closed point of its generic fiber gives a generically finite multisection; normalize its closure and shrink $G$ so that it is a finite *étale* cover $\nu:G'\to G$, of degree $d$. The universal rational cycle restricts to a cycle $C'$ over $G'$. Its trace $$\label{comp:relativeC}
 C=\frac1d\nu_*C'
 \in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^3(Z^S_G\times_GZ^X_G)_\mathbb Q$$ satisfies <a href="#comp:rcomparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:rcomparison">[comp:rcomparison]</a>: over every $s$, its action is the average of the $d$ successful actions over $\nu^{-1}(s)$. Generic flatness permits a final shrinking so that the chosen cycle representatives are flat over $G$. From this point onward, fix $G$, both models, and this relative cycle $C$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### *The second substitution and its cylinders.*

Choose $b\in G$, subject to the countable exclusions specified below, and set $s=b+v^2$. Lemma <a href="#comp:models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:models">27</a> supplies smooth projective fourfolds $M^S,M^X$ with the two required opens. Let $$V_0=\{v\in\mathbf A^1:b+v^2\in G\},\qquad
 Y^k=\mathcal Y^k\times_OQ_b\quad(k=S,X),$$ where $\mathcal Y^S=\mathcal S$ and $\mathcal Y^X=\mathcal X$. In the chart $w=1$, put $$Q_b^\circ=\{(u,v)\in\mathbf A^2:v\in V_0,\ b+u^2+v^2\in O\}.$$ The substitutions are $t=s+u^2$ and $s=b+v^2$. The two projections $\pi_t:Q_b\to O$ and $\pi_v:Q_b\to\mathbf P^1_v$ are given by $t$ and $[v:w]$. For each $k=S,X$, the preserved opens form the square $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal Y^k\times_OQ_b^\circ &\hookrightarrow& Z_G^k\times_GV_0\\
 \big\downarrow && \big\downarrow\\
 Y^k=\mathcal Y^k\times_OQ_b &\hookrightarrow& M^k.
 \end{array}$$ All arrows are open immersions, and the upper-left open is the intersection of the two opens in $M^k$. The relative product over $V_0$ of the two upper-right opens carries the pullback of the relative cycle $C$. The lower-left open contains the complete cylinders $F_b^k\times L_\pm$ defined below, and the upper-left open is the coefficient-comparison locus. The choice order is summarized by

<div class="center">

| Fixed before $b$                                        | Depending on $b$                                                |
|:--------------------------------------------------------|:----------------------------------------------------------------|
| Original pencil, $G$, $Z_G^S,Z_G^X$, relative cycle $C$ | $Q_b,V_0,M^k$ and the cycles $\widehat C,D_k$ constructed below |

</div>

Pull <a href="#comp:relativeC" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:relativeC">[comp:relativeC]</a> back to $V_0$ and close its cycle representatives in $M^S\times_{\mathbf P^1_v}M^X$. Viewed in the absolute product, the result is a codimension-four cycle $\widehat C$, hence acts degree-preservingly. Its support is over the diagonal of the $v$-line. Restriction of its action to $V_0$ is consequently the original relative action: restricting the target restricts the source as well, and proper pushforward commutes with this open restriction.

The fiber of $Q_b\to O$ at $b$ is the union of the two lines $$L_+=\{u=iv\},\qquad L_-=\{u=-iv\}.$$ Write $F_b^S=S_b$ and $F_b^X=X_b$ for the original surface fibers. The inclusions $i_{k,\pm}:F_b^k\times L_\pm\hookrightarrow M^k$, together with the projections $p_{k,\pm}:F_b^k\times L_\pm\to F_b^k$, define cylinder correspondences from $F_b^k$ to $M^k$. Let $D_k$ be their difference, with the plus component first. Thus $$D_k\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^3(F_b^k\times M^k)_\mathbb Q,\qquad
 d_k=(i_{k,+})_*p_{k,+}^*-(i_{k,-})_*p_{k,-}^*,$$ and its cohomological action is $$d_k:H^2(F_b^k)(-1)\longrightarrow H^4(M^k).$$ The full lines, including their points at infinity, are contained in the preserved open $Y^k$. Since these cylinders are projective, they are closed in $M^k$.

<a id="comp:minusfour"></a>
In the chart $w=1$, the two lines meet at $(u,v)=(0,0)$ with independent tangent directions. Their intersection multiplicity is therefore one. Their sum is the divisor of the base parameter $t-b$, whose restriction to either compact line has degree zero. Thus, on the smooth surface $Q_b$, $$L_+\cdot L_-=1,\qquad L_+^2=L_-^2=-1.$$ The normal bundles of the cylinder divisors are pulled back from these normal bundles on $Q_b$. The self-intersection and excess-intersection formulas <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[Fulton](#ref-Fulton), Theorem 6.3, Corollary 6.3, and Theorem 19.2] therefore give $$\label{comp:minusfour}
 {}^td_k\,d_k=-4\mathop{\mathrm{id}}
 \quad\hbox{on }H^2(F_b^k)(-1).$$ More explicitly, each self-term is the identity on $H^2(F_b^k)$ multiplied by $\deg N_{L_\pm/Q_b}=-1$, and each cross-term is the identity multiplied by the intersection multiplicity one. The difference convention gives $(-1)+(-1)-1-1=-4$. Every operation on the surface factor is the identity, so the formula holds on all of $H^2(F_b^k)$.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
###### *Coefficient classes of the cylinders.*

The relative comparison acts on coefficient cohomology over the affine conic chart. We must therefore identify the coefficient images of the two cylinder classes. For either complete component $L_\pm\subset Q_b$, its inverse image in $Y^k$ is $F_b^k\times L_\pm$. We shall show that the coefficient projection carries its total-space Gysin class to the Gysin class of the same coefficient on $Q_b$. We verify the compatibility on a general surface base before restricting to the comparison chart.

<a id="comp:thom"></a>
Let $B_2$ be a smooth surface with a map $\tau:B_2\to O$, and let $\sigma:D\hookrightarrow B_2$ be a smooth divisor whose map to $O$ is constant, with value $b$. Write $\mathcal S_{B_2}=\mathcal S\times_OB_2$ and similarly for $\mathcal X_{B_2}$. The inverse image of $D$ is $S_b\times D$, respectively $X_b\times D$. For $x\in U_b$, its pullback to this inverse image has a Gysin class in $H^4(\mathcal S_{B_2})$, with domain understood as $U_b(-1)$. Under the coefficient projection its image is $$\label{comp:thom}
 \sigma_*(x)\in H^2(B_2,\tau^*\mathscr U).$$ The same formula holds for the $e$-part on $\mathcal X_{B_2}$, with coefficient $\varphi_b(x)$.

To verify the formula, make the coefficient Thom construction explicit. The complex orientation of the normal line of $\sigma$ gives a morphism $$u_\sigma:\sigma_*\mathbb Q_D[-2]\longrightarrow\mathbb Q_{B_2}.$$ On a normal disc, the relative cochain complex of the disc and its punctured disc has one cohomology group, generated in degree two by this orientation. Tensoring this local calculation with a bounded locally constant rational complex $K$ gives the natural purity identification $\sigma^!K\simeq\sigma^*K[-2]$. More precisely, the globally defined map $$\mu_K:\sigma^*K\otimes\sigma^!\mathbb Q_{B_2}\longrightarrow\sigma^!K$$ is adjoint to the projection-formula/counit composite $$\sigma_*(\sigma^*K\otimes\sigma^!\mathbb Q_{B_2})
 \simeq K\otimes\sigma_*\sigma^!\mathbb Q_{B_2}\longrightarrow K.$$ The normal-disc calculation shows that $\mu_K$ is an isomorphism for such $K$, and the complex orientation identifies $\sigma^!\mathbb Q_{B_2}\simeq\mathbb Q_D[-2]$. This gives the stated natural purity isomorphism globally. Equivalently, the coefficient Gysin morphism is $$\sigma_*\sigma^*K[-2]
   \simeq (\sigma_*\mathbb Q_D[-2])\otimes K
   \xrightarrow{\ u_\sigma\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\ }K.$$ The first isomorphism is the projection formula, and this construction is natural in $K$. In the rational sheaf category the displayed shifts are only $[-2]$; the Tate $(-1)$ records the Hodge degree of the complex normal orientation on cohomology.

For completeness, let $h:Y\to B_2$ be either smooth proper surface family, let $Y_D=Y\times_{B_2}D$, and write $j:Y_D\hookrightarrow Y$ and $h_D:Y_D\to D$. The normal line of $j$ is $h_D^*N_{D/B_2}$ with its pulled complex orientation. The normal-disc construction therefore identifies the total-space Thom morphism with the pullback of $u_\sigma$. Under the natural proper base-change identification $$Rh_*j_*\mathbb Q_{Y_D}[-2]
   \simeq\sigma_*Rh_{D*}\mathbb Q_{Y_D}[-2]
   \simeq\sigma_*\sigma^*Rh_*\mathbb Q_Y[-2],$$ the projection formula consequently identifies $Rh_*(u_j)$ with the coefficient Gysin morphism for $K=Rh_*\mathbb Q_Y$. The formal base-change and projection-formula statements used here are natural for these bounded rational complexes <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[SchnuererSoergel](#ref-SchnuererSoergel), Theorems 5.10 and 6.3].

Finally $D\to O$ factors through $b$. Proper base change identifies the restricted splitting with the canonical splitting of $R\Gamma(S_b,\mathbb Q)$, respectively $eR\Gamma(X_b,\mathbb Q)$, pulled back from a point. The class pulled back from $x$ is therefore its degree-two product coefficient. Naturality of the coefficient Gysin map makes it commute with the splitting pulled from <a href="#comp:k3split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:k3split">[comp:k3split]</a>, its degree-two projection, and the idempotent <a href="#comp:xsplit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:xsplit">[comp:xsplit]</a>. This proves <a href="#comp:thom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:thom">[comp:thom]</a>. The construction applies to each divisor separately, including when two such divisors intersect.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
###### *Comparison on the affine conic chart.*

For $x\in U_b$, put $a=\widehat C_*d_S(x)$. Our immediate aim is to prove $e\,a=d_X(\varphi_bx)$ on $\mathcal X\times_OQ_b^\circ$, using only the degree-three action of $C$. On $Q_b^\circ$, write $q:Q_b^\circ\to V_0$ for projection to $v$ and $\tau(u,v)=b+u^2+v^2$. Put $c=d_S(x)|_{Z^S_G\times_GV_0}$. The restriction of $c$ to every threefold fiber over $v$ is zero. Each cylinder meets that fiber transversally: the map $L_\pm\to\mathbf P^1_v$ is an isomorphism. Its restriction is therefore the ordinary surface Gysin map at $u=\pm iv$, with no excess class, including at $v=0$. Pair either surface Gysin image of $x$ with an arbitrary class in the second cohomology of the threefold. This testing class restricts to a monodromy-invariant class on surface fibers over $I_{b+v^2}$. Its orthogonal projection to $\mathscr U$ is zero by <a href="#comp:noinvariantsI" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:noinvariantsI">[comp:noinvariantsI]</a>, so the pairing is zero. Poincaré duality on the projective threefold proves the claimed vanishing of each term. At $v=0$ the two surface inclusions also coincide.

<a id="comp:lerayone"></a>
<a id="comp:derivedcomparison"></a>
Write $\rho_k:Z^k_G\times_GV_0\to V_0$ and $A_k=R\rho_{k*}\mathbb Q$. Lemma <a href="#comp:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:affine">25</a>(ii) places $c$ in the canonical Leray subspace $$\label{comp:lerayone}
 H^1(V_0,\mathcal H^3A_S)
 \subset\mathbb H^4(V_0,A_S).$$ Set $K=Rq_*\tau^*\mathscr U[-2]$. Its cohomology sheaves are local systems, by the same noncollision of punctures used over $G$, now pulled back to $V_0$. Restriction to the open surface family over $Q_b^\circ$, followed by the coefficient projections <a href="#comp:k3split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:k3split">[comp:k3split]</a>–<a href="#comp:xsplit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:xsplit">[comp:xsplit]</a>, gives morphisms of rational complexes $$\beta_S:A_S\longrightarrow K,
 \qquad \beta_X:A_X\longrightarrow K.$$ The relative correspondence gives $\gamma:A_S\to A_X$: on the relative product, take cup product with its class of degree six and integrate along the proper relative threefold projection. Its action on $R^3$ is the action prescribed by $C$. Equation <a href="#comp:rcomparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:rcomparison">[comp:rcomparison]</a> says exactly that $$\mathcal H^3(\beta_X\gamma)=\mathcal H^3(\beta_S).$$ Functoriality in Lemma <a href="#comp:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:affine">25</a>(ii), applied to the subspace <a href="#comp:lerayone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:lerayone">[comp:lerayone]</a>, now yields $$\label{comp:derivedcomparison}
 (\beta_X\gamma)_*c=(\beta_S)_*c
 \quad\hbox{in }\mathbb H^4(V_0,K)=H^2(Q_b^\circ,\tau^*\mathscr U).$$ The reason degree-three agreement suffices is precisely the membership <a href="#comp:lerayone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:lerayone">[comp:lerayone]</a>: the left term of the functorial exact sequence in Lemma <a href="#comp:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:affine">25</a>(ii) only sees $\mathcal H^3$. No equality on the $H^0(\mathcal H^4)$ quotient is used. The $u$-fibers and the $v$-base have cohomological dimension one, so the last group is also canonically $$H^1\bigl(V_0,R^1q_*\tau^*\mathscr U\bigr).$$

<a id="comp:smallopen"></a>
The restrictions of the cylinders to $Q_b^\circ$ are the two smooth section divisors $\sigma_\pm(v)=(\pm iv,v)$. Formula <a href="#comp:thom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:thom">[comp:thom]</a> shows that the right side of <a href="#comp:derivedcomparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:derivedcomparison">[comp:derivedcomparison]</a> is $$\sigma_{+*}(x)-\sigma_{-*}(x).$$ The same formula on the $X$ side identifies this with the coefficient class of $d_X(\varphi_bx)$. Although the sections meet at $v=0$, purity has been applied to each regular embedding separately. By <a href="#comp:xsplit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:xsplit">[comp:xsplit]</a>, the coefficient projection identifies the entire $e$-part of the fourth cohomology over $Q_b^\circ$ with $H^2(Q_b^\circ,\tau^*\mathscr U)$. Hence we have proved $$\label{comp:smallopen}
 e\,a=d_X(\varphi_bx)
 \quad\hbox{on }\mathcal X\times_OQ_b^\circ.$$

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
###### *Excluding the boundary error.*

We must prove <a href="#comp:smallopen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:smallopen">[comp:smallopen]</a> on all of $Y^X$, since the complete cylinders also contain their points at infinity. Let $F=\mathbf A^1\setminus G$, a fixed finite set. The complement of $\mathcal X\times_OQ_b^\circ$ in $Y^X$ is a disjoint union of smooth divisors:

1.  the two infinity sections $[u:v:w]=[\pm i:1:0]$, each carrying the fixed family $\mathcal X\to O$;

2.  for each $c_0\in F$ and each of the two roots $a_0$ of $a_0^2=c_0-b$, the divisor $v=a_0$, carrying the fixed open threefold $$E_{c_0}^X
       =\{(x,u):g(x)=c_0+u^2\in O\}.$$

The second description follows by substituting $v=a_0$ in the affine conic equation. The projective equation of a finite divisor is $v=a_0w$; a point with $w=0$ on it would also have $v=u=0$, which is impossible. Thus it has no point on either infinity section. Different finite values of $v$ give disjoint divisors, and all the roots listed above are distinct since $b\in G$. Consequently the entire boundary is a disjoint union, with the fixed isomorphism types just listed. Their smoothness follows from smoothness of $g$ over $O$, including when $c_0$ is not a good value for a compactified threefold.

<a id="comp:localization"></a>
Denote their union by $D$. Topological localization and the complex-oriented Thom isomorphism for each smooth closed divisor give the underlying exact sequence $$\label{comp:localization}
 H^2(D)(-1)\longrightarrow H^4(Y^X)
 \longrightarrow H^4(\mathcal X\times_OQ_b^\circ).$$ This is exact in mixed Hodge structures as well. The closed immersion $D\hookrightarrow Y^X$ is proper even though both varieties may be nonproper. For smooth separated complex algebraic varieties, the adjunction and duality constructions of restriction and proper Gysin lift to mixed Hodge modules; the smooth duality normalization gives exactly the codimension-one shift $[-2](-1)$ <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[SaitoMHMIntro](#ref-SaitoMHMIntro), Sections 1.2–1.4]. Their cohomology mixed Hodge structures agree with Deligne’s . Thus the two arrows in <a href="#comp:localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:localization">[comp:localization]</a> are morphisms for the canonical mixed Hodge structures. Their underlying sequence is exact by the Thom calculation, hence it is exact in that abelian category. The first term’s weight-four part is isomorphic, independently of $b$, to $H_F(-1)$, where the fixed polarizable pure Hodge structure $H_F$ is $$H_F=
 \bigl(W_2H^2(\mathcal X)\bigr)^{\oplus2}
 \oplus
 \bigoplus_{c_0\in F}
 \bigl(W_2H^2(E_{c_0}^X)\bigr)^{\oplus2}.$$ Here these spaces are polarizable because lowest-weight cohomology of a smooth variety is an image of the cohomology of a smooth projective compactification.

<a id="comp:fixedtypes"></a>
For all but countably many $b\in G$, $$\label{comp:fixedtypes}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{HS}}(U_b,H_F)=0.$$ To check this precisely, exclude the countably many nonsimple fibers and trivialize $\mathscr U$ on a countable cover by simply connected discs. A nonzero map from a remaining $U_b$ is injective. There are countably many rational linear injections into the fixed rational vector space $H_F$. For each such injection, the condition of being a Hodge map determines the entire Hodge filtration on $U_b$ uniquely as the inverse image of the filtration on $H_F$. The nonconstant period map meets this one fixed filtration in a discrete set on a disc. Taking the indicated countable unions proves <a href="#comp:fixedtypes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixedtypes">[comp:fixedtypes]</a>.

<a id="comp:largeopen"></a>
Consider the error map $$U_b(-1)\longrightarrow H^4(Y^X),
 \qquad x\longmapsto e\,a-d_X(\varphi_bx).$$ Both terms are morphisms of Hodge structures: the cylinder and $\widehat C$ act algebraically on the projective models, restriction is a mixed-Hodge morphism, $e$ is algebraic on $Y^X$, and $\varphi_b$ is Hodge by hypothesis. Equation <a href="#comp:smallopen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:smallopen">[comp:smallopen]</a> places the error in the kernel of the last arrow of <a href="#comp:localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:localization">[comp:localization]</a>. Its source is pure of weight four. Strictness for the weight filtration <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[DeligneHodgeII](#ref-DeligneHodgeII), Theorem 2.3.5(iii)] therefore puts its image in the Gysin image of $W_4(H^2(D)(-1))\simeq H_F(-1)$. This image is a quotient of a polarizable pure Hodge structure. Semisimplicity splits that quotient, lifting the error map to $U_b(-1)\to H_F(-1)$; by <a href="#comp:fixedtypes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixedtypes">[comp:fixedtypes]</a> the lift is zero. We have proved $$\label{comp:largeopen}
 e\,\widehat C_*d_S(x)=d_X(\varphi_bx)
 \quad\hbox{in }H^4(Y^X),\qquad x\in U_b.$$

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
###### *Returning to the surfaces.*

The transpose cylinder can be evaluated on a class of $Y^X$ by restriction to $X_b\times L_\pm$ and integration along $L_\pm$. These are complete projective cylinders inside $Y^X$, so this operation agrees with its action on classes restricted from $M^X$. Moreover, $e_b\,{}^td_X={}^td_X e$, since $j,\delta$ act only on the surface coordinate. Define the algebraic correspondence <a id="comp:theta"></a>$$\label{comp:theta}
 \Theta_b=-\tfrac14\,e_b\circ{}^tD_X\circ\widehat C\circ D_S
 \in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S_b\times X_b)_\mathbb Q.$$ Applying the transpose to <a href="#comp:largeopen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:largeopen">[comp:largeopen]</a> and using <a href="#comp:minusfour" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:minusfour">[comp:minusfour]</a> gives, for every $x\in U_b$, $$(\Theta_b)_*x
 =-\frac14\,{}^td_X e\,\widehat C_*d_S(x)
 =-\frac14\,{}^td_Xd_X(\varphi_bx)
 =\varphi_bx.$$ In this calculation $e$ acts after restriction to $Y^X$. Thus the action is exactly the specified map on the whole of $U_b$. The codimension is $3+4+3-4-4=2$; composing with $e_b\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(X_b\times X_b)_\mathbb Q$ preserves it. Every exceptional choice of $b$ used after fixing $G$ belongs to the countable set excluded in <a href="#comp:fixedtypes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixedtypes">[comp:fixedtypes]</a>, together with the nonsimple fibers. This proves the theorem with the asserted order of choices. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Application to the abelian-cover pencil

<a id="stmt-comp-surface"></a>
<div id="comp:surface" class="corollary">

**Corollary 28**. *Fix abelian-cover data and a section pencil for which [geom:structure](#geom:structure), [geom:period](#geom:period), [pencil:realization](#pencil:realization) apply, with nonconstant $U$-period along the pencil and simple $U_t$ outside a countable set. On a nonempty open of that pencil, for all but countably many $b$, there is an algebraic correspondence $$\Theta_b\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S_b^0\times X_b)_\mathbb Q$$ whose action on $U_b$ is the given identification with $H^2(X_b)^{j=+,\,\delta=-}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-surface"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-surface</summary>

*Proof.* The variation, the projector, and the identification are supplied by [geom:structure](#geom:structure), [pencil:realization](#pencil:realization). [pencil:realization](#pencil:realization) gives the uniruled threefold models on the K3 side. For $X$, after $t=s+u^2$ the generic fiber over $B'$ is the conic $$z^2-\pi^*d_1\,u^2=\pi^*(d_0+s d_1)$$ over $\mathbb C(B')$. A finite extension splits this conic, so a generically finite surface cover of $B'$ yields a dominant rational map from a product of a surface and $\mathbf P^1$. Every smooth projective model of the threefold is therefore uniruled. Theorem <a href="#comp:twosquares" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:twosquares">23</a> applies. It applies separately to every fixed pencil with these properties; its countable set of excluded $b$ is chosen only after the pencil, the degree-three correspondence, and the good $s$-open have been fixed. ◻

</details>

<a id="final:section"></a>
### Recovery of the prescribed Kuga–Satake tensor

The comparison of the preceding section and the domination by curves give an algebraic injection from the auxiliary K3 cohomology into a tensor product of two curve cohomologies. This does not yet realize the standard tensor required by Theorem <a href="#ext:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:criterion">1</a>. We bridge the difference by proving that its compositions with rational Hodge maps on the two curve factors, together with their interchanges, span every Hodge map into the tensor square of the full even Clifford module. This permits us to select the prescribed right factor and polarization. The calculation applies at the construction periods selected in Lemma <a href="#final:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:curves">29</a>, where the Hodge group is the full special orthogonal group. At the end of the section we transfer the curve source by a rational Hodge isometry; Section <a href="#specialization:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="specialization:section">8</a> will use that transfer to obtain seeds with $\mathop{\mathrm{NS}}=P$ and extend one fixed tensor to every ample $P$-polarized target.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### An algebraic embedding into a tensor of curve cohomologies

<a id="stmt-final-curves"></a>
<div id="final:curves" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29**. *Choose the abelian data and the pencil as in Lemma <a href="#geom:genericity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:genericity">16</a>, and then choose $b$ in the common good open outside the countable exceptional sets of that lemma and Corollary <a href="#comp:surface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:surface">28</a>. Then there are smooth projective curves $C_1,C_2$ and a rational algebraic correspondence $$Z\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S_b^0\times C_1\times C_2)_{\mathbb Q}$$ whose action restricts to an injection $$f:U_b\longrightarrow H^1(C_1,\mathbb Q)\otimes H^1(C_2,\mathbb Q).$$ Here $U_b=T_{S_b^0}$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(U_b)=\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U_b)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-curves"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-curves</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\Theta_b\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(S_b^0\times X_b)_\mathbb Q$ be the correspondence of Corollary <a href="#comp:surface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:surface">28</a>; its restriction to $U_b$ is the specified identification $\varphi_b:U_b\to U_{X_b}$. At these periods $U_{X_b}$ has no nonzero rational $(1,1)$-class by Proposition <a href="#geom:period" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:period">11</a>. Proposition <a href="#geom:domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:domination">15</a> therefore supplies curves $C_1,C_2$, a morphism $g:C_1\times C_2\to X_b$, and the algebraic correspondence $$Z_X=\bigl(\pi_1^{C_1}\boxtimes\pi_1^{C_2}\bigr)
       \circ{}^t\Gamma_g\circ e
 \in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(X_b\times C_1\times C_2)_\mathbb Q,$$ where the curve projectors are those constructed there. Its action on $U_{X_b}$ is the injection $g^*$, with image entirely in $H^1(C_1,\mathbb Q)\otimes H^1(C_2,\mathbb Q)$. Set $$Z=Z_X\circ\Theta_b.$$ Its action on $U_b$ is $f=g^*\varphi_b$ and is injective. The composition passes through the surface $X_b$, so its codimension is $2+2-2=2$. The equality $U_b=T_{S_b^0}$ and the full Hodge group follow from Propositions <a href="#geom:period" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:period">11</a> and <a href="#pencil:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:realization">17</a> at these selected periods. ◻

</details>

The comparison just constructed fits into $$(U_b=T_{S_b^0},q_Y)
 \xrightarrow{\ \varphi_b=(\Theta_b)_*\ }
 (U_{X_b},q_X)
 \xrightarrow{\ g^*\ }
 H^1(C_1,\mathbb Q)\otimes H^1(C_2,\mathbb Q).$$ Here $q_X$ is the cup-product form on $X_b$, and $q_Y$ is the form inherited from $Y$ in Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">9</a>; the latter is exactly the K3 cup-product form by Proposition <a href="#pencil:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:realization">17</a>. The resolved pullback satisfies $\varphi_b^*q_X=2q_Y$. Thus the first map is an algebraic Hodge identification with a factor-two change of form, and the second is the algebraic injection supplied by the curves. The rational isometry used below concerns $q_Y$, not the rescaled form from $X_b$.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### The Clifford map and its tensor form

We recall the tensor of Section <a href="#intro:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:section">1</a> for an eight-dimensional quadratic space $(U,q)$. With the Clifford convention $v^2=q(v,v)$, the full even module is $$W=C^+(U,q),\qquad \dim_\mathbb QW=2^7=128.$$ If $e_1,e_2$ form an oriented orthonormal basis of the positive period plane, with $U^{2,0}=\mathbb C(e_1+i e_2)$, put $J=e_1e_2$. Then $J^2=-1$, and the Kuga–Satake Hodge circle acts on $W_\mathbb R$ by left multiplication by $\exp(\theta J)$. Conjugation by the same element acts on $U^{2,0}$ by $\exp(2i\theta)$, as required by the weight-two Hodge structure.

<a id="final:specified-clifford"></a>
Fix the prescribed rational vector $w\in U$ with $q(w,w)\ne0$. The standard embedding on the even module is $$\label{final:specified-clifford}
 j_w:U(1)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W),\qquad
 j_w(v)(a)=vaw\quad(a\in C^+(U,q)).$$ The output is even, and $w^{-1}=w/q(w,w)$ shows, by evaluation at $a=1$, that $j_w$ is injective. For $g\in\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}(U,q)$ and left multiplication $L_g$ one has $$L_gj_w(v)L_g^{-1}(a)=(gvg^{-1})aw=j_w(gvg^{-1})(a).$$ Thus $j_w$ is a rational Hodge map for every period; the right factor $w$ is fixed and need not have any particular Hodge type.

<a id="final:specified-tensor"></a>
Let $E$ be the prescribed rational polarization of $W=H^1(A,\mathbb Q)$. With $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W)=W\otimes W^\vee$, its identification $I_E=(E^\flat(1))^{-1}:W^\vee\xrightarrow{\sim}W(1)$ converts $j_w$ into $$\label{final:specified-tensor}
 \iota_{w,E}:U\longrightarrow W\otimes W
                  \subset H^2(A\times A,\mathbb Q).$$ Explicitly, $(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\otimes I_E)j_w$ maps $U(1)$ into $(W\otimes W)(1)$, and twisting by $\mathbb Q(-1)$ gives Equation <a href="#final:specified-tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:specified-tensor">[final:specified-tensor]</a>. This is the specified untwisted tensor. Its recovery will use the full rational space of Hodge maps to $W\otimes W$.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### A rational spanning lemma for the two half-spin factors

The Clifford tensor and the even-module half-spin decomposition are classical; see <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[VanGeemenKS](#ref-VanGeemenKS), Proposition 6.3 and Example 6.6]. The spanning argument below also uses rational Hodge-Hom descent and both tensor orientations. The following statement is about effective weight-one Hodge structures: their only types are $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$.

<a id="stmt-final-spin"></a>
<div id="final:spin" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30**. *Let $(U,q)$ be an eight-dimensional polarized rational Hodge structure of K3 type, of signature $(2,6)$, and suppose $\mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(U)=\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U,q)$. Let $W_1,W_2$ be polarized effective weight-one Hodge structures and let $W=C^+(U,q)$ have its Kuga–Satake Hodge structure. For every nonzero Hodge map $$f:U\longrightarrow W_1\otimes W_2,$$ put $$\mathcal H=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{HS}}(W_1,W)\otimes_{\mathbb Q}
       \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{HS}}(W_2,W).$$ If $\tau:W\otimes W\to W\otimes W$ is the ordinary tensor flip, the rational linear map $$\Phi:\mathcal H\oplus\mathcal H\longrightarrow
       \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{HS}}(U,W\otimes W),$$ defined by $$\begin{cases}
 \Phi(a\otimes b,0)=(a\otimes b)\circ f,\\
 \Phi(0,a\otimes b)=\tau\circ(a\otimes b)\circ f
 \end{cases}$$ is surjective.*

*If $U\subset H^2(M,\mathbb Q)$ for a smooth projective surface $M$, $W_i=H^1(C_i,\mathbb Q)$, $W=H^1(A,\mathbb Q)$, and $f$ is the restriction of a codimension-two algebraic correspondence from $M$ to $C_1\times C_2$, then every map in the target of $\Phi$ is induced by a cycle in $\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^2(M\times A\times A)_\mathbb Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-spin"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-spin</summary>

*Proof.* We prove surjectivity of the rational map $\Phi$ by spanning both complex half-spin tensor orientations. We first isolate the rational orthogonal Lie algebra in the common Hodge group. Weight-one effectivity will restrict the nontrivial constituents of $W_1,W_2$ to half-spins and identify their rational intertwiners with Hodge maps to $W$. This allows the tensor-product calculation to be made over $\mathbb C$ and its surjectivity to be descended to $\mathbb Q$.

*The rational Hodge group.* Let $$G=\mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(W_1\oplus W_2\oplus W).$$ The Hodge group is the connected rational algebraic group generated by the Hodge circle. Any rational invariant subspace of the polarized pure weight-one structure $W_1\oplus W_2\oplus W$ has a Hodge complement, obtained from its polarization. The complement is again $G$-invariant. Its faithful representation is therefore completely reducible, so $G$ is reductive.

<a id="final:lie-splitting"></a>
The embedding in Equation <a href="#final:specified-clifford" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:specified-clifford">[final:specified-clifford]</a> realizes $U(1)$ in $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(W)$; Tate twists do not change the Hodge-circle action. This gives a representation of $G$ on $U$ preserving $q$. Since $G$ is connected, its image lies in $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U,q)$. It contains the Hodge circle of $U$ and therefore $\mathop{\mathrm{Hg}}(U)=\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U,q)$, so the map is surjective. Write $\mathfrak g=\operatorname{Lie}(G)$. The derived Lie algebra of $\mathfrak g$ is a direct sum of rational simple ideals. Since $\mathfrak{so}(U,q)$ is absolutely simple, exactly one of those ideals maps nontrivially to it: two such images would be commuting copies of the same nonabelian simple algebra. The nonzero map is an isomorphism, and the remaining ideals and the center form its kernel. We obtain rational Lie ideals $$\label{final:lie-splitting}
 \mathfrak g=\mathfrak s\oplus\mathfrak k,
 \qquad
 \mathfrak s\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{so}(U,q),
 \qquad
 \mathfrak k=\ker\bigl(\mathfrak g\to\mathfrak{so}(U,q)\bigr).$$ The ideal $\mathfrak s$ integrates to a connected rational normal semisimple subgroup. Finite central kernels are harmless here; we do not identify a spin representation with a representation of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U,q)$.

*The allowed weight-one constituents.* Normalize the complexified infinitesimal Hodge-circle generator $D$ so that its eigenvalues on weight one are $1,-1$. Its image on $U$ has eigenvalues $2,0,\ldots,0,-2$. Write $D=D_{\mathfrak s}+D_{\mathfrak k}$ according to Equation <a href="#final:lie-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:lie-splitting">[final:lie-splitting]</a>. Each $\mathfrak s_\mathbb C$-isotypic summand of $W_{i,\mathbb C}$ has the form $R_\lambda\otimes M$, with $R_\lambda$ irreducible. The commuting operator $D_{\mathfrak k}$ acts only on $M$. The two projections of the semisimple element $D$ are semisimple, so their eigenvalues add on simultaneous eigenspaces.

Identify the root system of $\mathfrak s_{\mathbb C}$ with $D_4$, with roots $\pm e_i\pm e_j$, using a Cartan subalgebra in which $D_{\mathfrak s}$ pairs with weights as $2e_1$. A dominant highest weight has $$\lambda_1\geq\lambda_2\geq\lambda_3\geq|\lambda_4|,$$ with coordinates either all integral or all half-integral. Its Weyl orbit contains weights giving the eigenvalues $2\lambda_1$ and $-2\lambda_1$ for $D_{\mathfrak s}$. Translation by any eigenvalue of the commuting factor cannot reduce this spread. Effectivity of weight one therefore gives $4\lambda_1\leq2$. The only possibilities are the trivial representation and the two half-spin representations $S_+,S_-$, with highest weights $\tfrac12(e_1+e_2+e_3+e_4)$ and $\tfrac12(e_1+e_2+e_3-e_4)$. Their weights are all sign choices $\tfrac12(\pm e_1\pm e_2\pm e_3\pm e_4)$ of the respective parities. Each half-spin therefore has both $D_{\mathfrak s}$-eigenvalues $1,-1$. If $\mu$ is an eigenvalue of $D_{\mathfrak k}$ on its multiplicity space, both $\mu+1$ and $\mu-1$ must belong to $\{1,-1\}$; hence $\mu=0$. Semisimplicity now shows that $D_{\mathfrak k}$ is zero on that multiplicity space.

The rational decompositions $$W_i=W_i^{\mathfrak s}\oplus W_i^{\mathrm{nt}},
 \qquad W_i^{\mathrm{nt}}=\mathfrak s W_i$$ are $G$-stable. On $W_i^{\mathrm{nt}}$ the entire Hodge-circle generator consequently acts through $\mathfrak s$. The same assertion holds on $W$. Its Hodge group lies in the rational spin group acting by left multiplication on $C^+(U,q)$ and maps onto $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(U,q)$. The spin covering has finite kernel, so the Lie algebra of this image on $W$ maps isomorphically to $\mathfrak{so}(U,q)$; in particular $\mathfrak k$ acts trivially on $W$.

For completeness, write $U_\mathbb C=F\oplus F^\vee$ with $F$ maximal isotropic of dimension four. Exterior multiplication and contraction give the Clifford action on $\bigwedge^\bullet F$. These operators generate its matrix units, and the even algebra preserves parity. Thus, with $S_+=\bigwedge^{\mathrm{even}}F$ and $S_-=\bigwedge^{\mathrm{odd}}F$, one has $$C^+(U)_{\mathbb C}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(S_+)\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(S_-).$$ Since $\dim S_+=\dim S_-=8$, left multiplication gives eight copies of each half-spin. In particular $W$ has no $\mathfrak s$-invariant summand.

<a id="final:hom-equality"></a>
*Rational Hodge maps and complex multiplicities.* A rational $\mathfrak s$-intertwiner from $W_i^{\mathrm{nt}}$ to $W$ commutes with $D$ and hence with the connected Hodge circle. Extended by zero on $W_i^{\mathfrak s}$, it is therefore a Hodge map. Conversely every rational Hodge map commutes with $G$ and hence with $\mathfrak s$. We have proved $$\label{final:hom-equality}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{HS}}(W_i,W)
   &=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak s}(W_i^{\mathrm{nt}},W),\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{HS}}(U,W\otimes W)
   &=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak s}(U,W\otimes W).
 \end{aligned}$$ In the second equality, both Hodge structures have weight two and their Hodge-circle actions factor through $\mathfrak s$, so the same commutation argument applies. For any two rational $\mathfrak s$-modules $V_1,V_2$ the intertwiner space is the kernel of the rational linear map $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\mathbb Q(V_1,V_2)\longrightarrow
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\mathbb Q(\mathfrak s\otimes V_1,V_2),\qquad
 a\longmapsto\bigl((x,v)\mapsto a(xv)-x\,a(v)\bigr).$$ Extension of scalars is exact. Hence $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak s}(V_1,V_2)\otimes_\mathbb Q\mathbb C
=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak s_\mathbb C}(V_{1,\mathbb C},V_{2,\mathbb C})$. In particular, after complexifying the rational Hodge Hom spaces in Equation <a href="#final:hom-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:hom-equality">[final:hom-equality]</a>, all complex maps between the half-spin multiplicity spaces are available. This assertion does not require either half-spin type to be defined separately over $\mathbb Q$.

*Spanning both tensor orientations.* Write $$\begin{aligned}
 W_{i,\mathbb C}^{\mathrm{nt}}
   &=(S_+\otimes M_{i,+})\oplus(S_-\otimes M_{i,-}),\\
 W_{\mathbb C}&=(S_+\otimes N_+)\oplus(S_-\otimes N_-),
       \qquad \dim N_+=\dim N_-=8.
 \end{aligned}$$ Both half-spins are self-dual: the Weyl group contains simultaneous negation of all four coordinates, preserving the two weight parities. Clifford antisymmetrization identifies $C(U)_\mathbb C$ with $\bigwedge^\bullet U_\mathbb C$ as a module for conjugation. The even part consists of the diagonal endomorphism blocks of $S_+\oplus S_-$, and the odd part of the two off-diagonal blocks. Choose a normalized volume element $\omega$ with $\omega^2=1$. Left multiplication by $\omega$ exchanges exterior degrees $k$ and $8-k$ and distinguishes the two blocks of each parity. Each block therefore contains one copy of each paired degree; degree four splits into its two $35$-dimensional eigenspaces. This gives <a id="final:spin-products"></a>$$\label{final:spin-products}
 \begin{aligned}
 S_+\otimes S_-&\simeq U_{\mathbb C}\oplus\bigwedge^3U_{\mathbb C},\\
 S_\pm\otimes S_\pm&\simeq
